Show that for every \(x\in X\) there is an open neighbourhood \(U\ni x\) with \(\pi^{-1}(U)\simeq\mathbb A^1_U\). Deduce that \(Z\) is Noetherian or separated and quasi-compact.

(b) Let \(\mathcal F\) be a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module and \(\mathcal G=\pi^*\mathcal F\). Show that \(\mathcal G(\pi^{-1}(X_s))=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}(\mathcal L^n\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal F)(X_s)\) (use Exercise 1.1(c)).

(c) Regard \(s\) as an element of \(\mathcal O_Z(Z)\). Show that \(\mathcal G(Z_s)=\mathcal G(Z)_s\) and that \(Z_s\subset\pi^{-1}(X_s)\).

(d) Give a new proof of Lemma 1.25.

Solution

Throughout, \(X_s=\{x:\mathcal L_x=s_x\mathcal O_{X,x}\}\) and, for \(h\in\mathcal O(Y)\), \(Y_h=\{y:h_y\in\mathcal O_{Y,y}^*\}\). Fix a finite affine open cover \(X=X_1\cup\dots\cup X_r\) with \(\mathcal L|_{X_i}=e_i\mathcal O_{X_i}\) free (possible since \(X\) is quasi-compact and \(\mathcal L\) is invertible), and write \(s|_{X_i}=h_ie_i\) with \(h_i\in\mathcal O_X(X_i)\). Then \(X_s\cap X_i=D(h_i)\), which is affine; so \(X_s\) is a finite union of affine opens, hence quasi-compact.

(a) Algebra structure. The canonical isomorphisms \(\mathcal L^n\otimes\mathcal L^m\simeq\mathcal L^{n+m}\) (associativity of \(\otimes\), and \(\mathcal O_X\otimes\mathcal M\simeq\mathcal M\) when \(n\) or \(m\) is \(0\)) define a multiplication \(\mathcal A\otimes\mathcal A\to\mathcal A\) with \(\mathcal L^n\cdot\mathcal L^m\subseteq\mathcal L^{n+m}\), associative with unit \(1\in\mathcal L^{\otimes 0}=\mathcal O_X\). It is commutative: on \(X_i\), \(\mathcal L^n|_{X_i}=\mathcal O e_i^{n}\) where \(e_i^n=e_i\otimes\dots\otimes e_i\), and both \(e_i^n\otimes e_i^m\) and \(e_i^m\otimes e_i^n\) map to \(e_i^{n+m}\). In particular, on \(X_i\),

\[ \mathcal A|_{X_i}=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}\mathcal O_{X_i}e_i^n\simeq\mathcal O_{X_i}[T],\qquad e_i\leftrightarrow T, \]

the sheaf of polynomial algebras. Each \(\mathcal L^n\) is invertible, hence quasi-coherent, so \(\mathcal A\) is quasi-coherent by Prop. 1.12(a). Thus \(Z=\Spec\mathcal A\) exists by Ex. 1.17(a).

Local structure. Let \(x\in X\); take an affine open \(U\ni x\) on which \(\mathcal L=e\mathcal O_U\) is free. Sections of a direct sum of sheaves over a quasi-compact open set are the direct sum of the sections (a section of the sheaf \(\bigoplus\mathcal M_n\) is a family \((t_n)\) that locally has only finitely many non-zero components; on a quasi-compact open set finitely many neighbourhoods suffice, so only finitely many \(t_n\ne0\)). Hence \(\mathcal A(U)=\mathcal O_X(U)[T]\) and \(\pi^{-1}(U)=\Spec\mathcal A(U)=\Spec\mathcal O_X(U)[T]=\mathbb A^1_U\) (Ex. 1.17(a)).

Noetherian or separated quasi-compact. If \(X\) is Noetherian, cover it by finitely many affine opens \(U_i\) as above, each \(\mathcal O_X(U_i)\) Noetherian. Then \(Z\) is covered by the finitely many affine schemes \(\Spec\mathcal O_X(U_i)[T]\), which are Noetherian by Hilbert's basis theorem; so \(Z\) is Noetherian. If \(X\) is separated and quasi-compact: \(\pi\) is affine, hence quasi-compact (by the same finite affine cover \(\{V_j\}\) of \(X\): \(\pi^{-1}(V_j)\) is affine) and separated (as in Ex. 1.17(c)), so \(Z\to X\to\Spec\mathbb Z\) is separated, and \(Z\) is quasi-compact because it is a finite union of the affine opens \(\pi^{-1}(V_j)\).

(b) The projection formula for an affine morphism (Exercise 1.1(c)). Let \(g:Z\to X\) be affine and \(\mathcal F\) quasi-coherent on \(X\). The map \(\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}g_*\mathcal O_Z\to g_*g^*\mathcal F\) is defined on the presheaf \(V\mapsto\mathcal F(V)\otimes_{\mathcal O_X(V)}\mathcal O_Z(g^{-1}V)\) by \(t\otimes b\mapsto b\cdot g^*t\) (\(g^*t=t\otimes1\)) and then sheafified; it is \(g_*\mathcal O_Z\)-linear. It is an isomorphism: this is local on \(X\), so let \(X=\Spec R\), \(Z=\Spec C\), \(\mathcal F=\widetilde N\). Then \(g^*\mathcal F=(N\otimes_RC)^\sim\) (Prop. 1.14(b)) and \(g_*((N\otimes_RC)^\sim)=(N\otimes_RC)^\sim\) viewed over \(R\) (on \(D(r)\) both sides have sections \((N\otimes_RC)_r\)); also \(\widetilde N\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\widetilde C=(N\otimes_RC)^\sim\) by Prop. 1.5(d), and the map is the identity of this sheaf.

Apply this to \(g=\pi\): \(\pi_*\mathcal G\simeq\mathcal F\otimes\pi_*\mathcal O_Z=\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal A=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)\) (\(\otimes\) commutes with direct sums), and this is an isomorphism of \(\mathcal A\)-modules. Since \(\pi^{-1}(X_s)\) is the preimage of \(X_s\),

\[ \mathcal G(\pi^{-1}(X_s))=(\pi_*\mathcal G)(X_s)=\Bigl(\bigoplus_{n\ge0}\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n\Bigr)(X_s)=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}(\mathcal L^n\otimes\mathcal F)(X_s), \]

the last equality because \(X_s\) is quasi-compact (see the remark in (a)). The same computation with \(X_s\) replaced by \(X\) (quasi-compact) gives \(\mathcal G(Z)=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)(X)\). In these descriptions, the restriction maps are componentwise, and the element \(s\in\mathcal L(X)\subset\mathcal A(X)=\mathcal O_Z(Z)\) acts by \(t\mapsto t\otimes s\), sending the \(n\)-th component to the \((n+1)\)-st.

(c) By (a), \(Z\) is Noetherian or separated quasi-compact, and \(\mathcal G=\pi^*\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.14(b)). Prop. 1.6 applied to \(Z\), \(\mathcal G\) and \(s\in\mathcal O_Z(Z)\) gives \(\mathcal G(Z)_s\simeq\mathcal G(Z_s)\), which is the first assertion.

For the inclusion, it suffices to work above each trivializing affine open \(U=X_i\). There \(\pi^{-1}(U)=\Spec\mathcal O_X(U)[T]\) and \(s=hT\) with \(h=h_i\) (here \(T\) corresponds to \(e_i\)). Hence \(Z_s\cap\pi^{-1}(U)=D(hT)\subseteq D(h)=\pi^{-1}(D(h))=\pi^{-1}(X_s\cap U)\). Since the \(X_i\) cover \(X\), \(Z_s\subseteq\pi^{-1}(X_s)\).

(d) A new proof of Lemma 1.25. Let \(\mathcal F\), \(\mathcal L\), \(s\) be as in the lemma (\(f\in\mathcal F(X)\), \(g\in\mathcal F(X_s)\) below), and use \(\mathcal G=\pi^*\mathcal F\), \(P_n=(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)(X)\), \(Q_n=(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)(X_s)\), \(P=\bigoplus P_n=\mathcal G(Z)\), \(\mathcal G(W)=\bigoplus Q_n\) for \(W=\pi^{-1}(X_s)\supseteq Z_s\). By (b), the restriction \(\mathcal G(Z)\to\mathcal G(W)\) is \(\bigoplus(P_n\to Q_n)\) and multiplication by \(s\) is the shift \(t\mapsto t\otimes s\). By (c), \(\mathcal G(Z_s)=P_s\).

(a) of Lemma 1.25. Let \(f\in P_0=\mathcal F(X)\) with \(f|_{X_s}=0\). The image of \(f\) in \(\mathcal G(W)=\bigoplus Q_n\) is the element with only the \(0\)-th component, \(f|_{X_s}\); it is \(0\). Hence \(f|_{Z_s}=0\) in \(\mathcal G(Z_s)=P_s\), i.e. \(f/1=0\) in \(P_s\). So \(s^kf=0\) in \(P\) for some \(k\ge0\), i.e. \(f\otimes s^k=0\) in \(P_k=(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^k)(X)\). (If \(k=0\) then \(f=0\) and every \(n\ge1\) works.) Hence \(f\otimes s^n=0\) for all \(n\ge\max(k,1)\).

(b) of Lemma 1.25. Claim: the restriction \(\rho:\mathcal G(W)\to\mathcal G(Z_s)\) is injective. It suffices to prove injectivity above every \(U=X_i\cap X_s=D(h)\subseteq X_i\) (the opens \(\pi^{-1}(D(h_i))\) cover \(W\)). With \(\mathcal O=\mathcal O_X(X_i)\), \(N=\mathcal F(D(h))\), we have \(\pi^{-1}(D(h))=\Spec\mathcal O_h[T]\) and \(\mathcal G\) on it is \((N[T])^\sim\) (Prop. 1.14(b)), while \(Z_s\cap\pi^{-1}(D(h))=D(hT)=\Spec\mathcal O_h[T,T^{-1}]\) with sections \(N[T,T^{-1}]\). The restriction \(N[T]\to N[T,T^{-1}]\) is injective, proving the claim locally; so if \(\sigma\in\mathcal G(W)\) and \(\rho(\sigma)=0\) then \(\sigma\) vanishes on each \(\pi^{-1}(D(h_i))\), hence \(\sigma=0\).

Now let \(g\in\mathcal F(X_s)=Q_0\subseteq\mathcal G(W)\). Its image in \(\mathcal G(Z_s)=P_s\) has the form \(t/s^k\) with \(t\in P\), \(k\ge0\), i.e. \(s^k\rho(g)=\rho(t|_W)\) where \(t=\sum_mt_m\), \(t_m\in P_m\). As \(\rho\) is \(\mathcal O_Z(Z)\)-linear, \(\rho(s^kg)=\rho(t|_W)\), so by the claim \(s^kg=t|_W\) in \(\mathcal G(W)=\bigoplus Q_n\). The left side is \(g\otimes s^k|_{X_s}\in Q_k\); comparing \(k\)-th components, \(t_k|_{X_s}=g\otimes s^k|_{X_s}\). So \(g\otimes s^k|_{X_s}\) lifts to \(t_k\in(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^k)(X)\), and for \(n\ge n_0:=\max(k,1)\) the section \(t_k\otimes s^{n-k}\in(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^n)(X)\) lifts \(g\otimes s^n|_{X_s}\). This is Lemma 1.25(b). ∎

Exercise 1.19

Problem

By considering (if necessary infinite) direct sums of sheaves of the form \(\mathcal O_X(-m)\), show that there does not exist an integer \(n_0\) as in Theorem 1.27 which suits all coherent sheaves on \(X\), and that this theorem is false for quasi-coherent sheaves on \(X\).

Solution

Setting. Theorem 1.27 concerns a projective scheme \(X\) over a ring \(A\) with its sheaf \(\mathcal O_X(1)\). The statement needs \(X\) to be genuinely “big”; it is false for, e.g., \(X=\Spec A\) itself (a point of \(\mathbb P^0_A\)), where every \(\mathcal O_X(n)=\mathcal O_X\) is generated by global sections. I prove it for \(X=\mathbb P^d_A\) with \(d\ge1\) and \(A\neq0\) (in particular for projective spaces over fields); the same argument works for any \(X\) with \(\mathcal O_X(-k)(X)=0\) and \(\mathcal O_X(-k)\neq0\) for all \(k\ge1\). The sheaves below are finitely generated quasi-coherent (and coherent if \(A\) is Noetherian), as in Thm. 1.27.

Basic fact. For \(k<0\), \(\mathcal O_X(k)(X)=0\) (Lemma 1.22), whereas \(\mathcal O_X(k)_x\simeq\mathcal O_{X,x}\neq0\) for every \(x\) (\(X\neq\emptyset\) as \(A\neq0\)). Hence \(\mathcal O_X(k)\) is not generated by global sections, at any point (Def. 1.2: the map \(\mathcal O_X(k)(X)\otimes\mathcal O_{X,x}\to\mathcal O_X(k)_x\) has source \(0\) and non-zero target).

No uniform \(n_0\). Suppose \(n_0\) is such that \(\mathcal F(n)\) is generated by global sections for all \(n\ge n_0\) and for every finitely generated quasi-coherent (or coherent) \(\mathcal F\). Take \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X(-m)\) with \(m=n_0+1\) (invertible, so finitely generated quasi-coherent, and coherent if \(A\) is Noetherian by Prop. 1.11). Then \(\mathcal F(n_0)=\mathcal O_X(-m)\otimes\mathcal O_X(n_0)=\mathcal O_X(-1)\) (Example 1.19: \(\mathcal O_X(a)\otimes\mathcal O_X(b)=\mathcal O_X(a+b)\)), which is not generated by global sections. Contradiction. So \(n_0\) depends on \(\mathcal F\).

Failure for quasi-coherent sheaves. Let \(\mathcal F=\bigoplus_{m\ge1}\mathcal O_X(-m)\), quasi-coherent by Prop. 1.12(a) (not finitely generated). We claim that \(\mathcal F(n)\) is not generated by global sections for any \(n\in\mathbb Z\), so no \(n_0\) exists at all. Fix \(n\), take \(m>\max(n,0)\) and consider the projection \(q:\mathcal F(n)=\bigoplus_{m'\ge1}\mathcal O_X(n-m')\to\mathcal O_X(n-m)\) (\(\otimes\) commutes with direct sums), a surjective homomorphism of \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules. If \(\mathcal F(n)\) were generated by global sections, so would be its quotient \(\mathcal O_X(n-m)\) (the images \(q(t)\), \(t\in\mathcal F(n)(X)\), generate every stalk of the quotient by surjectivity of \(q_x\)). But \(n-m<0\), contradicting the basic fact. Hence Theorem 1.27 fails for quasi-coherent sheaves. ∎

Exercise 1.20

Problem

Let \(B\) be a graded algebra over a ring \(A\) and \(C\) an \(A\)-algebra. Let \(f:\operatorname{Proj}(B\otimes_AC)\to\operatorname{Proj}B\) be the projection morphism (Prop. 3.1.9). For any graded \(B\)-module \(M\), endow \(M\otimes_AC\) with the grading \((M\otimes_AC)_n=M_n\otimes_AC\). Show that it is a graded \(B\otimes_AC\)-module and that \(f^*(\widetilde M)=(M\otimes_AC)^\sim\).

Solution

Put \(E=B\otimes_AC\) (graded by \(E_d=B_d\otimes_AC\)) and \(N=M\otimes_AC\). Since \(\otimes_A C\) commutes with direct sums, \(N=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb Z}(M_n\otimes_AC)\), so the grading makes sense. For \(b\in B_d\), \(c\in C\), \(m\in M_n\), \(c'\in C\): \((b\otimes c)(m\otimes c')=bm\otimes cc'\in M_{n+d}\otimes C=N_{n+d}\). As the elements \(b\otimes c\) (resp. \(m\otimes c'\)) generate \(E_d\) (resp. \(N_n\)) additively, \(E_dN_n\subseteq N_{n+d}\). So \(N\) is a graded \(E\)-module.

Let \(\phi:B\to E\), \(b\mapsto b\otimes1\), a graded homomorphism, and let \(g=f:\operatorname{Proj}E\to\operatorname{Proj}B\) be the induced morphism; for homogeneous \(u\in B_+\), \(g^{-1}(D_+(u))=D_+(\phi(u))\) (proof of Prop. 3.1.9, via Lemma 2.3.40), and \(\operatorname{Proj}E=\bigcup_ug^{-1}D_+(u)\) because \(\operatorname{Proj}B=\bigcup D_+(u)\), \(u\) homogeneous in \(B_+\). Fix such \(u\), of degree \(r\ge1\). We have shown in the proof of Prop. 3.1.9 that the canonical map \(B_{(u)}\otimes_AC\to E_{(\phi(u))}\), \((b/u^k)\otimes c\mapsto(b\otimes c)/\phi(u)^k\) is an isomorphism.

Modules of degree \(0\). Localization commutes with \(\otimes_AC\), so \(N_{\phi(u)}=M_u\otimes_AC\), and the grading of \(N_{\phi(u)}\) is \((N_{\phi(u)})_e=(M_u)_e\otimes_AC\) (again because \(\otimes_AC\) commutes with direct sums). Taking \(e=0\) gives an isomorphism \(M_{(u)}\otimes_AC\xrightarrow{\sim}N_{(\phi(u))}\), \((m/u^k)\otimes c\mapsto(m\otimes c)/\phi(u)^k\).

Construction of the map. Define a morphism of \(\mathcal O_{\operatorname{Proj}B}\)-modules \(\beta:\widetilde M\to g_*\widetilde N\) on the base of principal opens as follows: for \(D_+(u)\), \(\widetilde M(D_+(u))=M_{(u)}\) and \((g_*\widetilde N)(D_+(u))=\widetilde N(D_+(\phi u))=N_{(\phi(u))}\) (Prop. 1.17(i)); let \(\beta_u(m/u^k)=(m\otimes1)/\phi(u)^k\). It is \(B_{(u)}\)-linear (where \(B_{(u)}\) acts on the target through \(B_{(u)}\to E_{(\phi u)}\)) and compatible with restriction to \(D_+(uv)\subseteq D_+(u)\) (both restrictions are given by the same formulas). By the adjunction \(g^*\dashv g_*\) (see the discussion in Ex. 1.16), \(\beta\) corresponds to a morphism \(\gamma:g^*\widetilde M\to\widetilde N\).

\(\gamma\) is an isomorphism. It suffices to check this on the open sets \(D_+(\phi(u))=g^{-1}D_+(u)\), which cover \(\operatorname{Proj}E\). Here \(D_+(u)=\Spec B_{(u)}\) and \(D_+(\phi u)=\Spec E_{(\phi u)}\) are affine, so by Prop. 1.14(b),

\[ g^*\widetilde M|_{D_+(\phi u)}=\bigl(M_{(u)}\otimes_{B_{(u)}}E_{(\phi u)}\bigr)^\sim=\bigl(M_{(u)}\otimes_{B_{(u)}}(B_{(u)}\otimes_AC)\bigr)^\sim=(M_{(u)}\otimes_AC)^\sim, \]

and \(\gamma\) on this open set is the map induced by \(M_{(u)}\otimes_AC\to N_{(\phi u)}\), \((m/u^k)\otimes c\mapsto(m\otimes c)/\phi(u)^k\) (this is what the adjunction produces: \(\gamma\) sends \(a\cdot g^*(m/u^k)\) to \(a\cdot\beta(m/u^k)\)). That map is an isomorphism by the previous paragraph. Hence \(\gamma\) is an isomorphism, i.e. \(f^*(\widetilde M)=(M\otimes_AC)^\sim\). ∎

Exercise 1.21

Problem

Let \(B\) be a graded Noetherian ring and \(M\) a finitely generated graded \(B\)-module. Show that \(\widetilde M\) is coherent on \(\operatorname{Proj}B\).

Solution

Here \(\widetilde M\) is the quasi-coherent sheaf of Prop. 1.17. Coherence is local (Def. 1.10), and \(\operatorname{Proj}B\) is covered by the \(D_+(u)=\Spec B_{(u)}\) with \(u\in B_+\) homogeneous (of degree \(r\ge1\)), on which \(\widetilde M|_{D_+(u)}=(M_{(u)})^\sim\) by Prop. 1.17(i). By Prop. 1.11, a quasi-coherent sheaf which is finitely generated on a locally Noetherian scheme is coherent. So it suffices to prove:

Claim. \(B_{(u)}\) is a Noetherian ring and \(M_{(u)}\) is a finitely generated \(B_{(u)}\)-module.

Given the claim, \(\Spec B_{(u)}\) is a Noetherian scheme, \(\widetilde{M_{(u)}}\) is quasi-coherent, and a surjection \(B_{(u)}^q\to M_{(u)}\) gives, by Prop. 1.5(b), a surjection \(\mathcal O^q\to\widetilde{M_{(u)}}\); so \(\widetilde{M_{(u)}}\) is finitely generated, hence coherent by Prop. 1.11.

Proof of the claim (this is Ex. 2.3.12 for \(B_{(u)}\); here is the argument). Since \(B\) is Noetherian, \(B_0=B/B_+\) is Noetherian and the ideal \(B_+\) is finitely generated; taking the homogeneous components of finitely many generators, \(B_+=(x_1,\dots,x_s)\) with \(x_i\) homogeneous of degree \(d_i\ge1\). Then \(B=B_0[x_1,\dots,x_s]\): by induction on \(n\), every \(b\in B_n\), \(n\ge1\), lies in \(B_+\), so \(b=\sum y_ix_i\) with \(y_i\) homogeneous of degrees \(n-d_i<n\).

Let \(B'=B_0[x_1^r,\dots,x_s^r,u]\subseteq B\), a finitely generated algebra over \(B_0\), hence Noetherian. \(B\) is a finitely generated \(B'\)-module: it is generated by the monomials \(x_1^{\alpha_1}\cdots x_s^{\alpha_s}\) with \(\alpha_i<r\) (since \(B=B_0[x_i]\) and \(x_i^r\in B'\)). Consider the Veronese subring \(B^{(r)}=\bigoplus_{k\ge0}B_{kr}\) and the \(B^{(r)}\)-module \(M^{(r)}=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb Z}M_{kr}\). Since \(M\) is finitely generated over \(B\), it is finitely generated over \(B'\), hence Noetherian over \(B'\); \(B^{(r)}\supseteq B'\) and \(M^{(r)}\) are \(B'\)-submodules of \(B\), resp. \(M\) (the elements \(x_i^r\) and \(u\) have degrees divisible by \(r\)), so both are finitely generated \(B'\)-modules, hence finitely generated over \(B^{(r)}\supseteq B'\). Therefore \(B^{(r)}\) is a finitely generated \(B_0\)-algebra (generated by the generators of \(B'\) and the finitely many module generators over \(B'\)), thus Noetherian.

The map \(B^{(r)}\to B_{(u)}\), \(\sum_ka_k\mapsto\sum_ka_k/u^k\) (\(a_k\in B_{kr}\)) is a surjective ring homomorphism; so \(B_{(u)}\) is Noetherian. Similarly \(M^{(r)}\to M_{(u)}\), \(\sum m_k\mapsto\sum m_k/u^k\) (\(m_k\in M_{kr}\)), is surjective and semilinear over \(B^{(r)}\to B_{(u)}\); since \(M^{(r)}\) is finitely generated over \(B^{(r)}\), the images of its generators generate \(M_{(u)}\) over \(B_{(u)}\). This proves the claim and the exercise. ∎

Exercise 1.22

Problem

Let \(B\) be a homogeneous algebra over a ring \(A\), quotient of some \(A[T_0,T_1,\dots,T_m]\). Let \(M\) be a graded \(B\)-module and fix \(n\in\mathbb Z\). Let \(\mathcal O_X(1)\) denote the sheaf on \(X=\operatorname{Proj}B\) given by the closed immersion \(X\to\mathbb P^m_A\). Consider the graded \(B\)-module \(M(n)\) given by \(M(n)_d=M_{d+n}\) for every \(d\in\mathbb Z\). Show that \(\widetilde{M(n)}=\widetilde M(n):=\widetilde M\otimes\mathcal O_X(n)\).

Solution

Setup. Let \(\pi:A[T_0,\dots,T_m]\to B\) be the surjective graded homomorphism, \(t_i=\pi(T_i)\in B_1\), \(P=\mathbb P^m_A=\operatorname{Proj}A[T]\) and \(\iota:X\to P\) the closed immersion induced by \(\pi\) (Lemma 2.3.40: \(\iota^{-1}D_+(T_i)=D_+(t_i)=:U_i\)). Since \(B_+\) is generated by \(t_0,\dots,t_m\) (as \(B\) is a homogeneous algebra), \(X=\bigcup U_i\). As in Def. 1.26, \(\mathcal O_X(n)=\iota^*\mathcal O_P(n)\). By Example 1.19, \(\mathcal O_P(n)|_{D_+(T_i)}=T_i^n\mathcal O_P|_{D_+(T_i)}\) is free with basis \(\varepsilon_i=T_i^n\), and on \(D_+(T_iT_j)\) one has \(\varepsilon_j=(T_j/T_i)^n\varepsilon_i\). Pulling back (\(\iota^*\mathcal O_P=\mathcal O_X\)): \(\mathcal O_X(n)|_{U_i}=\mathcal O_{U_i}e_i\) is free with basis \(e_i=\iota^*\varepsilon_i\), and \(e_j=(t_j/t_i)^ne_i\) on \(U_i\cap U_j\).

Local isomorphisms. On \(U_i=\Spec B_{(t_i)}\), \(\widetilde{M(n)}|_{U_i}=(M(n)_{(t_i)})^\sim\) (Prop. 1.17(i)). Define

\[ \lambda_i:M(n)_{(t_i)}\to M_{(t_i)},\qquad m/t_i^k\mapsto m/t_i^{n+k}\quad(m\in M(n)_k=M_{n+k}). \]

It is a well-defined \(B_{(t_i)}\)-linear bijection: \(M(n)_{t_i}=M_{t_i}\) as \(B_{t_i}\)-modules but with the grading shifted, so \(M(n)_{(t_i)}=(M(n)_{t_i})_0=(M_{t_i})_n\), and \(\lambda_i\) is multiplication by the unit \(t_i^{-n}\) of \(B_{t_i}\) (of degree \(-n\)), which maps \((M_{t_i})_n\) bijectively onto \((M_{t_i})_0=M_{(t_i)}\). Define

\[ \Psi_i:\widetilde{M(n)}|_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\ \lambda_i^\sim\ }\widetilde M|_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\ x\mapsto x\otimes e_i\ }(\widetilde M\otimes\mathcal O_X(n))|_{U_i}. \]

The second map is an isomorphism because \(\mathcal O_X(n)|_{U_i}=\mathcal O_{U_i}e_i\) is free of rank one with basis \(e_i\).

Gluing. On the stalk at \(\mathfrak p\in U_i\), \(\widetilde{M(n)}_{\mathfrak p}=M(n)_{(\mathfrak p)}\), whose elements are \(m/v\) with \(m\in M_{n+k}\), \(v\in B_k\setminus\mathfrak p\) homogeneous, and \(\Psi_i(m/v)=\bigl(m/(vt_i^n)\bigr)\otimes e_i\) (take \(t_i^k\) replaced by \(v\); this is the localization of the formula for \(\lambda_i\), consistent because \(\lambda_i\) is multiplication by \(t_i^{-n}\)). If also \(\mathfrak p\in U_j\), then

\[ \Psi_j(m/v)=\Bigl(\frac m{vt_j^n}\Bigr)\otimes e_j=\Bigl(\frac m{vt_j^n}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{t_j}{t_i}\Bigr)^n\otimes e_i=\Bigl(\frac m{vt_i^n}\Bigr)\otimes e_i=\Psi_i(m/v). \]

Hence the \(\Psi_i\) agree on all stalks, thus on \(U_i\cap U_j\), and glue to a morphism \(\Psi:\widetilde{M(n)}\to\widetilde M\otimes\mathcal O_X(n)\), which is an isomorphism because it is so on each \(U_i\). This is the canonical isomorphism \(\widetilde{M(n)}=\widetilde M(n)\).

Remark. For \(M=B\) this gives \(\widetilde{B(n)}\simeq\widetilde B\otimes\mathcal O_X(n)=\mathcal O_X(n)\), so the two descriptions of \(\mathcal O_X(n)\) (as \(\widetilde{B(n)}\) in Example 1.19 and as \(\iota^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^m_A}(n)\) in Def. 1.26) agree. ∎

Conventions for these exercises. Results of the text are cited by their numbers in Liu (“Prop. 1.31”, “Lemma 1.25”, …, all in Section 5.1). For a scheme \(Y=\operatorname{Proj}S[T_0,\dots,T_d]\) we write \(\mathcal{O}_Y(m)\) for the twists, and for a morphism \(g:Z\to Y\) the symbol \(g^*T_i\) denotes the global section of \(g^*\mathcal{O}_Y(1)\) induced by \(T_i\) (Prop. 1.31(a)). For an invertible sheaf \(\mathcal{L}\) and \(s\in\mathcal{L}(X)\), \(X_s\) is the open set of Def. 1.24.

Honest notes. (1) In Ex. 1.23 “automorphism” must mean automorphism of \(k\)-schemes (a field automorphism of \(\mathbb{C}\) induces an automorphism of \(\mathbb{P}^n_{\mathbb{C}}\) that is not linear); the fact that \(\operatorname{Pic}\mathbb{P}^n_k\) is generated by \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) is admitted, as in the statement. (2) Ex. 1.24 is false as printed: a counterexample is given, and the statement is proved under the extra hypothesis that \(\sigma^*\) preserves the space of sections \(f^*H^0(\mathbb{P}^n,\mathcal{O}(1))\) (this is automatic when that space is all of \(H^0(X,f^*\mathcal{O}(1))\)). (3) In Ex. 1.25(a) the equivalence with flatness is proved directly, without Ex. 1.15. (4) In Ex. 1.28 the “\(X'\)” of the first line is read as \(X\). (5) In Ex. 1.27(c) one needs \(A\neq0\) (for \(A=0\) everything is empty). (6) Ex. 1.29(b) and (c) are false as printed for an arbitrary faithfully flat morphism (counterexample given); they are proved when \(X'=X\otimes_AB\) for a faithfully flat ring map \(A\to B\), which is the situation in which they are used in Ex. 1.30. The lemma on flat base change of global sections needed for this is proved in the solution of 1.29. (7) In Ex. 1.30(a) the sections \(s_0,\dots,s_n\) have to be chosen suitably (an arbitrary generating family does not work; example given). (8) In Ex. 1.33(b) the map \(\rho\) is read as the projection \(X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\to X\) (otherwise \(\rho^*\mathcal{F}\) makes no sense). Exercises are cited below only with the needed argument given. Everything else is proved completely.

Exercise 1.23

Problem

(Automorphisms of \(\mathbb{P}^n_k\)) Let \(k\) be a field, \(B=k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) with \(n\ge1\) and \(X=\operatorname{Proj}B\). We are going to show that the automorphisms of \(X\) are linear; that is, that they come from \(k\)-linear transformations of \(B\). We admit that the group \(\operatorname{Pic}(X)\) (see Exercise 1.12) is generated by the class of \(\mathcal{O}_X(1)\) (Proposition 7.2.9). Let \(\sigma\) be an automorphism of \(X\). (a) Show that we have an isomorphism \(\lambda:\sigma^*\mathcal{O}_X(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(1)\). (b) Let \(\tau:\mathcal{O}_X(1)(X)\to\sigma^*\mathcal{O}_X(1)(X)\to\mathcal{O}_X(1)(X)\) be the composition of the canonical homomorphism followed by \(\lambda(X)\). Show that \(\tau\) is an automorphism of the \(k\)-vector space \(B_1=\mathcal{O}_X(1)(X)\). (c) Show that \(\tau\) canonically induces an automorphism \(\tilde\tau\) of the graded \(k\)-algebra \(B\) and that \(\sigma\) is the automorphism of \(X\) induced by \(\tilde\tau\).

Solution

Write \(\mathcal{O}(m)=\mathcal{O}_X(m)\). Recall: \(\mathcal{O}(m)\otimes\mathcal{O}(m')=\mathcal{O}(m+m')\) (Example 1.19 of the text), \(\mathcal{O}(m)(X)=B_m\) for \(m\ge0\) and \(=0\) for \(m<0\) (Lemma 1.22); inverse images commute with tensor products and send invertible sheaves to invertible sheaves. As said, \(\sigma\) is an automorphism of the \(k\)-scheme \(X\).

Two preliminary facts. (F1) For a linear form \(t\in B_1=\mathcal{O}(1)(X)\), the section \(t\) generates \(\mathcal{O}(1)_x\) exactly when \(x\in D_+(t)\). Indeed, for \(x\in D_+(T_j)\) we have \(\mathcal{O}(1)|_{D_+(T_j)}=T_j\mathcal{O}\), so \(t=(t/T_j)T_j\) with \(t/T_j\in\mathcal{O}(D_+(T_j))\), which is a unit at \(x\) iff \(t\notin x\), i.e. \(x\in D_+(t)\).

(F2) (Uniqueness, a strengthening of Rem. 1.32: the morphism is not assumed to be the one constructed in Prop. 1.31(b).) Let \(S\) be a ring, \(Y=\mathbb{P}^d_S=\operatorname{Proj}S[T_0,\dots,T_d]\), \(Z\) an \(S\)-scheme, \(\mathcal{M}\) an invertible sheaf on \(Z\) and \(s_0,\dots,s_d\in\mathcal{M}(Z)\) generating \(\mathcal{M}\). Let \(g:Z\to Y\) be an \(S\)-morphism and \(\mu:g^*\mathcal{O}_Y(1)\to\mathcal{M}\) an isomorphism with \(\mu(g^*T_i)=s_i\) for all \(i\). Then \(g^{-1}(D_+(T_i))=Z_{s_i}\) and \(g\) is the morphism attached to \((\mathcal{M},s_i)\) in Prop. 1.31(b). In particular two such morphisms (with possibly different \(\mu\)) coincide. Proof of (F2). Let \(z\in Z\), \(y=g(z)\). We have \((g^*\mathcal{O}(1))_z=\mathcal{O}(1)_y\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z,z}\). If \(y\in D_+(T_i)\) then \(T_i\) generates \(\mathcal{O}(1)_y\) by (F1), so \(g^*T_i\) generates the stalk at \(z\). If \(y\notin D_+(T_i)\) choose \(j\) with \(y\in D_+(T_j)\); then \(T_i=(T_i/T_j)T_j\) with \(T_i/T_j\in\mathfrak{m}_y\), so the germ of \(g^*T_i\) at \(z\) lies in \(\mathfrak{m}_z(g^*\mathcal{O}(1))_z\) and is not a generator. Since \(\mu\) is an isomorphism sending \(g^*T_i\) to \(s_i\), \(g^{-1}D_+(T_i)=Z_{s_i}\). On \(Z_{s_i}\) the morphism \(g\) maps to \(D_+(T_i)=\operatorname{Spec}S[T_j/T_i]_j\), so it is determined by the ring homomorphism \(T_j/T_i\mapsto g^\#(T_j/T_i)=g^*T_j/g^*T_i=s_j/s_i\in\mathcal{O}_Z(Z_{s_i})\). These data are exactly those defining the morphism of Prop. 1.31(b). \(\square\)

(a) Put \(\mathcal{L}=\sigma^*\mathcal{O}(1)\), an invertible sheaf. Since \(\operatorname{Pic}(X)\) is generated by \(\mathcal{O}(1)\), \(\mathcal{L}\simeq\mathcal{O}(m)\) for some \(m\in\mathbb{Z}\). By Prop. 1.31(a) (its proof does not use that \(\sigma\) is over a base), \(\mathcal{L}\) is generated by the global sections \(\sigma^*T_i\). If \(m<0\), then \(\mathcal{O}(m)(X)=0\) would force \(\mathcal{O}(m)_x=0\) for all \(x\), which is absurd as \(\mathcal{O}(m)_x\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\ne0\). So \(m\ge0\). The same argument for \(\sigma^{-1}\) gives \((\sigma^{-1})^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}(m')\) with \(m'\ge0\). Then

\[ \mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\sigma^*(\sigma^{-1})^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\sigma^*\mathcal{O}(m')\simeq(\sigma^*\mathcal{O}(1))^{\otimes m'}\simeq\mathcal{O}(mm'). \]

Taking global sections, \(n+1=\dim_kB_1=\dim_kB_{mm'}=\binom{n+mm'}{n}\) (here \(mm'\ge0\)). Since \(n\ge1\), \(j\mapsto\binom{n+j}{n}\) is strictly increasing on \(j\ge0\) (the increment is \(\binom{n+j}{n-1}>0\)), and takes the value \(n+1\) at \(j=1\). Hence \(mm'=1\), so \(m=1\), and \(\lambda\) exists.

(b) The canonical map \(\mathcal{F}(X)\to(\sigma^*\mathcal{F})(X)\) is \(s\mapsto\sigma^{-1}s\otimes1\). Since \(\sigma\) is an isomorphism of ringed spaces, \(\sigma^{-1}\mathcal{O}_X\to\mathcal{O}_X\) is an isomorphism and \(\sigma^{-1}\mathcal{F}(U)=\mathcal{F}(\sigma(U))\), so \(\sigma^*\mathcal{F}\simeq\sigma^{-1}\mathcal{F}\) and \(\mathcal{F}(X)\to(\sigma^*\mathcal{F})(X)\) is bijective. It is \(k\)-linear because \(\sigma\) is a \(k\)-morphism. As \(\lambda(X)\) is a \(k\)-linear bijection, \(\tau\) is a \(k\)-linear bijection of \(B_1\).

(c) Since \(B=k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) is the symmetric algebra of \(B_1\), \(\tau\) extends uniquely to a graded \(k\)-algebra endomorphism \(\tilde\tau\) of \(B\), and \(\tilde\tau\) is an automorphism with inverse \(\widetilde{\tau^{-1}}\). A graded automorphism \(\tilde\tau\) induces the automorphism \(g_\tau=\operatorname{Proj}\tilde\tau\) of \(X\) with \(g_\tau^{-1}(D_+(f))=D_+(\tilde\tau f)\), given there by \(\tilde\tau:B_{(f)}\to B_{(\tilde\tau f)}\).

Claim: for every \(\tau\in GL(B_1)\) (with \(t_i=\tau(T_i)\)) the \(t_i\) generate \(\mathcal{O}(1)\), and \(g_\tau\) is the morphism of Prop. 1.31(b) attached to \((\mathcal{O}(1),t_i)\). The \(T_i\) are \(k\)-linear combinations of the \(t_j\), so the \(t_j\) generate wherever the \(T_i\) do. By (F1), \(X_{t_i}=D_+(t_i)=D_+(\tilde\tau T_i)\) and the morphism of Prop. 1.31(b) maps \(D_+(t_i)\) to \(D_+(T_i)\) via \(T_j/T_i\mapsto t_j/t_i=\tilde\tau(T_j)/\tilde\tau(T_i)=\tilde\tau(T_j/T_i)\). This is the restriction of \(g_\tau\). The sets \(D_+(t_i)\) cover \(X\), which proves the claim.

Now by definition of \(\tau\), \(\lambda\) is an isomorphism \(\sigma^*\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathcal{O}(1)\) with \(\lambda(\sigma^*T_i)=\tau(T_i)=t_i\). By (F2) (with \(S=k\)), \(\sigma\) is the morphism attached to \((\mathcal{O}(1),t_i)\), i.e. \(\sigma=g_\tau=\operatorname{Proj}\tilde\tau\). So \(\sigma\) is induced by the automorphism \(\tilde\tau\) of the graded algebra \(B\). (The choice of \(\lambda\) is unique up to \(\operatorname{Aut}\mathcal{O}(1)=\mathcal{O}(X)^*=k^*\), so \(\tau\) and \(\tilde\tau\) are determined by \(\sigma\) up to the scalar actions \(c^{\deg}\), which induce the identity of \(X\).) ∎

Exercise 1.24

Problem

Let \(X\) be an algebraic variety over a field \(k\). Let \(f:X\to Y:=\mathbb{P}^n_k\) be a morphism of algebraic \(k\)-varieties. Let \(\mathcal{L}=f^*\mathcal{O}_Y(1)\). Let \(\sigma\) be an automorphism of \(X\) such that \(\sigma^*\mathcal{L}\simeq\mathcal{L}\). Show that there exists an automorphism \(\sigma'\) of \(Y\) such that \(\sigma'\circ f=f\circ\sigma\).

Solution

Automorphisms are \(k\)-automorphisms. Notation: \(B=k[T_0,\dots,T_n]\), \(s_i=f^*T_i\in\mathcal{L}(X)\), \(V=\sum_ik\,s_i\subseteq\mathcal{L}(X)\), the image of \(f^*:B_1\to\mathcal{L}(X)\); the \(s_i\) generate \(\mathcal{L}\) (Prop. 1.31(a)).

The statement as printed is false. Let \(\operatorname{char}k\ne2,3\) (e.g. \(k=\mathbb{C}\)), \(X=\operatorname{Proj}k[s,t]=\mathbb{P}^1_k\), \(n=3\), and \(F_0=s^4\), \(F_1=s^3t\), \(F_2=st^3\), \(F_3=t^4\in\mathcal{O}_X(4)(X)=k[s,t]_4\). They generate \(\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) (\(F_0\) generates on \(D_+(s)\), \(F_3\) on \(D_+(t)\)), so Prop. 1.31(b) gives \(f:X\to\mathbb{P}^3_k\) with \(\mathcal{L}=f^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) and \(f^*T_i\leftrightarrow F_i\) (one checks that \(f\) is even a closed immersion; this is not needed). Let \(\tilde\tau\) be the graded automorphism \(s\mapsto s,\ t\mapsto s+t\) of \(k[s,t]\) and \(\sigma=\operatorname{Proj}\tilde\tau\). By (F2) of the solution of 1.23 (Claim in (c)), \(\sigma^*\mathcal{O}_X(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(1)\) with \(\sigma^*s\leftrightarrow s\), \(\sigma^*t\leftrightarrow s+t\); taking tensor powers, \(\sigma^*\mathcal{O}_X(4)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) with \(\sigma^*F\leftrightarrow\tilde\tau(F)=F(s,s+t)\). So \(\sigma^*\mathcal{L}\simeq\mathcal{L}\).

Suppose \(\sigma'\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^3_k)\) with \(\sigma'f=f\sigma=:\varphi\). By 1.23(c), \(\sigma'^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}(1)\) with \(\sigma'^*T_i\leftrightarrow\sum_ja_{ij}T_j\) for some invertible matrix \((a_{ij})\). Then \(\varphi^*\mathcal{O}(1)=(f\sigma)^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\sigma^*\mathcal{O}_X(4)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) with \(\varphi^*T_i\leftrightarrow G_i:=\tilde\tau(F_i)\), and also \(\varphi^*\mathcal{O}(1)=(\sigma'f)^*\mathcal{O}(1)=f^*\sigma'^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) with \(\varphi^*T_i\leftrightarrow H_i:=\sum_ja_{ij}F_j\). Comparing the two isomorphisms gives an automorphism of \(\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) sending \(G_i\) to \(H_i\) for all \(i\). Automorphisms of \(\mathcal{O}_X(4)\) are multiplications by units of \(\mathcal{O}_X(X)=k\), so \(H_i=cG_i\) for some \(c\in k^*\). In particular \((s+t)^4=G_3=c^{-1}H_3\in\operatorname{span}(s^4,s^3t,st^3,t^4)\). But \((s+t)^4=s^4+4s^3t+6s^2t^2+4st^3+t^4\) has the nonzero coefficient \(6\) at \(s^2t^2\). Contradiction. So no such \(\sigma'\) exists.

Corrected statement. Assume, in addition, (i) the sections \(s_0,\dots,s_n\) are linearly independent over \(k\) (i.e. \(f^*:B_1\to\mathcal{L}(X)\) is injective), and (ii) there is an isomorphism \(\mu:\sigma^*\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}\) such that the \(k\)-linear automorphism \(\tilde\mu:\mathcal{L}(X)\to\mathcal{L}(X)\), \(\tilde\mu(s)=\mu(X)(\sigma^*s)\), satisfies \(\tilde\mu(V)\subseteq V\). (The map \(\tilde\mu\) is bijective by 1.23(b). Condition (ii) holds automatically if \(V=\mathcal{L}(X)\), i.e. if \(f\) is given by the complete linear system; in that case the printed statement is true as soon as (i) holds.) Then there is \(\sigma'\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{P}^n_k)\) with \(\sigma'f=f\sigma\).

Proof. \(\tilde\mu|_V\) is injective with \(V\) finite dimensional and \(\tilde\mu(V)\subseteq V\), so \(\tilde\mu(V)=V\). Write \(\tilde\mu(s_i)=\sum_jc_{ij}s_j\); by (i) the matrix \((c_{ij})\) is uniquely determined and invertible. Let \(\tau\in GL(B_1)\) with \(\tau(T_i)=\sum_jc_{ij}T_j=:t_i\) and \(\sigma'=\operatorname{Proj}\tilde\tau\). By the Claim in 1.23(c), \(\sigma'^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}(1)\) with \(\sigma'^*T_i\leftrightarrow t_i\). Hence \((\sigma'f)^*\mathcal{O}(1)=f^*\sigma'^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq f^*\mathcal{O}(1)=\mathcal{L}\) with \((\sigma'f)^*T_i\leftrightarrow f^*t_i=\sum_jc_{ij}s_j=\tilde\mu(s_i)\). On the other hand \((f\sigma)^*\mathcal{O}(1)=\sigma^*\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{\mu}\mathcal{L}\) with \((f\sigma)^*T_i=\sigma^*s_i\mapsto\tilde\mu(s_i)\). The sections \(\tilde\mu(s_i)\) generate \(\mathcal{L}\) (they span the same space as the \(s_j\)). Both \(\sigma'f\) and \(f\sigma\) are \(k\)-morphisms \(X\to\mathbb{P}^n_k\) satisfying the hypothesis of (F2) for the data \((\mathcal{L},\tilde\mu(s_i))\), so they are equal. ∎

Exercise 1.25

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a finite dominant morphism of integral locally Noetherian schemes. The integer \(n=[K(X):K(Y)]\) is called the degree of \(f\). (a) Show that for any \(y\in Y\), \(\mathcal{O}(X_y)\) is a \(k(y)\)-vector space of finite dimension and that \(\dim_{k(y)}\mathcal{O}(X_y)\ge n\). Show that \(f\) is flat if and only if there is equality for every \(y\in f(X)\) (use Exercise 1.15). (b) Let us suppose \(f\) is flat above \(y\) and that \(X_y\) is made up of exactly \(n\) points. Show that \(f\) is etale at every point of \(f^{-1}(y)\).

Solution

Local set-up. Let \(y\in Y\) and let \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\) be an affine open neighborhood, \(\mathfrak{p}\subset A\) the prime of \(y\). As \(f\) is finite, \(f^{-1}(\operatorname{Spec}A)=\operatorname{Spec}B\) with \(B\) a finite \(A\)-algebra; it is nonempty as \(f\) is dominant. Since \(X,Y\) are integral, \(A,B\) are domains. The kernel of \(A\to B\) is nilpotent because \(f\) is dominant, hence zero. So \(A\subseteq B\), \(K:=K(Y)=\operatorname{Frac}A\), and \(B\otimes_AK\) is a domain finite over the field \(K\), hence a field, equal to \(\operatorname{Frac}B=K(X)\). Thus \(n=\dim_K(B\otimes_AK)<\infty\). Also \(f\) is closed (\(f\) is finite) and dominant, hence surjective: \(f(X)=Y\). The fiber \(X_y=X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}k(y)=\operatorname{Spec}(B\otimes_Ak(y))\), so \(\mathcal{O}(X_y)=B\otimes_Ak(y)=B_\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}B_\mathfrak{p}\).

(a) Let \(M=B_\mathfrak{p}\), a finite torsion-free module over the Noetherian local domain \(A_\mathfrak{p}\); \(M\otimes K=K(X)\) has dimension \(n\). Then \(\mathcal{O}(X_y)=M/\mathfrak{p}M\) is finite dimensional over \(k(y)\) because \(M\) is finite. Let \(r=\dim_{k(y)}M/\mathfrak{p}M\). By Nakayama's lemma \(M\) is generated by \(r\) elements, i.e. there is a surjection \(\alpha:A_\mathfrak{p}^r\to M\). Tensoring with the flat module \(K\) gives a surjection \(K^r\to K^n\), so \(r\ge n\).

Flatness. \(f\) is flat iff \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) is flat over \(\mathcal{O}_{Y,f(x)}\) for all \(x\). For fixed \(\mathfrak{p}\), the module \(B_\mathfrak{p}\) is flat over \(A_\mathfrak{p}\) iff \((B_\mathfrak{p})_\mathfrak{q}=B_\mathfrak{q}\) is flat over \(A_\mathfrak{p}\) for all maximal ideals \(\mathfrak{q}\) of \(B_\mathfrak{p}\) (Tor commutes with localization of the \(B_\mathfrak{p}\)-module), and these are the primes of \(B\) above \(\mathfrak{p}\) (the maximal ideals of the finite extension \(A_\mathfrak{p}\subseteq B_\mathfrak{p}\) contract to the maximal ideal of \(A_\mathfrak{p}\)). Hence \(f\) is flat iff \(B_\mathfrak{p}\) is flat over \(A_\mathfrak{p}\) for all \(y\) (i.e. for all \(y\in f(X)=Y\)). If \(M=B_\mathfrak{p}\) is flat, then, being finitely presented over a local ring, it is free; its rank is \(\dim_K M\otimes K=n\). So \(r=n\). Conversely assume \(r=n\). Then \(K\otimes\alpha:K^n\to K^n\) is a surjective endomorphism of a finite dimensional space, hence injective; by flatness of \(K\), \(K\otimes\operatorname{Ker}\alpha=0\). But \(\operatorname{Ker}\alpha\subseteq A_\mathfrak{p}^n\subseteq K^n\) and \(\operatorname{Ker}\alpha\to K\otimes\operatorname{Ker}\alpha\subseteq K^n\) is the inclusion; hence \(\operatorname{Ker}\alpha=0\) and \(M\simeq A_\mathfrak{p}^n\) is free, in particular flat. This proves (a). (The argument replaces Exercise 1.15.)

(b) Etale means unramified and flat (Section 4.3 of the text): \(\mathfrak{m}_y\mathcal{O}_{X,x}=\mathfrak{m}_x\) and \(k(x)/k(y)\) finite separable. Let \(y\) be as in (b) and \(A,B,\mathfrak{p}\) as above. Flatness above \(y\) means that \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) is flat over \(\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\) for all \(x\in f^{-1}(y)\), i.e. \(B_\mathfrak{p}\) is flat over \(A_\mathfrak{p}\) as in (a). So \(B_\mathfrak{p}\) is free of rank \(n\), and \(F:=B_\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}B_\mathfrak{p}=\mathcal{O}(X_y)\) has dimension exactly \(n\) over \(k(y)\). \(F\) is a finite dimensional algebra over a field, hence artinian, hence the product of its localizations \(F_x=\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{m}_y\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) at its finitely many points \(x\in X_y=f^{-1}(y)\). By assumption there are exactly \(n\) points, each \(F_x\ne0\) has \(k(y)\)-dimension \(\ge1\), and the dimensions add up to \(n\). So \(\dim F_x=1\) for each \(x\), i.e. \(F_x=k(y)\cdot1\) is a field. Then \(F_x=\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{m}_y\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) being a field forces \(\mathfrak{m}_y\mathcal{O}_{X,x}=\mathfrak{m}_x\) and \(k(x)=F_x=k(y)\), which is trivially separable over \(k(y)\). Hence \(f\) is unramified and flat, i.e. etale, at every \(x\in f^{-1}(y)\) (\(f\) is finite, hence of finite type, and \(X,Y\) are locally Noetherian as required). ∎

Exercise 1.26

Problem

Let \(\mathcal{L}\) be an invertible sheaf on a scheme \(X\). We call a point \(x\in X\) such that \(H^0(X,\mathcal{L})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}_x\mathcal{L}_x\) a base point of \(\mathcal{L}\). Let \(x_1,\dots,x_n\in X\) be points that are not base points. Let us suppose that \(X\) is an algebraic variety over a field \(k\) with \(k\) infinite or \(\operatorname{Card}k\ge n\). Show that there exists a section \(s\in H^0(X,\mathcal{L})\) such that \(\mathcal{L}_{x_i}=s_{x_i}\mathcal{O}_{X,x_i}\) for every \(i\le n\).

Solution

(The inclusion in the definition means that the image of \(H^0(X,\mathcal{L})\) in \(\mathcal{L}_x\) lies in \(\mathfrak{m}_x\mathcal{L}_x\).) Only the facts that \(X\) is a \(k\)-scheme and \(|k|\ge n\) (or \(k\) infinite) are used. Let \(W=H^0(X,\mathcal{L})\), a \(k\)-vector space. For each \(i\) let \(\varepsilon_i:W\to\mathcal{L}_{x_i}/\mathfrak{m}_{x_i}\mathcal{L}_{x_i}\) be the germ map; it is \(k\)-linear, the target being a \(k(x_i)\)-vector space, hence a \(k\)-vector space. Put \(W_i=\operatorname{Ker}\varepsilon_i=\{s: s_{x_i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{x_i}\mathcal{L}_{x_i}\}\). Since \(x_i\) is not a base point, some \(s\in W\) has \(s_{x_i}\notin\mathfrak{m}_{x_i}\mathcal{L}_{x_i}\), so \(W_i\ne W\).

Lemma. A vector space \(W\) over \(k\) is not a union of \(m\) proper subspaces \(W_1,\dots,W_m\) if \(m\le|k|\) (in particular if \(k\) is infinite). Induction on \(m\); \(m=1\) is clear. Let \(m\ge2\) and suppose \(W=\bigcup W_i\). By the induction hypothesis \(W\ne\bigcup_{i\ge2}W_i\), so there is \(b\in W\setminus\bigcup_{i\ge2}W_i\); necessarily \(b\in W_1\). Take \(a\in W\setminus W_1\). For \(\lambda\in k\), \(a+\lambda b\notin W_1\) (else \(a\in W_1\)). For \(i\ge2\), \(W_i\) contains at most one of the elements \(a+\lambda b\): if it contained \(a+\lambda b\) and \(a+\lambda'b\) with \(\lambda\ne\lambda'\), then \(b\in W_i\). So at most \(m-1\) of the \(|k|\ge m\) elements \(a+\lambda b\) lie in \(\bigcup_{i\ge2}W_i\), and some \(a+\lambda b\) lies in no \(W_i\), a contradiction. \(\square\)

By the lemma there is \(s\in W\) with \(s\notin W_i\) for all \(i\le n\), i.e. \(s_{x_i}\notin\mathfrak{m}_{x_i}\mathcal{L}_{x_i}\). As \(\mathcal{L}_{x_i}=\mathcal{O}_{X,x_i}e\) is free of rank \(1\) over the local ring \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x_i}\), writing \(s_{x_i}=ae\) we get \(a\notin\mathfrak{m}_{x_i}\), so \(a\) is a unit and \(s_{x_i}\) generates \(\mathcal{L}_{x_i}\). ∎

Exercise 1.27

Problem

(d-uple embedding) Let \(A\) be a ring, and \(X=\operatorname{Proj}A[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) with \(n\ge1\). Let us fix an integer \(d\ge1\). (a) Let \(\{M_\alpha\}_\alpha\) denote the set of monomials of degree \(d\) in \(A[T_0,\dots,T_n]\). This set is of cardinal \(N:=\binom{n+d}{n}\). Let us consider the homomorphism \(\varphi:A[S_\alpha]_\alpha\to A[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) which sends \(S_\alpha\) to \(M_\alpha(T_0,\dots,T_n)\). Show that \(\varphi\) induces a closed immersion \(f:X\to\mathbb{P}^{N-1}_A=\operatorname{Proj}A[S_\alpha]_\alpha\) (Lemma 2.3.40). (b) Show that \(\{M_\alpha\}_\alpha\) generates \(H^0(X,\mathcal{O}_X(d))\), that \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\simeq f^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{N-1}_A}(1)\), and that \(f\) is the morphism associated to \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\) and the global sections \(\{M_\alpha\}_\alpha\) as in Proposition 1.31(b). (c) Show that \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\) is very ample if and only if \(d\ge1\).

Solution

Write \(B=A[T_0,\dots,T_n]\) and \(P=\mathbb{P}^{N-1}_A\). Recall \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)(X)=B_d\), a free \(A\)-module with basis \(\{M_\alpha\}\) (Lemma 1.22).

(b), first part. For \(x\in D_+(T_i)\) the sheaf \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\) is \(T_i^d\mathcal{O}_X\) on \(D_+(T_i)\) (Example 1.19 of the text), and \(T_i^d\) is one of the \(M_\alpha\). Hence the \(M_\alpha\) generate \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\), and they span \(H^0(X,\mathcal{O}_X(d))=B_d\) over \(A\).

Let \(f:X\to P\) be the morphism of Prop. 1.31(b) attached to \((\mathcal{O}_X(d),\{M_\alpha\})\). Then \(f^*\mathcal{O}_P(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(d)\) with \(f^*S_\alpha\leftrightarrow M_\alpha\). We describe \(f\). For a monomial \(M_\alpha\) the set \(X_{M_\alpha}\) is \(D_+(M_\alpha)\): on \(D_+(T_i)\) we have \(M_\alpha=(M_\alpha/T_i^d)T_i^d\) and \(M_\alpha/T_i^d\in B_{(T_i)}\) is a unit at the prime \(\mathfrak{p}\) iff \(M_\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}\). By the proof of Prop. 1.31(b), (F2) of the solution of 1.23, \(f^{-1}(D_+(S_\alpha))=D_+(M_\alpha)=\operatorname{Spec}B_{(M_\alpha)}\), and \(f\) is on it given by the ring homomorphism

\[ \varphi_\alpha:A[S_\beta/S_\alpha]_\beta\to B_{(M_\alpha)},\qquad S_\beta/S_\alpha\mapsto M_\beta/M_\alpha. \]

This is exactly the ring homomorphism induced by \(\varphi\) on the degree-\(0\) localizations, so \(f\) is the morphism of \(\operatorname{Proj}\)'s induced by \(\varphi\) (the graded ring map \(A[S_\alpha]\to B^{(d)}=\bigoplus_kB_{kd}\), and \(\operatorname{Proj}B^{(d)}=\operatorname{Proj}B\) via the covering by the \(D_+(M_\alpha)\)). This is the last assertion of (b), and the isomorphism \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\simeq f^*\mathcal{O}_P(1)\) is the one of Prop. 1.31(b).

(a) We show that each \(\varphi_\alpha\) is surjective. An element of \(B_{(M_\alpha)}\) is \(m/M_\alpha^k\) with \(m\in B_{kd}\), a combination of monomials \(T^c\) of degree \(kd\). Splitting the \(kd\) variable factors of \(T^c\) into \(k\) groups of \(d\), we write \(T^c=M_{\beta_1}\cdots M_{\beta_k}\), so \(T^c/M_\alpha^k=\prod_l(M_{\beta_l}/M_\alpha)=\varphi_\alpha(\prod_lS_{\beta_l}/S_\alpha)\). Therefore \(f|:D_+(M_\alpha)=f^{-1}(D_+(S_\alpha))\to D_+(S_\alpha)\) is the morphism of affine schemes attached to a surjective ring homomorphism, i.e. a closed immersion. The \(D_+(S_\alpha)\) cover \(P\) and being a closed immersion is local on the target, so \(f\) is a closed immersion.

(c) Assume \(A\ne0\) in the “only if” part. If \(d\ge1\), \(f\) is a closed immersion with \(f^*\mathcal{O}_P(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}_X(d)\), so \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\) is very ample (Def. 1.26). Conversely let \(d\le0\) and suppose \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\simeq i^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^M_A}(1)\) for an immersion \(i:X\to\mathbb{P}^M_A\). By Prop. 1.31(a) the sheaf \(i^*\mathcal{O}(1)\) is generated by the global sections \(i^*T_j\).

If \(d<0\), then \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)(X)=0\) (Lemma 1.22), so the stalks of \(\mathcal{O}_X(d)\) would be \(0\); but they are isomorphic to \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\ne0\) (\(X\neq\emptyset\) since \(A\ne0\)). Contradiction.

If \(d=0\): let \(\phi:i^*\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathcal{O}_X\) be an isomorphism and \(a_j=\phi(i^*T_j)\in\mathcal{O}_X(X)=B_0=A\). The \(a_j\) generate \(\mathcal{O}_X\). For each prime \(\mathfrak{p}\) of \(A\) there is a point \(x\) over \(\mathfrak{p}\) (\(X\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) is surjective), and some \(a_j\) is a unit in \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\), hence \(a_j\notin\mathfrak{p}\) (\(A_\mathfrak{p}\to\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) is local). So \((a_0,\dots,a_M)=A\), and the constant sections \(a_j\) generate \(\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Spec}A}\); Prop. 1.31(b) gives \(\varsigma:\operatorname{Spec}A\to\mathbb{P}^M_A\) attached to them. Let \(\pi:X\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) be the structure morphism and \(g=\varsigma\pi\). Then \(g^*\mathcal{O}(1)=\pi^*\varsigma^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\pi^*\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Spec}A}=\mathcal{O}_X\) with \(g^*T_j\leftrightarrow a_j\). By (F2) of 1.23, \(i=g=\varsigma\pi\). But \(i\) is an immersion, hence injective on points, while \(\pi\) is not injective: for any prime \(\mathfrak{p}\) the fiber over \(\mathfrak{p}\) is \(\mathbb{P}^n_{k(\mathfrak{p})}\) with \(n\ge1\), which contains the two distinct points \([1:0:\dots:0]\) and \([0:1:0:\dots:0]\). Contradiction. ∎

Exercise 1.28

Problem

Let \(X'\) be a projective scheme over a ring \(A\), and let \(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}\) be two invertible sheaves generated by their global sections. Let \(f:X\to\mathbb{P}^n_A\), \(g:X\to\mathbb{P}^m_A\) be morphisms such that \(\mathcal{L}\simeq f^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^n_A}(1)\) and \(\mathcal{M}\simeq g^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^m_A}(1)\). Let \(Y=\mathbb{P}^n_A\times_A\mathbb{P}^m_A\), and let \(p,q\) denote the respective projections of \(Y\) onto \(\mathbb{P}^n_A\) and \(\mathbb{P}^m_A\). (a) Show that \(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}\simeq h^*\mathcal{N}\), where \(h=(f,g)\) and \(\mathcal{N}:=p^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^n_A}(1)\otimes q^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^m_A}(1)\). (b) Let \(i:Y\to\mathbb{P}^{nm+n+m}_A\) be the Segre embedding (Lemma 3.3.31). Show that \(\mathcal{N}\simeq i^*\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{nm+n+m}_A}(1)\). (c) Show that if \(f\) is a closed immersion then so is \(h\) (use Lemma 3.3.15(b)). Deduce from this that \(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}\) is very ample if \(\mathcal{L}\) is very ample.

Solution

The first line of the statement says \(X'\); since \(f,g\) are defined on \(X\), we read \(X'=X\) (the projectivity of \(X\) is not used).

(a) \(h^*\mathcal{N}=h^*p^*\mathcal{O}(1)\otimes h^*q^*\mathcal{O}(1)=(ph)^*\mathcal{O}(1)\otimes(qh)^*\mathcal{O}(1)=f^*\mathcal{O}(1)\otimes g^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}\), since \(ph=f\), \(qh=g\) and inverse images commute with tensor products.

(b) Let \(T_0,\dots,T_n\) and \(U_0,\dots,U_m\) be the coordinates of \(\mathbb{P}^n_A\) and \(\mathbb{P}^m_A\) and \(S_{kl}\) (\(0\le k\le n\), \(0\le l\le m\)) those of \(\mathbb{P}^{nm+n+m}_A\). Recall that the Segre embedding \(i\) is glued from the morphisms \(Y_{kl}:=p^{-1}D_+(T_k)\cap q^{-1}D_+(U_l)=D_+(T_k)\times_AD_+(U_l)\to D_+(S_{kl})\) given by the ring homomorphisms \(S_{ab}/S_{kl}\mapsto(T_a/T_k)(U_b/U_l)\). Consider the sections \(\sigma_{kl}=p^*T_k\otimes q^*U_l\in\mathcal{N}(Y)\). At \(y\in Y\) the stalk \((p^*\mathcal{O}(1))_y=\mathcal{O}(1)_{p(y)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\) is free of rank \(1\) and \(p^*T_k\) generates it iff \(p(y)\in D_+(T_k)\) (see (F2) of the solution of 1.23); similarly for \(q^*U_l\). The tensor product of the two free rank \(1\) modules is generated by \(\sigma_{kl}\) iff both factors are generated by their sections (a product of two elements of a local ring is a unit iff both are). So \(Y_{\sigma_{kl}}=Y_{kl}\), these sets cover \(Y\), and the \(\sigma_{kl}\) generate \(\mathcal{N}\). On \(Y_{kl}\) we have \(\sigma_{ab}/\sigma_{kl}=(T_a/T_k)(U_b/U_l)\). Hence the morphism attached to \((\mathcal{N},\sigma_{kl})\) in Prop. 1.31(b) is glued from the morphisms \(Y_{kl}\to D_+(S_{kl})\) above, i.e. it is \(i\). By Prop. 1.31(b), \(\mathcal{N}\simeq i^*\mathcal{O}(1)\).

(c) Claim. Let \(h:X\to Y\) and \(p:Y\to Z\) be morphisms of schemes with \(p\) separated. If \(ph\) is a closed immersion (resp. an immersion), so is \(h\). Indeed \(h=\operatorname{pr}_2\circ\Gamma_h\) with \(\Gamma_h=(\operatorname{id}_X,h):X\to X\times_ZY\) and \(\operatorname{pr}_2:X\times_ZY\to Y\). The graph \(\Gamma_h\) is the base change of the diagonal \(\Delta:Y\to Y\times_ZY\) along \(h\times\operatorname{id}:X\times_ZY\to Y\times_ZY\), hence a closed immersion because \(p\) is separated. The projection \(\operatorname{pr}_2\) is the base change of \(ph:X\to Z\) along \(p:Y\to Z\), hence a closed immersion (resp. immersion) if \(ph\) is. A composition of two closed immersions is a closed immersion, and a closed immersion followed by an immersion is an immersion, which proves the claim.

Apply the claim with \(Z=\mathbb{P}^n_A\) and \(p:Y\to\mathbb{P}^n_A\) the projection, which is separated being the base change of \(\mathbb{P}^m_A\to\operatorname{Spec}A\). Since \(ph=f\), if \(f\) is a closed immersion then \(h\) is a closed immersion. In the same way, if \(f\) is an immersion then \(h\) is an immersion.

Now let \(\mathcal{L}\) be very ample, i.e. we may take \(f\) an immersion (closed or not) with \(\mathcal{L}\simeq f^*\mathcal{O}(1)\) (Def. 1.26); \(g\) is the given morphism with \(\mathcal{M}\simeq g^*\mathcal{O}(1)\). Then \(h\) is an immersion, \(i\) is a closed immersion (Lemma 3.3.31), so \(i\circ h:X\to\mathbb{P}^{nm+n+m}_A\) is an immersion, and by (a), (b)

\[ (ih)^*\mathcal{O}(1)=h^*i^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq h^*\mathcal{N}\simeq\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}. \]

So \(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}\) is very ample. ∎

Exercise 1.29

Problem

Let \(\pi:X'\to X\) be a faithfully flat morphism of schemes. (a) Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Show that there exists a homomorphism \(\varphi:\mathcal{O}_X^{(I)}\to\mathcal{F}\) such that \(\varphi(X)\) is surjective. Show that \(\mathcal{F}\) is generated by its global sections if and only if \(\varphi\) is surjective. (b) Show that \(\mathcal{F}\) is generated by its global sections if and only if \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is generated by its global sections. (c) Let us suppose \(X,X'\) are quasi-compact. Let \(\mathcal{L}\) be an invertible sheaf on \(X\). Show that if \(\pi^*\mathcal{L}\) is ample, then \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample.

Solution

(a) Let \(I=\mathcal{F}(X)\) and let \(\varphi=\psi:\mathcal{O}_X^{(I)}\to\mathcal{F}\) be the homomorphism of the proof of Lemma 1.3: \(\varphi\) sends the canonical basis element \(\varepsilon_s\) to \(s\) (\(s\in I\)). On the submodule \(\mathcal{O}_X(X)^{(I)}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_X^{(I)}(X)\) the map \(\varphi(X)\) sends \(\varepsilon_s\mapsto s\), hence \(\varphi(X)\) is onto \(\mathcal{F}(X)\). On stalks, \(\varphi_x(\sum a_s\varepsilon_s)=\sum a_ss_x\), so the image of \(\varphi_x\) is the \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\)-module generated by the germs \(s_x\), \(s\in\mathcal{F}(X)\). Since a morphism of sheaves is surjective iff it is surjective on stalks, \(\varphi\) is surjective iff \(\mathcal{F}\) is generated by its global sections. (Quasi-coherence is not needed.)

The statements (b) and (c) are false as printed. Let \(k\) be a field, \(X=\mathbb{P}^1_k\), \(U=D_+(T_0)\), \(V=D_+(T_1)\), \(X'=U\sqcup V\) (a disjoint union of two affine schemes, hence affine, and quasi-compact) and \(\pi:X'\to X\) the natural morphism; it is flat (open immersions are flat) and surjective, so faithfully flat. Let \(\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}_X(-1)\). Since \(X'\) is affine and \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.14(b)), \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is generated by its global sections (Thm. 1.7 and Lemma 1.3), and \(\pi^*\mathcal{L}^{\otimes r}\otimes\mathcal{G}\) is globally generated for every quasi-coherent \(\mathcal{G}\) for the same reason, so \(\pi^*\mathcal{L}\) is ample. However \(\mathcal{O}_X(-1)(X)=0\) by Lemma 1.22 while its stalks are nonzero, so \(\mathcal{F}\) is not generated by global sections, and \(\mathcal{L}^{\otimes r}=\mathcal{O}_X(-r)\) is not generated by global sections for any \(r\ge1\), so \(\mathcal{L}\) is not ample (take the f.g. sheaf \(\mathcal{O}_X\) in Def. 1.33). The implication “\(\Rightarrow\)” in (b) is however true for every morphism \(\pi\) (see below).

Corrected hypothesis. (BC): \(A\to B\) is a faithfully flat ring homomorphism, \(X\) is an \(A\)-scheme which is Noetherian or separated and quasi-compact, \(X'=X\times_{\operatorname{Spec}A}\operatorname{Spec}B\) and \(\pi\) is the projection. Then \(\pi\) is affine, surjective and flat (base change of \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\)), so faithfully flat, and \(X'\) is quasi-compact. This is the situation of Ex. 1.30.

Lemma (FB), flat base change of sections. Let \(R\to R'\) be a flat ring homomorphism, \(X\) an \(R\)-scheme that is Noetherian or separated and quasi-compact, \(X'=X\times_RR'\), \(\pi:X'\to X\), and \(\mathcal{G}\) a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Then the canonical map \(c:\mathcal{G}(X)\otimes_RR'\to(\pi^*\mathcal{G})(X')\), \(s\otimes r\mapsto r\cdot\pi^*s\), is bijective. Proof. For an open \(W\subseteq X\) put \(W'=\pi^{-1}(W)=W\times_RR'\) and let \(c_W:\mathcal{G}(W)\otimes_RR'\to(\pi^*\mathcal{G})(W')\) be the analogous map. Step 1: if \(W=\operatorname{Spec}C\) is affine, then \(W'=\operatorname{Spec}(C\otimes_RR')\) and \(\pi^*\mathcal{G}|_{W'}=(\mathcal{G}(W)\otimes_C(C\otimes_RR'))^\sim\) (Prop. 1.14(b), Thm. 1.7), whose global sections are \(\mathcal{G}(W)\otimes_RR'\); so \(c_W\) is bijective. Step 2: let \(W=\operatorname{Spec}C\) be affine and \(V=D(g_1)\cup\dots\cup D(g_m)\subseteq W\). We show \(c_V\) bijective by induction on \(m\); \(m=1\) is Step 1. For \(m\ge2\) let \(V_1=D(g_1)\cup\dots\cup D(g_{m-1})\) and \(D_m=D(g_m)\); then \(V=V_1\cup D_m\) and \(V_1\cap D_m=\bigcup_{j<m}D(g_jg_m)\) is a union of \(m-1\) principal open sets. The sheaf axiom gives an exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal{G}(V)\to\mathcal{G}(V_1)\oplus\mathcal{G}(D_m)\to\mathcal{G}(V_1\cap D_m)\), and the same for \(\pi^*\mathcal{G}\) on the primed sets. Tensoring the first with the flat module \(R'\) keeps it exact, and \(c\) gives a morphism between the two exact sequences whose second and third components are bijective by induction. Hence \(c_V\) is bijective. Step 3: let \(W=U_1\cup\dots\cup U_r\) with \(U_j\) affine open in \(X\); we prove \(c_W\) bijective by induction on \(r\) (\(r=1\) is Step 1). Put \(W_1=U_1\cup\dots\cup U_{r-1}\), \(U=U_r\). The set \(W_1\cap U\) is quasi-compact (in the Noetherian case every open set is; in the separated case it is the finite union of the affine sets \(U_j\cap U\)), and open in the affine scheme \(U\), hence a finite union of principal open subsets of \(U\). The Mayer–Vietoris argument of Step 2 applied to \(W=W_1\cup U\) shows that \(c_W\) is bijective, using the induction hypothesis for \(W_1\), Step 1 for \(U\) and Step 2 for \(W_1\cap U\). Finally \(X\) is quasi-compact, so it is such a \(W\). \(\square\)

Corrected (b). The implication “\(\Rightarrow\)” holds for any \(\pi\): if \(\mathcal{F}_x\) is generated by the germs of \(\mathcal{F}(X)\), then \((\pi^*\mathcal{F})_{x'}=\mathcal{F}_{\pi(x')}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X',x'}\) is generated by the germs of the sections \(\pi^*s\). Under (BC), “\(\Leftarrow\)” holds. Let \(\varphi:\mathcal{O}_X^{(I)}\to\mathcal{F}\) be as in (a) and \(\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Coker}\varphi\), a quasi-coherent sheaf (Prop. 1.12(c)). By (FB), \((\pi^*\mathcal{F})(X')=\mathcal{F}(X)\otimes_AB\), so every global section of \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is a finite sum \(\sum b_k\pi^*s_k\) with \(b_k\in B\) (acting through \(B\to\mathcal{O}_{X'}(X')\)) and \(s_k\in\mathcal{F}(X)\). Since \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is generated by global sections, its stalks are generated by the germs of such sections, hence by the germs of the sections \(\pi^*s\), \(s\in\mathcal{F}(X)\). This means that \(\pi^*\varphi:\mathcal{O}_{X'}^{(I)}\to\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is surjective (stalkwise, \((\pi^*\varphi)_{x'}=\varphi_{\pi(x')}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X',x'}\) has image generated by the \(\pi^*s\)). So \(\pi^*\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Coker}(\pi^*\varphi)=0\) (\(\pi^*\) is right exact). For \(x\in X\) choose \(x'\in\pi^{-1}(x)\) (\(\pi\) is surjective); \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\to\mathcal{O}_{X',x'}\) is a flat local homomorphism, hence faithfully flat, and \(0=(\pi^*\mathcal{C})_{x'}=\mathcal{C}_x\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X',x'}\) gives \(\mathcal{C}_x=0\). So \(\mathcal{C}=0\), i.e. \(\varphi\) is onto and \(\mathcal{F}\) is generated by global sections by (a).

Corrected (c). Assume (BC) and \(\pi^*\mathcal{L}\) ample on \(X'\) (which is quasi-compact). \(X\) is quasi-compact by hypothesis. Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be a finitely generated quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Then \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\) is finitely generated quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.14(b)), so there is \(n_0\) such that \(\pi^*\mathcal{F}\otimes(\pi^*\mathcal{L})^{\otimes n}=\pi^*(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n})\) is generated by global sections for \(n\ge n_0\). By corrected (b), \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}\) is generated by global sections for \(n\ge n_0\). Hence \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample. ∎

Exercise 1.30

Problem

Let \(X\) be a proper scheme over a Noetherian ring \(A\), and let \(\mathcal{L}\) be an invertible sheaf on \(X\). Let us suppose that there exists a faithfully flat base change \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) such that \(B\) is Noetherian and that \(\pi^*\mathcal{L}\) is very ample relative to \(B\), where \(\pi:X_B\to X\) is the canonical projection. We are going to show that \(\mathcal{L}\) is very ample relative to \(A\). (a) Show that \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample and generated by some global sections \(s_0,\dots,s_n\). Let \(f:X\to Y:=\mathbb{P}^n_A\) be the morphism associated to \(\mathcal{L}\) and to the \(s_i\). Show that \(f_B:X_B\to Y_B\) is a closed immersion. (b) Let \(y\in Y\) and let \(y'\in Y_B\) be a point lying above \(y\). Show that the fiber of \(X_B\) over \(y'\) is isomorphic to \(X_y\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k(y)}\operatorname{Spec}k(y')\). Deduce from this that \(f^{-1}(y)\) consists of at most one point, and that \(f\) is a topological closed immersion. (c) Show that \(\mathcal{O}_Y\to f_*\mathcal{O}_X\) is surjective and that \(f\) is a closed immersion.

Solution

Throughout, \(X\) is proper over \(A\), hence separated and quasi-compact over \(A\), and \(X_B=X\times_AB\), so (BC) of Ex. 1.29 holds and Lemma (FB) of the solution of 1.29 is available. Write \(\mathcal{L}_B=\pi^*\mathcal{L}\), \(Y_B=\mathbb{P}^n_B=\mathbb{P}^n_A\times_AB\), \(p:Y_B\to Y\) the projection, and \(f_B=f\times_A\operatorname{id}_B\), so that \(pf_B=f\pi\).

(a) Ampleness and generation. By hypothesis there is an immersion \(i:X_B\to\mathbb{P}^m_B\) with \(\mathcal{L}_B\simeq i^*\mathcal{O}(1)\), and \(t_j:=i^*T_j\) (viewed in \(\mathcal{L}_B(X_B)\)) generate \(\mathcal{L}_B\) (Prop. 1.31(a)). The scheme \(X_B\) is proper over \(B\) and \(\mathbb{P}^m_B\) is separated, so \(i\) is proper, hence has closed image; an immersion with closed image is a closed immersion (it is a closed immersion into an open subscheme \(W\subseteq\mathbb{P}^m_B\) with image closed in \(\mathbb{P}^m_B\), and the sheaf map \(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^m_B}\to i_*\mathcal{O}_{X_B}\) is onto at points of \(W\) and has zero target stalk outside the closed image). So \(X_B\) is projective over \(B\) and \(\mathcal{L}_B=\mathcal{O}_{X_B}(1)\) is ample by Thm. 1.27. By corrected 1.29(c), \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample; since \(\mathcal{L}_B\) is globally generated, corrected 1.29(b) shows that \(\mathcal{L}\) is generated by global sections.

Choice of the sections. By (FB), \(\mathcal{L}_B(X_B)=\mathcal{L}(X)\otimes_AB\), so we can write \(t_j=\sum_kb_{jk}\pi^*r_{jk}\) with \(b_{jk}\in B\), \(r_{jk}\in\mathcal{L}(X)\) (finitely many). Since \(X\) is quasi-compact and \(\mathcal{L}\) is generated by \(\mathcal{L}(X)\), finitely many global sections generate \(\mathcal{L}\) (each point has a neighborhood where finitely many sections generate; cover). Let \(s_0,\dots,s_n\in\mathcal{L}(X)\) consist of such a finite generating family together with all the \(r_{jk}\). (Some choice like this is necessary: for \(X=\mathbb{P}^1_A\), \(\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(2)\), which is very ample, the generating sections \(T_0^2,T_1^2\) give a degree \(2\) morphism \(\mathbb{P}^1\to\mathbb{P}^1\), not an immersion.) Let \(f:X\to Y=\mathbb{P}^n_A\) be the morphism of Prop. 1.31(b) with \(f^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{L}\), \(f^*T_i\leftrightarrow s_i\). Then \(f_B^*\mathcal{O}_{Y_B}(1)=f_B^*p^*\mathcal{O}_Y(1)=\pi^*f^*\mathcal{O}_Y(1)\simeq\mathcal{L}_B\), with \(f_B^*T_i\leftrightarrow\pi^*s_i\).

Each \(t_j=\sum_ic_{ji}\pi^*s_i\) with \(c_{ji}\in B\) (put \(c_{ji}=0\) for the \(s_i\) not occurring). Let \(\ell_j=\sum_ic_{ji}T_i\in H^0(Y_B,\mathcal{O}(1))\) and \(W=\bigcup_jD_+(\ell_j)\subseteq Y_B\), open. By (F1) of 1.23, \(\ell_j\) generates \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) exactly on \(D_+(\ell_j)\), so the \(\ell_j|_W\) generate \(\mathcal{O}(1)|_W\), and Prop. 1.31(b) gives a \(B\)-morphism \(c:W\to\mathbb{P}^m_B\) with \(c^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{O}(1)|_W\) and \(c^*T_j\leftrightarrow\ell_j\). We have \(f_B(X_B)\subseteq W\): for \(x\in X_B\) some \(t_j\) generates \(\mathcal{L}_{B,x}\); as \(f_B^*\ell_j\leftrightarrow\sum_ic_{ji}\pi^*s_i=t_j\), by the argument in the proof of (F2) of 1.23 (for any section \(\ell\) of \(\mathcal{O}(1)\), write \(\ell_{g(z)}=ue\) with \(e\) a generator; \(g^*\ell\) generates at \(z\) iff \(g^\#u\) is a unit iff \(u\) is a unit, as \(g^\#\) is local) \(f_B(x)\in D_+(\ell_j)\). Moreover \(cf_B:X_B\to\mathbb{P}^m_B\) satisfies \((cf_B)^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq f_B^*\mathcal{O}(1)\simeq\mathcal{L}_B\) with \((cf_B)^*T_j\leftrightarrow f_B^*\ell_j=t_j\), and \(i\) has the same property. By (F2), \(cf_B=i\). Now \(W\) and \(\mathbb{P}^m_B\) are separated \(B\)-schemes, so \(c\) is separated, and \(i=cf_B\) is a closed immersion. By the Claim in the solution of 1.28(c), \(f_B:X_B\to W\) is a closed immersion. Therefore \(f_B:X_B\to Y_B\) is an immersion; it is also proper (\(X_B\) proper over \(B\), \(Y_B\) separated), so its image is closed, and as in the previous paragraph it is a closed immersion.

(b) Since \(X_B=X\times_AB=X\times_YY_B\) (\(Y_B=Y\times_AB\)) and \(f_B\) is the second projection, the fiber of \(f_B\) over \(y'\) is

\[ X_B\times_{Y_B}\operatorname{Spec}k(y')=X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}k(y')=(X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}k(y))\times_{\operatorname{Spec}k(y)}\operatorname{Spec}k(y')=X_y\times_{k(y)}\operatorname{Spec}k(y'), \]

because \(\operatorname{Spec}k(y')\to Y\) factors through \(\operatorname{Spec}k(y)\) (as \(y'\) lies above \(y\)). Such a \(y'\) exists for every \(y\), since \(p:Y_B\to Y\) is surjective (base change of the surjection \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\)).

As \(f_B\) is a closed immersion, its fiber over \(y'\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{Y_B,y'}/\mathcal{I}\otimes k(y'))\), a quotient of the field \(k(y')\): it is empty or \(\operatorname{Spec}k(y')\), a single point. The morphism \(X_y\times_{k(y)}\operatorname{Spec}k(y')\to X_y\) is surjective (base change of \(\operatorname{Spec}k(y')\to\operatorname{Spec}k(y)\)). Hence \(X_y\) has at most one point, i.e. \(f^{-1}(y)\) has at most one point. Thus \(f\) is injective. The morphism \(f\) is proper (\(X\) proper over \(A\), \(Y\) separated over \(A\)), hence a closed map. An injective, continuous, closed map is a homeomorphism onto a closed subset: \(f\) is a topological closed immersion.

(c) The morphism \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact, so \(f_*\mathcal{O}_X\) is quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.14(c)). Let \(Y'=\operatorname{Spec}C\subseteq Y\) be an affine open set, \(X''=f^{-1}(Y')\) (separated and quasi-compact over \(C\), since \(f\) is) and let \(C_B=C\otimes_AB=\mathcal{O}(p^{-1}Y')\), so that \(f_B^{-1}(p^{-1}Y')=X''\times_CC_B=X''_B\). The sheaf \(f_*\mathcal{O}_X|_{Y'}\) is \(\mathcal{O}(X'')^\sim\) (Thm. 1.7), so it suffices to show that \(u:C\to\mathcal{O}(X'')\) is surjective. Let \(M=\operatorname{Coker}u\). By (FB) (applied to \(X''\) over \(R=C\) with \(R'=C_B\), flat over \(C\)), \(\mathcal{O}(X'')\otimes_CC_B=\mathcal{O}(X''_B)\), and under this identification \(u\otimes\operatorname{id}\) becomes the structure map \(C_B\to\mathcal{O}(X''_B)\) of \(f_B\) over the affine scheme \(p^{-1}Y'=\operatorname{Spec}C_B\). This map is surjective, because \(f_B\) restricted to \(X''_B\) is a closed immersion into an affine scheme (a closed subscheme of \(\operatorname{Spec}C_B\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}C_B/J\)). Therefore \(M\otimes_CC_B=\operatorname{Coker}(u\otimes\operatorname{id})=0\). Since \(C\to C_B=C\otimes_AB\) is faithfully flat (base change of \(A\to B\)), \(M=0\). So \(u\) is onto for all affine \(Y'\), i.e. \(\mathcal{O}_Y\to f_*\mathcal{O}_X\) is surjective (Prop. 1.5(b)). Together with (b), \(f\) is a closed immersion. Since \(\mathcal{L}\simeq f^*\mathcal{O}_Y(1)\), the sheaf \(\mathcal{L}\) is very ample relative to \(A\) (Def. 1.26). ∎

Exercise 1.31

Problem

Let \(\mathcal{L}\) be an ample sheaf and \(\mathcal{M}\) an invertible sheaf on a Noetherian scheme. Show that \(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}\) is ample if \(\mathcal{M}\) is ample or if \(\mathcal{M}\) is generated by its global sections.

Solution

A Noetherian scheme is quasi-compact. We use two remarks. (R1) If \(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}\) are generated by global sections, so is \(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{H}\): for \(x\in X\), \((\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{H})_x=\mathcal{G}_x\otimes\mathcal{H}_x\) is generated by the elements \(u_x\otimes v_x\) with \(u\in\mathcal{G}(X)\), \(v\in\mathcal{H}(X)\), which are the germs of the global sections \(u\otimes v\). (R2) Consequently if \(\mathcal{M}\) is generated by global sections, so is \(\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}\) for all \(n\ge1\).

In both cases there is \(n_1\ge1\) such that \(\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}\) is generated by global sections for all \(n\ge n_1\): if \(\mathcal{M}\) is globally generated take \(n_1=1\) by (R2); if \(\mathcal{M}\) is ample apply Def. 1.33 to the finitely generated sheaf \(\mathcal{O}_X\).

Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be a finitely generated quasi-coherent sheaf. As \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample there is \(n_0\) with \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}\) generated by global sections for \(n\ge n_0\). For \(n\ge\max(n_0,n_1)\),

\[ \mathcal{F}\otimes(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})^{\otimes n}\simeq(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n})\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n} \]

is generated by global sections by (R1). The sheaf \(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}\) is invertible; hence it is ample. ∎

Exercise 1.32

Problem

Let \(\mathcal{L}\) be an invertible sheaf on a quasi-compact scheme. Show that the following properties are equivalent: (i) \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample; (ii) \(\mathcal{L}^n\) is ample for every \(n\ge1\); (iii) there exists an \(n\ge1\) such that \(\mathcal{L}^n\) is ample.

Solution

Write \(\mathcal{L}^n=\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}\).

(i)\(\Rightarrow\)(ii). Let \(n\ge1\) and let \(\mathcal{F}\) be finitely generated quasi-coherent. By (i) there is \(n_0\) with \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^m\) globally generated for all \(m\ge n_0\). For \(q\ge n_0\) we have \(nq\ge n_0\), so \(\mathcal{F}\otimes(\mathcal{L}^n)^q=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{nq}\) is globally generated. Hence \(\mathcal{L}^n\) is ample.

(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(iii). Take \(n=1\).

(iii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i). Let \(\mathcal{L}^n\) be ample, and let \(\mathcal{F}\) be finitely generated quasi-coherent. For each \(r=0,\dots,n-1\) the sheaf \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^r\) is finitely generated quasi-coherent, so there is \(q_r\) with \((\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^r)\otimes(\mathcal{L}^n)^q\) globally generated for all \(q\ge q_r\). Let \(q_0'=\max_rq_r\) and \(N_0=nq_0'\). If \(N\ge N_0\), write \(N=nq+r\) with \(0\le r<n\); then \(n(q+1)>N\ge nq_0'\) gives \(q\ge q_0'\), and \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N=(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^r)\otimes(\mathcal{L}^n)^q\) is globally generated. So \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample. ∎

Exercise 1.33

Problem

Let \(X\) be a Noetherian or separated and quasi-compact scheme, and let \(f:X\to Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\) be a morphism. (a) Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be a finitely generated quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Let \(M\) be a sub-\(A\)-module of \(\mathcal{F}(X)\), and let \(x\in X\) be such that \(M\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\to\mathcal{F}_x\) is surjective. Show that this is then true in an open neighborhood of \(x\). (b) Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be as in (a). Show that \(\mathcal{F}\) is generated by its global sections if and only if for every \(y\in Y\), the sheaf \(\rho^*\mathcal{F}\), where \(\rho\) is the projection \(X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\), is generated by its global sections. (c) Let \(\mathcal{L}\) be an invertible sheaf on \(X\). Let us suppose that \(\rho^*\mathcal{L}\) is ample for every \(y\in Y\). Show that \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample.

Solution

In (b), (c) the map \(\rho=\rho_y\) is read as the projection \(X_{(y)}:=X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}=X\times_A\operatorname{Spec}A_\mathfrak{p}\to X\), where \(\mathfrak{p}\) is the prime of \(y\) (as in Prop. 1.37), since \(\mathcal{F}\) lives on \(X\). The morphism \(\rho_y\) is affine and flat, \(X_{(y)}\) is quasi-compact, and \(\rho_y^*\mathcal{F}\) is finitely generated quasi-coherent when \(\mathcal{F}\) is (Prop. 1.14(b)).

(a) The hypothesis says that the images of the elements of \(M\) generate the finitely generated \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\)-module \(\mathcal{F}_x\). Each of finitely many generators of \(\mathcal{F}_x\) is a finite \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\)-combination of germs of elements of \(M\), so there are \(m_1,\dots,m_r\in M\) whose germs generate \(\mathcal{F}_x\). Let \(\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{Coker}(\mathcal{O}_X^r\to\mathcal{F})\), the map sending the \(j\)-th basis vector to \(m_j\). Then \(\mathcal{G}\) is a quotient of the finitely generated sheaf \(\mathcal{F}\), so finitely generated, and \(\mathcal{G}_x=0\). Choose an open \(V\ni x\) and a surjection \(\mathcal{O}_V^k\to\mathcal{G}|_V\); the images \(g_1,\dots,g_k\in\mathcal{G}(V)\) of the basis vectors have zero germ at \(x\), so they vanish on an open \(V'\ni x\), \(V'\subseteq V\). Then \(\mathcal{G}|_{V'}=0\), i.e. \(\mathcal{O}_{V'}^r\to\mathcal{F}|_{V'}\) is surjective, and so \(M\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X,x'}\to\mathcal{F}_{x'}\) is surjective for all \(x'\in V'\). (No hypothesis on \(X\) is needed.)

(b) Local rings of \(X_{(y)}\). Let \(x\in X\) with \(f(x)=y\) (\(y\) corresponds to \(\mathfrak{p}\)). If \(U=\operatorname{Spec}C\) is an affine open neighborhood of \(x\), \(x\) corresponds to a prime \(\mathfrak{q}\) of \(C\) with \(\mathfrak{q}\cap A=\mathfrak{p}\), and \(U\times_A\operatorname{Spec}A_\mathfrak{p}=\operatorname{Spec}S^{-1}C\) with \(S\) the image of \(A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}\) in \(C\), \(S\cap\mathfrak{q}=\emptyset\). So there is a point \(x'\in X_{(y)}\) above \(x\), and \(\mathcal{O}_{X_{(y)},x'}=C_\mathfrak{q}=\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\).

Claim P. Let \(\mathcal{G}\) be a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\), \(x\in X\), \(y=f(x)\) and \(x'\) as above. If \((\rho_y^*\mathcal{G})_{x'}\) is generated by the germs of the global sections of \(\rho_y^*\mathcal{G}\), then \(\mathcal{G}_x\) is generated by the germs of \(\mathcal{G}(X)\). Proof. By Lemma (FB) of the solution of 1.29 (applied with \(R=A\), \(R'=A_\mathfrak{p}\), flat), \((\rho_y^*\mathcal{G})(X_{(y)})=\mathcal{G}(X)\otimes_AA_\mathfrak{p}\); its elements are the sums \(\sum(a_k/s_k)\rho^*u_k\) with \(u_k\in\mathcal{G}(X)\), \(a_k\in A\), \(s_k\in A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{p}\). Moreover \((\rho_y^*\mathcal{G})_{x'}=\mathcal{G}_x\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}\mathcal{O}_{X_{(y)},x'}=\mathcal{G}_x\), and the germ at \(x'\) of such a section is \(\sum(a_k/s_k)(u_k)_x\), where \(a_k/s_k\) acts through \(A_\mathfrak{p}\to\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) (the \(s_k\) are units in \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\) since \(f(x)=y\)). Thus the germs of global sections of \(\rho_y^*\mathcal{G}\) at \(x'\) lie in the \(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\)-submodule of \(\mathcal{G}_x\) generated by the germs of \(\mathcal{G}(X)\). If these generate \(\mathcal{G}_x\), so does \(\mathcal{G}(X)\). \(\square\)

Now (b). “\(\Rightarrow\)”: if \(\mathcal{F}\) is generated by global sections, then for each \(x'\in X_{(y)}\) over \(x\), \((\rho^*\mathcal{F})_{x'}=\mathcal{F}_x\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{(y)},x'}\) is generated by the germs of the sections \(\rho^*u\), \(u\in\mathcal{F}(X)\), which are global sections of \(\rho^*\mathcal{F}\). “\(\Leftarrow\)”: let \(x\in X\) and \(y=f(x)\); \(\rho_y^*\mathcal{F}\) is generated by global sections, in particular at \(x'\), so by Claim P, \(\mathcal{F}_x\) is generated by the germs of \(\mathcal{F}(X)\). (Finite generation of \(\mathcal{F}\) is not needed here.)

(c) \(X\) is quasi-compact (Noetherian, or by hypothesis), so the definition of ample applies. Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be a finitely generated quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). We show that every \(x\in X\) has an open neighborhood \(W_x\) and an integer \(N_x\) such that \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N\) is generated by its global sections on \(W_x\) (at all points of \(W_x\)) for every \(N\ge N_x\). Since \(X\) is quasi-compact, finitely many \(W_x\) cover \(X\), and then \(N_0=\max N_x\) works for \(\mathcal{F}\), proving that \(\mathcal{L}\) is ample.

Fix \(x\), \(y=f(x)\), \(x'\in X_{(y)}\) above \(x\). Since \(\rho_y^*\mathcal{L}\) is ample on \(X_{(y)}\), applying Def. 1.33 to \(\rho_y^*\mathcal{F}\) and to \(\mathcal{O}_{X_{(y)}}\) gives an \(n_y\) and a \(c\ge1\) such that \(\rho_y^*(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N)=\rho_y^*\mathcal{F}\otimes(\rho_y^*\mathcal{L})^N\) is globally generated for \(N\ge n_y\) and \(\rho_y^*(\mathcal{L}^c)=(\rho_y^*\mathcal{L})^c\) is globally generated. By Claim P, \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N\) is generated by \(\mathcal{G}(X)\) at \(x\) for all \(N\ge n_y\) (with \(\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N\)) and \(\mathcal{L}^c\) is generated by its global sections at \(x\). The sheaves \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N\) and \(\mathcal{L}^c\) are finitely generated quasi-coherent, so by (a) (with \(M\) the whole module of global sections) there is an open neighborhood \(W_x\) of \(x\) on which \(\mathcal{L}^c\) and the \(c\) sheaves \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N\), \(N=n_y,\dots,n_y+c-1\), are all generated by global sections. Let \(N\ge n_y\) and write \(N=j+qc\) with \(n_y\le j<n_y+c\), \(q\ge0\). Then \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^N=(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}^j)\otimes(\mathcal{L}^c)^q\) is generated by global sections on \(W_x\) by (R1) of the solution of 1.31 (stalkwise tensor product of globally generated sheaves; the case \(q=0\) is trivial). Take \(N_x=n_y\). This proves the claim and (c). ∎

5.2 Cech cohomology

Cohomology means Cech cohomology as in Def. 2.10, and results of the text are cited by number (“Thm. 2.19”, “Prop. 1.6”, …); Chapter 2 and 3 results are cited as “Ex. 2.2.8”, “Prop. 3.3.6”, and so on. For a sequence \(\alpha=(\alpha_0,\dots,\alpha_{q-1})\) of indices, \(U_\alpha=U_{\alpha_0}\cap\dots\cap U_{\alpha_{q-1}}\), and \(\alpha^{(l)}\) denotes \(\alpha\) with the entry \(\alpha_l\) removed.

Honest notes. In Ex. 2.1(c) a total ordering is not enough for the Zorn argument (one needs to be able to take the least element not yet treated), so the ordering is taken to be a well-ordering and (H\(_J\)) is only considered for initial segments \(J\); also (a) is vacuous as printed. In Ex. 2.4(b) the finiteness statement needs \(X\) Noetherian (it can fail for a non-Noetherian one-dimensional domain), so (b) is proved for Noetherian \(X\); (c) is not affected. In Ex. 2.6(b) direct limits commute with finite products only, so (b) is proved for finite coverings (the statement for infinite coverings is not proved); this is enough for (c), because the finite coverings are cofinal on the quasi-compact space \(X\). In Ex. 2.7(c) the gluing direction of Ex. 2.2.8 is as in the book. In Ex. 2.8(b) the complex (2.9) is taken with ordered cochains \(C''\) (Cor. 2.4), which is what makes it zero from rank \(n\) on. Ex. 2.9 is proved by replacing the kernel of \(\mathcal O_X^n\to\mathcal O_X\) by an induction involving only ideal sheaves.

Exercise 2.1

Problem

(Cohomology of flasque sheaves) Let \(X\) be a topological space. We say that a sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\) is flasque if for every pair of open subsets \(V\subseteq U\), the restriction \(\mathcal F(U)\to\mathcal F(V)\) is surjective. Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a covering of \(X\). We are going to show that \(H^{p+1}(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=0\) for every \(p\ge0\). Let us endow \(I\) with a total ordering with minimal element \(i_0\). Let us fix an \(f\in C^{p+1}(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\) such that \(df=0\).

  1. (a)

    For any \(i\in I\), let \(\mathcal U_i\) denote the covering \(U_i\cap\mathcal U\) of \(U_i\). Note that \(C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=C^{p-1}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F)\times\prod_{i'\ne i}C^{p-1}(\mathcal U_{i'},\mathcal F)\). Let \(J\) be a subset of \(I\) containing \(i_0\). Consider the hypothesis (H\(_J\)): there exist \(g_i=(g_{i\alpha})_{\alpha\in I^p}\in C^{p-1}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})\), \(i\in J\), such that for every \(i,j\in J\) with \(i<j\) and every \(\alpha\in I^p\) we have \(f_{ij\alpha}=g_{j\alpha}|_{U_{ij\alpha}}-(dg_i)_{j\alpha}\). Show that (H\(_J\)) is satisfied if \(J=\{i_0\}\).

  2. (b)

    Suppose (H\(_J\)) is true. Let \(k\in I\) be such that \(k>i\) for every \(i\in J\). Show that for any \(i,j\in J\), \(i\ne j\), we have \(f_{ik\alpha}+(dg_i)_{k\alpha}=f_{jk\alpha}+(dg_j)_{k\alpha}\) on \(U_{ijk\alpha}\) (develop \((df)_{ijk\alpha}\)). Show that there exist \(h_{k\alpha}\in\mathcal F(\bigcup_{i\in J}U_{ik\alpha})\) such that \(h_{k\alpha}|_{U_{ik\alpha}}=f_{ik\alpha}+(dg_i)_{k\alpha}\) for every \(i\in J\). Deduce that (H\(_{J\cup\{k\}}\)) is true (lift \(h_{k\alpha}\) to \(g_{k\alpha}\in\mathcal F(U_{k\alpha})\)).

  3. (c)

    Show that (H\(_I\)) is true (consider the set of couples \((J,(g_i)_{i\in J})\) verifying (H\(_J\)), ordered in a natural manner, and apply Zorn's lemma). Deduce from this that \(H^{p+1}(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=0\) (use Corollary 2.4).

Solution

Conventions. For \(i\in I\) the complex \(C^\bullet(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})\) is the Cech complex of the covering \(\{U_i\cap U_m\}_{m\in I}\) of \(U_i\), so \(C^{q}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})=\prod_{\beta\in I^{q+1}}\mathcal F(U_{i\beta})\).
