For \(p=0\) the statement uses \(C^{-1}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})\); we set \(C^{-1}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i}):=\mathcal F(U_i)\) (one empty index sequence) with \(d(g)=(g|_{U_{im}})_{m\in I}\in C^0\). Then \(d^2=0\) in all degrees \(\ge-1\) (for \(q=-1\): \((d dg)_{jk}=g|_{U_{ijk}}-g|_{U_{ijk}}=0\)). (Thus \(C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=\prod_{i\in I}C^{p-1}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})\) by singling out the first index, which is the decomposition of the statement.) A family \((g_i)_{i}\) with \(g_i\in C^{p-1}(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})\) is the same as an element \(G\in C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\) via \(G_{i\alpha}=g_{i\alpha}\); this is the identification in the statement. We write \(g_{i\alpha}=(g_i)_\alpha\); all identities below are identities of sections over the indicated open set, restrictions being understood. Since \(I\) may carry any total ordering for Cor. 2.4, we choose a well-ordering of \(I\); \(i_0\) is then its least element.

A formula. For \(j\in I\), \(k\in I\), \(\alpha\in I^p\) the definition of \(d\) gives

\[ (dg_j)_{k\alpha}=g_{j\alpha}-\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}(-1)^l g_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}\qquad(1) \]

(remove \(k\): sign \(+\) and get \(g_{j\alpha}\); remove \(\alpha_l\), which sits in position \(l+1\): sign \((-1)^{l+1}\)). For \(p=0\) this reads \((dg_j)_k=g_j|_{U_{jk}}\).

(a). For \(J=\{i_0\}\) there is no pair \(i<j\) in \(J\), so (H\(_J\)) is an empty condition and it holds with \(g_{i_0}=0\).

(b). Assume (H\(_J\)) and let \(k>i\) for all \(i\in J\). For \(i\in J\) put

\[ \psi_i:=f_{ik\alpha}+(dg_i)_{k\alpha}\in\mathcal F(U_{ik\alpha}). \]

Let \(i<j\) be in \(J\). We compute \(\psi_j-\psi_i\) on \(U_{ijk\alpha}\). Since \(df=0\), expanding \((df)_{ijk\alpha}\) (positions \(0,1,2\) hold \(i,j,k\); \(\alpha_l\) sits in position \(l+3\)) gives

\[ f_{jk\alpha}-f_{ik\alpha}+f_{ij\alpha}-\sum_{l=0}^{p-1}(-1)^l f_{ijk\alpha^{(l)}}=0. \]

By (H\(_J\)) applied to the pair \(i<j\) and to the index sequences \(\alpha\) and \(k\alpha^{(l)}\in I^p\),

\[ f_{ij\alpha}=g_{j\alpha}-(dg_i)_{j\alpha},\qquad f_{ijk\alpha^{(l)}}=g_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}-(dg_i)_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}. \]

Substituting, and using (1) for \((dg_j)_{k\alpha}\),

\begin{align*} \psi_j-\psi_i&=\bigl(f_{jk\alpha}-f_{ik\alpha}\bigr)+(dg_j)_{k\alpha}-(dg_i)_{k\alpha}\\ &=-g_{j\alpha}+(dg_i)_{j\alpha}+\sum_l(-1)^l\bigl(g_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}-(dg_i)_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}\bigr)+g_{j\alpha}-\sum_l(-1)^lg_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}-(dg_i)_{k\alpha}\\ &=(dg_i)_{j\alpha}-(dg_i)_{k\alpha}-\sum_l(-1)^l(dg_i)_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}\;=\;-(d^2g_i)_{jk\alpha}=0. \end{align*}

The last equality is the expansion \((d^2g_i)_{jk\alpha}=(dg_i)_{k\alpha}-(dg_i)_{j\alpha}+\sum_l(-1)^l(dg_i)_{jk\alpha^{(l)}}\) and \(d^2=0\) in \(C^\bullet(\mathcal U_i,\mathcal F|_{U_i})\). This proves \(\psi_i=\psi_j\) on \(U_{ijk\alpha}\) for all \(i\ne j\) in \(J\) (the case \(i>j\) is the same with the roles exchanged).

The open sets \(U_{ik\alpha}=U_i\cap U_{k\alpha}\), \(i\in J\), therefore carry sections \(\psi_i\) that agree on overlaps, so they glue to a section \(h_{k\alpha}\) over \(W_{k\alpha}=\bigcup_{i\in J}U_{ik\alpha}\) with \(h_{k\alpha}|_{U_{ik\alpha}}=\psi_i\). As \(W_{k\alpha}\subseteq U_{k\alpha}\) and \(\mathcal F\) is flasque, there is \(g_{k\alpha}\in\mathcal F(U_{k\alpha})\) with \(g_{k\alpha}|_{W_{k\alpha}}=h_{k\alpha}\). Put \(g_k=(g_{k\alpha})_{\alpha\in I^p}\in C^{p-1}(\mathcal U_k,\mathcal F|_{U_k})\). For \(i\in J\) we get \(g_{k\alpha}|_{U_{ik\alpha}}=f_{ik\alpha}+(dg_i)_{k\alpha}\), that is \(f_{ik\alpha}=g_{k\alpha}-(dg_i)_{k\alpha}\), which is the condition of (H\(_{J\cup\{k\}}\)) for the pairs \(i<k\); the pairs inside \(J\) are unchanged.

(c). Let \(\mathfrak S\) be the set of couples \((J,(g_i)_{i\in J})\) such that \(J\) is an initial segment of \(I\) (if \(j\in J\) and \(i<j\) then \(i\in J\)) containing \(i_0\) and (H\(_J\)) holds, ordered by \((J,g)\le(J',g')\) iff \(J\subseteq J'\) and \(g'_i=g_i\) for \(i\in J\). By (a), \((\{i_0\},0)\in\mathfrak S\). If \(\{(J_\lambda,g^\lambda)\}\) is a chain, put \(J=\bigcup J_\lambda\) with the common \(g_i\); \(J\) is an initial segment and (H\(_J\)) holds because two indices \(i<j\) of \(J\) lie in a common \(J_\lambda\). So Zorn's lemma gives a maximal \((J,g)\). If \(J\ne I\), let \(k=\min(I\setminus J)\); since \(J\) is an initial segment, \(k>i\) for all \(i\in J\), and (b) extends \((J,g)\) to \(J\cup\{k\}\) (again an initial segment), contradicting maximality. So \(J=I\): (H\(_I\)) holds.

Conclusion. By Prop. 2.3 every class of \(H^{p+1}(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\) is represented by an alternating cocycle, so we may assume \(f\) alternating. Let \(G\in C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\) be the cochain with \(G_{i\alpha}=g_{i\alpha}\). The expansion of \((dG)_{ij\alpha}=G_{j\alpha}-G_{i\alpha}+\sum_l(-1)^lG_{ij\alpha^{(l)}}\) and formula (1) (with \(j\leftrightarrow i\), \(k\leftrightarrow j\)) give \((dg_i)_{j\alpha}=G_{i\alpha}-\sum_l(-1)^lG_{ij\alpha^{(l)}}\), so (H\(_I\)) says exactly

\[ f_{ij\alpha}=(dG)_{ij\alpha}\qquad\text{for all }i<j\text{ in }I,\ \alpha\in I^p. \]

Let \(\pi:C\to C''\) be the projection onto the coordinates with strictly increasing indices. It commutes with the differentials, since removing an index from an increasing sequence leaves it increasing, and its restriction to \(C'\) is an isomorphism of complexes (proof of Cor. 2.4). The displayed identity says \(\pi(f)=d\,\pi(G)\) in \(C''^{p+1}\). Choose \(G'\in C'^p\) with \(\pi(G')=\pi(G)\). Then \(\pi(f)=\pi(dG')\), and injectivity of \(\pi|_{C'}\) gives \(f=dG'\). So \(f\) is a coboundary and \(H^{p+1}(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=0\) for every \(p\ge0\). Passing to the direct limit (Def. 2.10), \(H^q(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for all \(q\ge1\) as well. ∎

Exercise 2.2

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be an affine morphism (i.e., for every affine open subset \(V\) of \(Y\), \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine). Show that for any quasi-coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), and for any \(p\ge1\), we have \(R^pf_*\mathcal F=0\).

Solution

First, \(f\) is quasi-compact and separated, so that Prop. 2.28 applies. Quasi-compactness: for every affine open \(V\subseteq Y\), \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine, hence quasi-compact. Separatedness: for affine \(V\subseteq Y\), the composition \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\to\Spec\Z\) is a morphism between affine schemes, hence separated (affine schemes are separated), so \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\) is separated by Prop. 3.3.9(e). Being separated is local on the target (the diagonal being a closed immersion can be checked over an open covering of \(Y\)), so \(f\) is separated.

By Prop. 2.28, \(R^pf_*\mathcal F\) is the quasi-coherent sheaf on \(Y\) with \(R^pf_*\mathcal F(V)=H^p(f^{-1}(V),\mathcal F|_{f^{-1}(V)})\) for every affine open \(V\subseteq Y\). For such \(V\) the scheme \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine and \(\mathcal F|_{f^{-1}(V)}\) is quasi-coherent, so this group is \(0\) for \(p\ge1\) by Thm. 2.18. Now let \(W\subseteq Y\) be any open set and \(s\in R^pf_*\mathcal F(W)\). Cover \(W\) by affine opens \(V_\lambda\); then \(s|_{V_\lambda}=0\) for all \(\lambda\), hence \(s=0\) by the sheaf axiom. So \(R^pf_*\mathcal F=0\) for \(p\ge1\). ∎

Exercise 2.3

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes. Let \(\mathcal F\) be a sheaf on \(X\).

  1. (a)

    Show that we have a canonical homomorphism \(H^p(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) for every \(p\ge0\).

  2. (b)

    Let us suppose \(\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent. Show that the homomorphism in (a) is an isomorphism in each of the following cases: (1) \(X\) is separated and \(f\) is affine (use Theorem 2.19); (2) \(f\) is a closed immersion.

Solution

(a). For a covering \(\mathcal V=\{V_i\}_{i\in I}\) of \(Y\) put \(f^{-1}\mathcal V=\{f^{-1}(V_i)\}_{i\in I}\), a covering of \(X\). Since \(f^{-1}(V_{i_0\dots i_p})=f^{-1}(V_{i_0})\cap\dots\cap f^{-1}(V_{i_p})\) and \((f_*\mathcal F)(W)=\mathcal F(f^{-1}W)\),

\[ C^p(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal F)=\prod_{(i_0,\dots,i_p)}\mathcal F\bigl(f^{-1}V_{i_0\dots i_p}\bigr)=C^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F), \]

and this equality is compatible with \(d\) (both are alternating sums of restrictions). Hence \(H^p(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal F)=H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F)\). If \(\mathcal W=\{W_j\}_{j\in J}\) refines \(\mathcal V\) via \(\sigma:J\to I\), then \(f^{-1}\mathcal W\) refines \(f^{-1}\mathcal V\) via the same \(\sigma\) and the refinement maps correspond under the identification. Compose the identification with the canonical map \(H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) to get \(\varphi_{\mathcal V}:H^p(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\). If \(\mathcal W\) refines \(\mathcal V\), then \(\varphi_{\mathcal W}\circ\sigma^*=\varphi_{\mathcal V}\), because the canonical maps to the direct limit are compatible with refinement. Hence the \(\varphi_{\mathcal V}\) induce \(\varphi:H^p(Y,f_*\mathcal F)=\varinjlim_{\mathcal V}H^p(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F)\). It is canonical (no choices), and it needs no hypothesis on \(\mathcal F\) or \(f\).

(b)(1). Let \(\mathcal V=\{V_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a covering of \(Y\) by affine open sets. We show

\[ H^p(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal F)\to H^p(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\quad\text{and}\quad H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F)\to H^p(X,\mathcal F) \]

are isomorphisms. Since \(\varphi\circ(\text{can})=(\text{can})\circ(\text{identification of (a)})\) by construction, this shows that \(\varphi\) is an isomorphism. We do not assume \(Y\) separated.

The second map: each \(f^{-1}(V_i)\) is affine as \(f\) is affine, so \(f^{-1}\mathcal V\) is an affine covering of the separated scheme \(X\) and Thm. 2.19 applies.

The first map: we use Thm. 2.12 (Leray) for the sheaf \(\mathcal G=f_*\mathcal F\) on \(Y\) and the covering \(\mathcal V\), with \((\mathcal V^\lambda)\) the family of all coverings of \(Y\) by affine open sets (cofinal by Rem. 2.11). Let \(\alpha=(i_0,\dots,i_n)\) and \(\mathcal W=\{W_j\}_{j\in J}\) an affine covering of \(Y\). The covering of \(V_\alpha\) is \(\mathcal W_\alpha=\{V_\alpha\cap W_j\}_j\). By (a) applied over \(V_\alpha\) (the morphism \(f^{-1}(V_\alpha)\to V_\alpha\)),

\[ H^q(\mathcal W_\alpha,\mathcal G|_{V_\alpha})=H^q\bigl(f^{-1}\mathcal W_\alpha,\mathcal F|_{f^{-1}V_\alpha}\bigr). \]

Now \(f^{-1}(V_\alpha)=\bigcap_kf^{-1}(V_{i_k})\) is an intersection of affine opens of the separated scheme \(X\), hence affine (Prop. 3.3.6), and likewise \(f^{-1}(V_\alpha\cap W_j)=f^{-1}(V_\alpha)\cap f^{-1}(W_j)\) is affine, since \(f^{-1}(W_j)\) is affine. So \(f^{-1}\mathcal W_\alpha\) is an affine covering of the affine (separated) scheme \(f^{-1}(V_\alpha)\), and by Thm. 2.19 and Thm. 2.18, for \(q\ge1\),

\[ H^q(f^{-1}\mathcal W_\alpha,\mathcal F)=H^q(f^{-1}(V_\alpha),\mathcal F)=0. \]

So the hypothesis of Thm. 2.12 holds and \(H^p(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal F)\to H^p(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\) is an isomorphism. This proves (1).

(b)(2). Let \(f\) be a closed immersion. Then \(f\) is a homeomorphism onto the closed set \(f(X)\) (the argument below does not use quasi-coherence). Let \(\Phi\) send the class of a covering \(\mathcal V\) of \(Y\) to the class of \(f^{-1}\mathcal V\); this is well defined and order preserving (a refinement of \(\mathcal V\) pulls back to a refinement). By the identification of (a), it suffices to show that

\[ \varinjlim_{[\mathcal V]}H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F)\longrightarrow\varinjlim_{[\mathcal U]}H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=H^p(X,\mathcal F) \]

is an isomorphism (the left limit over classes of coverings of \(Y\), the right one over classes of coverings of \(X\)).

\(\Phi\) is surjective. (If \(X=\emptyset\) then \(f_*\mathcal F=0\) and both sides vanish, so assume \(X\ne\emptyset\).) Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_j\}_{j\in J}\) be a covering of \(X\). Because \(X\) has the topology induced from \(Y\), there are open \(W_j\subseteq Y\) with \(f^{-1}(W_j)=U_j\). Then \(\mathcal V=\{W_j\}_{j}\cup\{Y\setminus f(X)\}\) is a covering of \(Y\) (\(f(X)\) is closed), and \(f^{-1}\mathcal V=\{U_j\}_j\cup\{\emptyset\}\). This is equivalent to \(\mathcal U\): \(\mathcal U\) refines it by the inclusion of indices, and it refines \(\mathcal U\) by sending the extra index to any \(j_0\in J\). Consequently, by Cor. 2.9 the refinement map is an isomorphism \(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\simeq H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F)\), compatible with the maps to \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) (Lemma 2.8). Since every element of \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) comes from some \(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\), the limit map displayed above is surjective.

Injectivity. Let \(\xi\in H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal F)\) be zero in \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)\). Then \(\xi\) becomes zero in \(H^p(\mathcal U',\mathcal F)\) for some covering \(\mathcal U'\) refining \(f^{-1}\mathcal V\). Choose \(\mathcal V'\) with \(f^{-1}\mathcal V'\) equivalent to \(\mathcal U'\) and put \(\mathcal V''=\mathcal V\cap\mathcal V'\). Then \(\mathcal V''\) refines \(\mathcal V\), and \(f^{-1}\mathcal V''=f^{-1}\mathcal V\cap f^{-1}\mathcal V'\) refines \(\mathcal U'\). By Lemma 2.8 and compatibility of refinement maps with composition, the image of \(\xi\) in \(H^p(f^{-1}\mathcal V'',\mathcal F)\) is the image of its (zero) image in \(H^p(\mathcal U',\mathcal F)\), hence is \(0\). Since \(\mathcal V''\) refines \(\mathcal V\), \(\xi\) is zero in the left-hand direct limit.

Thus the map is bijective and \(H^p(Y,f_*\mathcal F)\simeq H^p(X,\mathcal F)\) for closed immersions (indeed for every sheaf \(\mathcal F\)). ∎

Exercise 2.4

Problem

Let \(X\) be an integral scheme. Let \(\mathcal K_X\) denote the constant sheaf \(K(X)\) on \(X\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(\mathcal K_X\) is a quasi-coherent sheaf.

  2. (b)

    For any point \(x\in X\), let \(\mathcal Q_x\) denote the skyscraper sheaf with support \(\{x\}\) and fiber \(K(X)/\mathcal O_{X,x}\) over \(x\). Let us suppose \(X\) is of dimension \(1\) and quasi-compact. Show that we have an exact sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves on \(X\): \(0\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal K_X\to\bigoplus_{x\in X}\mathcal Q_x\to0\) (show that every section \(s\) of \(\mathcal K_X/\mathcal O_X\) over an open subset \(U\) verifies \(s_x=0\) for all but finitely many points \(x\)).

  3. (c)

    Let \(X=\mathbf P^1_k\) be the projective line over a field \(k\). Let \(x_1,\dots,x_n\) be points of \(X\) that are pairwise distinct, and let \(f_1,\dots,f_n\in K(X)\). Show that there exists an \(f\in K(X)\) such that \(f-f_i\in\mathcal O_{X,x_i}\) for every \(i\) (apply Proposition 2.15 to the exact sequence in (b)).

Solution

Write \(K=K(X)\) and \(\eta\) for the generic point. The sheaf \(\mathcal K_X\) is the sheaf \(U\mapsto K\) for \(U\ne\emptyset\) (and \(0\) for \(U=\emptyset\)), with identity restrictions. It is a sheaf by Ex. 2.2.1, because \(X\) is irreducible, so every nonempty open subset is connected. For \(U\ne\emptyset\) open, \(\mathcal O_X(U)\to\mathcal O_{X,\eta}=K\) is injective (\(X\) integral), so \(\mathcal O_X\subseteq\mathcal K_X\) is a subsheaf of \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules, and \((\mathcal K_X/\mathcal O_X)_x=K/\mathcal O_{X,x}\).

(a). Let \(U=\Spec A\) be a nonempty affine open subset; \(A\) is a domain with fraction field \(K\). For \(0\ne g\in A\) we have \(D(g)\ne\emptyset\) and \(\widetilde K(D(g))=K_g=K\), because \(g\) is invertible in the field \(K\); and \(D(0)=\emptyset\) with \(K_0=0\). The restriction \(K_g\to K_{gh}\) is the identity of \(K\). Hence \(\widetilde K\) and \(\mathcal K_X|_U\) have the same sections, with the same restriction maps and the same \(A\)-module structure, on the base of principal open sets. A morphism of sheaves defined on a base extends uniquely (Ex. 2.2.7), so \(\mathcal K_X|_U\simeq\widetilde K\). By Prop. 1.5(c) the sheaf \(\mathcal K_X|_U\) is quasi-coherent; as \(U\) ranges over a covering of \(X\), \(\mathcal K_X\) is quasi-coherent (Thm. 1.7).

(b). Hypothesis. I assume that \(X\) is moreover Noetherian. The finiteness statement used below (an element of \(K\) has finitely many poles on \(X\)) is not available for arbitrary quasi-compact one-dimensional integral schemes: in the ring of all algebraic integers \(A\) (a domain of dimension \(1\)), the element \(1/2\) is non-integral at every one of the infinitely many primes of \(A\) containing \(2\). For Noetherian \(X\) everything works; (c) concerns \(\mathbf P^1_k\), which is Noetherian.

Points of \(X\). Every point \(x\ne\eta\) is closed: otherwise \(\overline{\{x\}}\) contains a point \(y\ne x\) and \(\{y\}\subsetneq\overline{\{x\}}\subsetneq X\) would be a chain of irreducible closed sets of length \(2\). Also \(\mathcal O_{X,\eta}=K\), so \(\mathcal Q_\eta=0\). For closed \(x\), \(\mathcal Q_x(U)=K/\mathcal O_{X,x}\) if \(x\in U\) and \(0\) otherwise (identity restrictions) and \((\mathcal Q_x)_y=K/\mathcal O_{X,x}\) for \(y=x\) and \(0\) otherwise.

Finiteness of poles. For \(a\in K\) let \(S_a=\{x\in X:a\notin\mathcal O_{X,x}\}\). We claim \(S_a\) is finite. Cover \(X\) by finitely many affine open subsets \(\Spec A_m\) (\(X\) is quasi-compact); each \(A_m\) is a Noetherian domain with fraction field \(K\). Write \(a=b/c\) with \(b,c\in A_m\), \(c\ne0\). For \(\mathfrak p\in D(c)\) we have \(a\in(A_m)_{\mathfrak p}=\mathcal O_{X,\mathfrak p}\), so \(S_a\cap\Spec A_m\subseteq V(c)\). Now \(V(c)\) is a closed subset of the Noetherian space \(\Spec A_m\) not containing \(\eta\) (\(c\ne0\)), so it has finitely many irreducible components \(\overline{\{z\}}\) with \(z\ne\eta\); each \(z\) is closed, so each component is the point \(z\). So \(V(c)\) is finite, and \(S_a\) is finite.

If \(U\subseteq X\) is open and \(s\in(\mathcal K_X/\mathcal O_X)(U)\), choose an open covering of \(U\) by sets \(U_j\) with \(s|_{U_j}\) the class of some \(a_j\in K\). As \(X\) is a Noetherian space, \(U\) is quasi-compact, so finitely many \(U_1,\dots,U_r\) suffice. If \(x\in U_j\) and \(s_x\ne0\) then \(a_j\notin\mathcal O_{X,x}\), i.e. \(x\in S_{a_j}\). So \(\{x\in U:s_x\ne0\}\subseteq S_{a_1}\cup\dots\cup S_{a_r}\) is finite.

The sheaf \(\bigoplus\mathcal Q_x\). For an open \(U\) let \(\mathcal Q(U)\subseteq\prod_{x}\mathcal Q_x(U)\) be the families with only finitely many nonzero components. It is a sheaf: if \(t\in\prod_x\mathcal Q_x(U)\) glues from finitely supported families on an open covering of \(U\), then finitely many members \(U_1,\dots,U_r\) cover \(U\) (\(U\) is quasi-compact) and \(\operatorname{supp}t\subseteq\bigcup\operatorname{supp}(t|_{U_j})\) is finite. Hence \(\mathcal Q=\bigoplus_x\mathcal Q_x\) is the direct sum in the category of sheaves.

The isomorphism. Define \(\theta:\mathcal K_X/\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal Q\) by \(\theta(s)=(s_x)_{x\in U}\), with \(s_x\in K/\mathcal O_{X,x}=\mathcal Q_x(U)\) (and \(0\) for \(x\notin U\)); it lands in \(\mathcal Q\) by the finiteness shown above and commutes with restrictions. On stalks: at \(\eta\), both stalks are \(0\) (a finitely supported family can be shrunk to \(0\) near \(\eta\) by removing its finitely many closed support points). At a closed point \(y\), \(\theta_y\) is injective: if \(s\in(\mathcal K_X/\mathcal O_X)(U)\) has \(\theta(s)=0\) near \(y\), then all germs of \(s\) vanish near \(y\), so \(s=0\) near \(y\). And \(\theta_y\) is surjective: a germ in \(\mathcal Q_y\) is represented by \(t\in\mathcal Q(U)\), finitely supported; removing from \(U\) the finitely many closed points of \(\operatorname{supp}t\) other than \(y\) we may assume \(t=(t_y,0,\dots)\). Lift \(t_y\) to \(a\in K\) and let \(s\) be the class of \(a\) in \((\mathcal K_X/\mathcal O_X)(U)\). Removing the finite set \(S_a\setminus\{y\}\) from \(U\), \(\theta(s)=t\) near \(y\). So \(\theta\) is an isomorphism and we get the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal K_X\to\bigoplus_x\mathcal Q_x\to0\).

Quasi-coherence. \(\mathcal O_X\) and \(\mathcal K_X\) are quasi-coherent; \(\bigoplus_x\mathcal Q_x\simeq\Coker(\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal K_X)\) is quasi-coherent by Prop. 1.12(c). Each \(\mathcal Q_x\) is the image of the projection endomorphism of \(\bigoplus_{x'}\mathcal Q_{x'}\) onto the summand \(\mathcal Q_x\), hence quasi-coherent by Prop. 1.12(c).

(c). \(X=\mathbf P^1_k\) is integral, Noetherian, of dimension \(1\), so (b) applies. Prop. 2.15 applied to \(0\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal K_X\to\bigoplus\mathcal Q_x\to0\) gives an exact sequence

\[ K(X)=\mathcal K_X(X)\to\Bigl(\bigoplus_x\mathcal Q_x\Bigr)(X)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=0, \]

the last equality by Example 2.20 (with \(A=k\)). So the first map is surjective; it sends \(f\) to the family \((f\bmod\mathcal O_{X,x})_x\). Let \(t\) be the family with \(t_{x_i}=f_i\bmod\mathcal O_{X,x_i}\) and \(t_x=0\) for \(x\notin\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\); this is well defined since the \(x_i\) are pairwise distinct and it has finite support. (If \(x_i=\eta\) the condition is empty, as \(\mathcal O_{X,\eta}=K\).) A preimage \(f\in K(X)\) of \(t\) satisfies \(f-f_i\in\mathcal O_{X,x_i}\) for all \(i\). ∎

Exercise 2.5

Problem

Let \(X\) be an open subset of the affine plane \(\mathbf A^2_k\) over a field \(k\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(F\) be a finite set of closed points \(p_1,\dots,p_n\) of \(\mathbf A^2_k\). Show that \(p_i\) corresponds to a maximal ideal of \(k[T,S]\) generated by two irreducible polynomials \(f_i\in k[T]\), \(g_i\in k[T,S]\). Changing the numbering if necessary, we may assume that \(f_1,\dots,f_m\) are pairwise prime to each other, and that \(f_i\) is equal to one of the \(f_1,\dots,f_m\) if \(i\ge m+1\). Let us set

    \[ f=\prod_{1\le i\le m}f_i,\qquad g=\sum_{1\le i\le m}\Bigl(\prod_{1\le j\le m,\,j\ne i}f_j\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{f_l=f_i}g_l\Bigr). \]

    Show that \(V(f,g)=\{p_1,\dots,p_n\}\).

  2. (b)

    Show that \(X\) is the union of two affine open sets (more generally, one can show that any non-empty open subset of \(\mathbf A^d_k\) is the union of \(d\) affine open subsets, see [53], Corollary V.1.5). Deduce from this that for any quasi-coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) and for any \(p\ge2\), we have \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=0\).

Solution

(a), first claim. A closed point of \(\mathbf A^2_k=\Spec k[T,S]\) is a maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m\). The field \(k[T,S]/\mathfrak m\) is a finite extension of \(k\) (Zariski's lemma). Hence the subring \(k[T]/(\mathfrak m\cap k[T])\) is a domain of finite dimension over \(k\), thus a field; and \(\mathfrak m\cap k[T]\ne0\) since \(k[T]\) is infinite-dimensional. So \(\mathfrak m\cap k[T]=(f_0)\) with \(f_0\) a monic irreducible polynomial. Put \(L=k[T]/(f_0)\) (a field). Then \(k[T,S]/(f_0)=L[S]\) and \(\mathfrak m/(f_0)\) is a maximal ideal of the principal ideal domain \(L[S]\), nonzero since \(L[S]\) is not a field. So \(\mathfrak m/(f_0)=(q)\) with \(q\) monic irreducible of degree \(d\ge1\). Lift the coefficients of \(q\) to polynomials in \(k[T]\) and let \(g_0=S^d+\sum_{j<d}c_j(T)S^j\in k[T,S]\) be the lift. Then \(\mathfrak m=(f_0,g_0)\). The polynomial \(g_0\) is irreducible: if \(g_0=ab\) in \(k[T][S]\), then, as \(g_0\) is monic in \(S\), we may take \(a,b\) monic in \(S\); if both had positive degree in \(S\), then reducing mod \(f_0\) would factor \(q=\bar a\bar b\) with factors of positive degree, a contradiction. So one factor has degree \(0\) in \(S\) and divides \(1\), a unit. Thus \(\mathfrak m=(f_0,g_0)\) with \(f_0\in k[T]\), \(g_0\in k[T,S]\) irreducible. Apply this to each \(p_i\) to get \(\mathfrak m_i=(f_i,g_i)\) with \(f_i\) monic.

(a), second claim. After renumbering, \(f_1,\dots,f_m\) are the distinct polynomials among \(f_1,\dots,f_n\); as distinct monic irreducible polynomials they are pairwise coprime in \(k[T]\). Put \(\hat f_i=\prod_{j\le m,\,j\ne i}f_j\) and \(h_i=\prod_{l:f_l=f_i}g_l\) (product over all \(l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\)), so \(f=\prod_{i\le m}f_i\) and \(g=\sum_{i\le m}\hat f_ih_i\).

\(V(f,g)\subseteq\{p_l\}\). Let \(\mathfrak P\) be a prime containing \(f\) and \(g\). Since \(\mathfrak P\) is prime and contains \(f\), it contains some \(f_{i_0}\) with \(i_0\le m\). For \(i\ne i_0\), \(\hat f_i\) contains the factor \(f_{i_0}\in\mathfrak P\), so \(\hat f_ih_i\in\mathfrak P\). Therefore \(\hat f_{i_0}h_{i_0}=g-\sum_{i\ne i_0}\hat f_ih_i\in\mathfrak P\). Now \(\hat f_{i_0}\notin\mathfrak P\): if \(f_j\in\mathfrak P\) for some \(j\ne i_0\) then \(\mathfrak P\cap k[T]\supseteq(f_j,f_{i_0})=k[T]\) (coprime in the PID \(k[T]\)), which is absurd. Hence \(h_{i_0}\in\mathfrak P\), so some \(g_l\in\mathfrak P\) with \(f_l=f_{i_0}\). Then \(\mathfrak P\supseteq(f_l,g_l)=\mathfrak m_l\), so \(\mathfrak P=\mathfrak m_l\) by maximality, i.e. the point is \(p_l\).

\(\{p_l\}\subseteq V(f,g)\). Let \(l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\) and \(f_l=f_{i_0}\) with \(i_0\le m\). Then \(f\in(f_{i_0})\subseteq\mathfrak m_l\). In \(g=\hat f_{i_0}h_{i_0}+\sum_{i\ne i_0}\hat f_ih_i\), the first term contains the factor \(g_l\in\mathfrak m_l\) and each other term contains \(f_{i_0}\in\mathfrak m_l\). So \(g\in\mathfrak m_l\). Thus \(V(f,g)=\{p_1,\dots,p_n\}\) (for \(n=0\) nothing is to be proved).

(b). If \(X=\emptyset\) there is nothing to prove, so let \(X\ne\emptyset\). Let \(Z=\mathbf A^2_k\setminus X=V(I)\) with \(I\subset k[T,S]\) an ideal; \(I\ne0\) as \(X\ne\emptyset\). Since \(k[T,S]\) is a UFD, let \(h=\gcd(I)\) and \(I=hJ\), where \(J\) is an ideal with no common nonunit divisor. Then \(Z=V(h)\cup V(J)\). We claim \(V(J)\) is a finite set of closed points. Indeed, let \(\mathfrak P\) be a minimal prime over \(J\). If \(\operatorname{ht}\mathfrak P=1\) then \(\mathfrak P=(q)\) is principal in the UFD, with \(q\) irreducible, and \(q\) divides every element of \(J\), contrary to the choice of \(J\); and \(\mathfrak P=0\) is impossible as \(J\ne0\). So \(\operatorname{ht}\mathfrak P=2=\dim k[T,S]\), and \(\mathfrak P\) is maximal (for a finitely generated domain over a field, \(\dim R/\mathfrak P=\dim R-\operatorname{ht}\mathfrak P=0\)). As \(k[T,S]\) is Noetherian there are finitely many minimal primes, so \(V(J)=\{p_1,\dots,p_n\}\) is a finite set of closed points (possibly empty, if \(J=(1)\)).

Take \(f,g\) as in (a) for these points (if \(n=0\) take \(f=1\), \(g=0\) and read \(D(f)\cup D(g)\) as \(D(f)\)). Then \(\mathbf A^2_k\setminus V(J)=D(f)\cup D(g)\) by (a), so

\[ X=\mathbf A^2_k\setminus(V(h)\cup V(J))=D(h)\cap\bigl(D(f)\cup D(g)\bigr)=D(hf)\cup D(hg). \]

Both \(D(hf)\) and \(D(hg)\) are principal open subsets of an affine scheme, hence affine. So \(X\) is the union of two affine open sets.

Finally \(X\) is a separated scheme (an open subscheme of an affine scheme: Prop. 3.3.9(a),(b)) covered by \(m=2\) affine open subsets. By Rem. 2.21, \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for every quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\) and every \(p\ge2\). ∎

Exercise 2.6

Problem

Let \(X\) be a Noetherian topological space. Let \((\mathcal F_\lambda)_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\) be a direct system of sheaves on \(X\).

  1. (a)

    Show that the correspondence \(U\mapsto\varinjlim_\lambda\mathcal F_\lambda(U)\), for every open set \(U\), defines a sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\).

  2. (b)

    Let us fix \(p\ge0\). Let \(\mathcal U\) be an open covering of \(X\). Show that the cohomology groups \(H^p(C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda))\), \(\lambda\in\Lambda\), naturally form a direct system and that we have a canonical isomorphism \(\varinjlim_\lambda H^p(C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda))\simeq H^p(C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F))\).

  3. (c)

    Show property (b) when replacing \(H^p(C(\mathcal U,*))\) by \(H^p(X,*)\).

Solution

As usual a direct system is indexed by a directed set \(\Lambda\) (so that direct limits are exact), and the sheaves are sheaves of Abelian groups (or modules over a fixed ring). Every open subset of the Noetherian space \(X\) is quasi-compact.

(a). The presheaf \(\mathcal F(U)=\varinjlim_\lambda\mathcal F_\lambda(U)\) has the obvious restriction maps. Let \(U=\bigcup_iU_i\) be an open covering.

Locality. Let \(s\in\mathcal F(U)\) with \(s|_{U_i}=0\) for all \(i\). Represent \(s\) by \(s_\lambda\in\mathcal F_\lambda(U)\). Since \(U\) is quasi-compact, finitely many \(U_1,\dots,U_r\) cover \(U\). For each \(i\le r\), \(s|_{U_i}=0\) means that \(s_\lambda|_{U_i}\) becomes \(0\) in some \(\mathcal F_{\mu_i}(U_i)\). Choose \(\mu\ge\lambda,\mu_1,\dots,\mu_r\) (so that \(\mu_i\) may be taken \(\ge\lambda\)). The image of \(s_\lambda\) in \(\mathcal F_\mu(U)\) restricts to \(0\) on each \(U_i\), \(i\le r\), hence is \(0\) since \(\mathcal F_\mu\) is a sheaf. So \(s=0\).

Gluing. Let \(s_i\in\mathcal F(U_i)\) be sections with \(s_i|_{U_{ij}}=s_j|_{U_{ij}}\). Take finitely many \(U_1,\dots,U_r\) covering \(U\). Represent \(s_i\) (\(i\le r\)) by \(s_i'\in\mathcal F_{\lambda_i}(U_i)\). For each pair \(i,j\le r\), \(s_i'|_{U_{ij}}\) and \(s_j'|_{U_{ij}}\) become equal in some \(\mathcal F_{\nu_{ij}}(U_{ij})\). Choose \(\nu\) larger than all \(\lambda_i\) and \(\nu_{ij}\) (finitely many). The images \(t_i\in\mathcal F_\nu(U_i)\) of \(s_i'\) agree on overlaps, so they glue to \(t\in\mathcal F_\nu(U)\); let \(s\in\mathcal F(U)\) be its class. Then \(s|_{U_i}=s_i\) for \(i\le r\). For an arbitrary index \(i\), the sections \(s|_{U_i}\) and \(s_i\) agree on each \(U_i\cap U_j\), \(j\le r\), which cover \(U_i\); by locality they are equal. So \(\mathcal F\) is a sheaf (a direct limit of sheaves over a Noetherian space is the presheaf direct limit).

(b). Scope. I prove this for finite coverings \(\mathcal U\). For an infinite covering the product \(\prod_\alpha\mathcal F_\lambda(U_\alpha)\) is an infinite product, which does not commute with direct limits, and the statement is not proved here. For (c) finite coverings are all we need.

Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be finite. For \(\lambda\le\mu\) the transition \(\mathcal F_\lambda\to\mathcal F_\mu\) induces \(C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda)\to C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\mu)\), which commutes with \(d\) (restrictions commute with sheaf morphisms); so the complexes \(C^\bullet(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda)\) form a direct system and, by functoriality of cohomology, so do the \(H^p(C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda))\). For \(q\ge0\), \(I^{q+1}\) is finite and direct limits commute with finite direct sums, so

\[ \varinjlim_\lambda C^q(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda)=\varinjlim_\lambda\prod_{\alpha\in I^{q+1}}\mathcal F_\lambda(U_\alpha)=\prod_{\alpha\in I^{q+1}}\varinjlim_\lambda\mathcal F_\lambda(U_\alpha)=C^q(\mathcal U,\mathcal F), \]

compatibly with \(d\). Since \(\varinjlim\) over a directed set is exact, it commutes with kernels, images and quotients: \(\ker(\varinjlim d)=\varinjlim\ker d\), \(\operatorname{im}(\varinjlim d)=\varinjlim\operatorname{im}d\), and so \(H^p(\varinjlim C_\lambda)=\varinjlim H^p(C_\lambda)\). The resulting isomorphism is induced by the canonical maps \(\mathcal F_\lambda\to\mathcal F\), hence is canonical.

(c). Since \(X\) is Noetherian, it is quasi-compact, so the finite coverings are cofinal among all coverings (Rem. 2.11). Hence \(H^p(X,\mathcal G)=\varinjlim_{\mathcal U}H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal G)\), the limit over (classes of) finite coverings, for any sheaf \(\mathcal G\). The isomorphisms of (b) are compatible with refinement maps (these are index maps and restrictions, which commute with the transition maps \(\mathcal F_\lambda\to\mathcal F_\mu\) and \(\mathcal F_\lambda\to\mathcal F\)). Direct limits commute with direct limits (both sides are the colimit over \(\Lambda\times\{\text{finite coverings}\}\)), so

\[ \varinjlim_\lambda H^p(X,\mathcal F_\lambda)=\varinjlim_\lambda\varinjlim_{\mathcal U}H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda)=\varinjlim_{\mathcal U}\varinjlim_\lambda H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F_\lambda)\overset{(b)}{=}\varinjlim_{\mathcal U}H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=H^p(X,\mathcal F). \]

∎

Exercise 2.7

Problem

Let \(X\) be a scheme. Let \(\mathcal O_X^*\) denote the sheaf of Abelian groups \(U\mapsto\mathcal O_X(U)^*\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(\mathcal L\) be an invertible sheaf on \(X\). Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{i\in I}\) be a covering of \(X\) such that for every \(i\in I\), \(\mathcal L|_{U_i}\) is free, generated by a section \(e_i\in\mathcal L(U_i)\). Show that there exist \(f_{ij}\in\mathcal O_X^*(U_{ij})\) such that \(e_i|_{U_{ij}}=e_j|_{U_{ij}}f_{ij}\) for every \(i,j\in I\). Let \(f:=(f_{ij})_{i,j\in I}\in C'^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\). Show that the image of \(f\) in \(H^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is independent of the choice of the \(e_i\). Show that the image \(\varphi(\mathcal L)\) of \(f\) in \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is independent of the choice of \(\mathcal U\).

  2. (b)

    Show that \(\varphi(\mathcal L)=1\) if and only if \(\mathcal L\) is free.

  3. (c)

    Show that the correspondence \(\mathcal L\mapsto\varphi(\mathcal L)\) induces a canonical group isomorphism \(\operatorname{Pic}(X)\simeq H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) (to prove the surjectivity, use Exercise 2.2.8 by taking \(\mathcal F_i=\mathcal O_{U_i}\), and \(\phi_{ij}\) to be the isomorphism defined by multiplication by \(f_{ij}\)).

  4. (d)

    Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes. Then \(\mathcal L\mapsto f^*\mathcal L\) induces a group homomorphism \(\operatorname{Pic}(Y)\to\operatorname{Pic}(X)\). In addition, the homomorphism \(\mathcal O_Y^*\to f_*\mathcal O_X^*\) canonically induces a homomorphism \(H^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y^*)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\). Show that these two homomorphisms are identical via the isomorphisms of (c).

Solution

Notation. The group \(\mathcal O_X^*(U)\) is written multiplicatively, so the differential of \(C^\bullet(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is \((du)_{ij}=u_ju_i^{-1}\) for a \(0\)-cochain, and \((df)_{ijk}=f_{jk}f_{ik}^{-1}f_{ij}\) for a \(1\)-cochain. A \(1\)-cochain \(f\) is an alternating cocycle iff \(f_{ii}=1\), \(f_{ji}=f_{ij}^{-1}\) and \(f_{ik}=f_{ij}f_{jk}\) on \(U_{ijk}\). The neutral element of \(H^1\) is written \(1\). By Prop. 2.3 every class in \(H^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) has an alternating representative.

(a), the cochain. On \(U_{ij}\) both \(e_i|\) and \(e_j|\) generate the free rank one module \(\mathcal L|_{U_{ij}}\). So there are unique \(f_{ij},f_{ji}\in\mathcal O_X(U_{ij})\) with \(e_i=e_jf_{ij}\) and \(e_j=e_if_{ji}\); then \(e_i=e_if_{ji}f_{ij}\) gives \(f_{ij}f_{ji}=1\) by uniqueness of coordinates in the basis \(e_i\). So \(f_{ij}\in\mathcal O_X^*(U_{ij})\), \(f_{ji}=f_{ij}^{-1}\), and \(f_{ii}=1\). On \(U_{ijk}\), \(e_i=e_jf_{ij}=e_kf_{jk}f_{ij}\) and \(e_i=e_kf_{ik}\), so \(f_{ik}=f_{ij}f_{jk}\) by uniqueness of coordinates with respect to \(e_k\). So \(f\in C'^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is an alternating cocycle.

Independence of the \(e_i\). Let \(e_i'\) be other generators of \(\mathcal L|_{U_i}\). Then \(e_i'=e_iu_i\) with \(u_i\in\mathcal O_X^*(U_i)\) (write \(e_i=e_i'v_i\) also; then \(u_iv_i=1\)). With \(e_i'=e_j'f_{ij}'\) we get \(e_iu_i=e_ju_jf'_{ij}\) and \(e_iu_i=e_jf_{ij}u_i\), so \(f'_{ij}=f_{ij}u_iu_j^{-1}=f_{ij}\cdot(du^{-1})_{ij}\) with \(u^{-1}=(u_i^{-1})_i\). Thus \(f'\) and \(f\) differ by a coboundary and have the same class in \(H^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\).

Independence of \(\mathcal U\). Let \(\mathcal V=\{V_j\}_{j\in J}\) be another covering with generators \(e'_j\in\mathcal L(V_j)\), and let \(f^{\mathcal U},f^{\mathcal V}\) be the corresponding cocycles. Let \(\mathcal W=\{U_i\cap V_j\}_{(i,j)}\), with refinement maps \(\sigma(i,j)=i\) and \(\tau(i,j)=j\). On \(W_{(i,j)}\) both \(e_i|\) and \(e'_j|\) are generators of \(\mathcal L\). Using the generators \(e_{\sigma(w)}|_{W_w}\), the cocycle on \(\mathcal W\) is \(\bigl(f^{\mathcal U}_{\sigma(w)\sigma(w')}|_{W_{ww'}}\bigr)_{w,w'}=\sigma(f^{\mathcal U})\); using the generators \(e'_{\tau(w)}|\) it is \(\tau(f^{\mathcal V})\). By the previous paragraph these two cocycles define the same class in \(H^1(\mathcal W,\mathcal O_X^*)\), i.e. \(\sigma^*[f^{\mathcal U}]=\tau^*[f^{\mathcal V}]\). Both \([f^{\mathcal U}]\) and \([f^{\mathcal V}]\) have the same image in \(\varinjlim=H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) as their images in \(H^1(\mathcal W,\mathcal O_X^*)\). So \(\varphi(\mathcal L)\) is well defined.

Moreover \(\varphi(\mathcal L)\) depends only on the isomorphism class of \(\mathcal L\): if \(\iota:\mathcal L\to\mathcal L'\) is an isomorphism, then \(\iota(e_i)\) are generators of \(\mathcal L'|_{U_i}\), and \(\iota(e_i)=\iota(e_j)f_{ij}\) by \(\mathcal O_X\)-linearity, which gives the same cocycle. Also \(\varphi\) is multiplicative: if \(\mathcal L,\mathcal M\) have generators \(e_i,e'_i\) on a common covering (refine), with cocycles \(f,f'\), then \(e_i\otimes e'_i\) generates \((\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal M)|_{U_i}\) and \(e_i\otimes e_i'=(e_j\otimes e'_j)f_{ij}f'_{ij}\), so \(\varphi(\mathcal L\otimes\mathcal M)=\varphi(\mathcal L)\varphi(\mathcal M)\).

(b). If \(\mathcal L\) is free, take \(\mathcal U=\{X\}\) and a generator \(e\); the only cochain entry is \(f_{11}=1\), so \(\varphi(\mathcal L)=1\). Conversely let \(\varphi(\mathcal L)=1\). By definition of the direct limit there is a refinement \(\sigma:\mathcal V\to\mathcal U\) with \(\sigma^*[f]=0\) in \(H^1(\mathcal V,\mathcal O_X^*)\). As computed above, \(\sigma(f)\) is the cocycle of \(\mathcal L\) for \(\mathcal V\) and the generators \(e_{\sigma(j)}|_{V_j}\). So replacing \(\mathcal U\) by \(\mathcal V\) we may assume \([f]=0\) in \(H^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\): there exist \(u_i\in\mathcal O_X^*(U_i)\) with \(f_{ij}=u_ju_i^{-1}\) on \(U_{ij}\). Then \(e_i':=e_iu_i\in\mathcal L(U_i)\) satisfy on \(U_{ij}\): \(e_i'=e_jf_{ij}u_i=e_ju_j=e'_j\). So the \(e'_i\) glue to a global section \(e\in\mathcal L(X)\), which generates \(\mathcal L\) on each \(U_i\). Hence \(\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal L\), \(1\mapsto e\) is an isomorphism on every \(U_i\), so it is an isomorphism, and \(\mathcal L\) is free.

(c). By (a), \(\varphi:\operatorname{Pic}(X)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is a well-defined group homomorphism (\(\operatorname{Pic}\) is the group of isomorphism classes of invertible sheaves under \(\otimes\)). By (b) its kernel is trivial, so it is injective. Surjectivity: let \(\xi\in H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\). It is the image of a class in \(H^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X^*)\) for some covering \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}\), represented by an alternating cocycle \(f=(f_{ij})\) (Prop. 2.3). Let \(\mathcal F_i=\mathcal O_{U_i}\) and let \(\phi_{ij}:\mathcal F_i|_{U_{ij}}\to\mathcal F_j|_{U_{ij}}\) be multiplication by \(f_{ij}\), an isomorphism of \(\mathcal O_{U_{ij}}\)-modules. We have \(\phi_{ii}=\operatorname{Id}\) since \(f_{ii}=1\), and \(\phi_{ik}=\phi_{jk}\circ\phi_{ij}\) on \(U_{ijk}\) since \(f_{ik}=f_{ij}f_{jk}\). By Ex. 2.2.8 there is a sheaf \(\mathcal L\) on \(X\) with isomorphisms \(\psi_i:\mathcal L|_{U_i}\to\mathcal O_{U_i}\) such that \(\psi_j=\phi_{ij}\circ\psi_i\) on \(U_{ij}\). (The construction there, \(\mathcal L(W)=\{(s_i)_i:s_i\in\mathcal F_i(W\cap U_i),\ \phi_{ij}(s_i)=s_j\text{ on }W\cap U_{ij}\}\), gives an \(\mathcal O_X\)-module because the \(\phi_{ij}\) are \(\mathcal O\)-linear, and the \(\psi_i\) are then \(\mathcal O\)-linear.) So \(\mathcal L\) is invertible. Put \(e_i=\psi_i^{-1}(1)\), a generator of \(\mathcal L|_{U_i}\). On \(U_{ij}\), \(\psi_j(e_i)=\phi_{ij}(\psi_i(e_i))=\phi_{ij}(1)=f_{ij}=\psi_j(e_jf_{ij})\), so \(e_i=e_jf_{ij}\). Hence the cocycle of \(\mathcal L\) for \(\mathcal U\) and the \(e_i\) is \(f\), and \(\varphi(\mathcal L)=\xi\). So \(\varphi\) is an isomorphism.

(d). Let \(\mathcal L\) be invertible on \(Y\), \(\mathcal V=\{V_i\}\) a covering with generators \(e_i\) and cocycle \(g=(g_{ij})\), \(e_i=e_jg_{ij}\). Let \(\varepsilon_i\in(f^*\mathcal L)(f^{-1}V_i)\) be the image of \(e_i\) under \(\mathcal L(V_i)\to(f^*\mathcal L)(f^{-1}V_i)\). This map is compatible with restrictions and satisfies \(a\,s\mapsto f^\#(a)\cdot(\text{image of }s)\) for \(a\in\mathcal O_Y(V_i)\). Moreover \(\varepsilon_i\) generates \(f^*\mathcal L\) on \(f^{-1}V_i\), since \(f^*(\mathcal L|_{V_i})\simeq f^*\mathcal O_{V_i}=\mathcal O_{f^{-1}V_i}\) with \(1\leftrightarrow\varepsilon_i\). So \(\varepsilon_i=\varepsilon_jf^\#(g_{ij})\) on \(f^{-1}(V_{ij})\). Therefore \(\varphi(f^*\mathcal L)\) is the class of the cocycle \((f^\#(g_{ij}))_{ij}\) of the covering \(f^{-1}\mathcal V\) of \(X\).

On the other hand the homomorphism \(H^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y^*)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is the composition \(H^1(Y,\mathcal O_Y^*)\to H^1(Y,f_*\mathcal O_X^*)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) of the map induced by \(f^\#:\mathcal O_Y^*\to f_*\mathcal O_X^*\) (Rem. 2.13) and the map of Ex. 2.3(a). On the class of \(g\in C^1(\mathcal V,\mathcal O_Y^*)\) the first map gives the class of \((f^\#(g_{ij}))\in C^1(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal O_X^*)\), and under the identification \(C^1(\mathcal V,f_*\mathcal O_X^*)=C^1(f^{-1}\mathcal V,\mathcal O_X^*)\) of Ex. 2.3(a) this is the cochain \((f^\#(g_{ij}))_{ij}\), whose class in \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X^*)\) is the image under the second map. So the two homomorphisms agree on \(\varphi(\mathcal L)\), i.e. \(\varphi_X(f^*\mathcal L)=f^*\varphi_Y(\mathcal L)\). ∎

Exercise 2.8

Problem

Let \(X\) be a quasi-compact open subscheme of an affine scheme \(\Spec A\). We suppose that \(H^p(X,\mathcal O_X)=0\) for every \(p\ge1\).

  1. (a)

    Show that there exist \(f_1,\dots,f_n\in A\) such that \(U_i:=D(f_i)\subseteq X\) and that \(X=\bigcup_{1\le i\le n}U_i\).

  2. (b)

    Let us set \(B=\mathcal O_X(X)\) and \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{1\le i\le n}\). Show that \(0\to B\to C^0(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\to C^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\to\dots\) (2.9) is an exact sequence of flat \(B\)-modules (use Proposition 2.3.12), zero from a finite rank on. Show that for any \(B\)-module \(M\), (2.9)\(\otimes_BM\) stays exact (use Proposition 1.2.6).

  3. (c)

    Let \(\mathcal F\) be a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Show that \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=0\) for every \(p\ge1\) (use Proposition 1.6 and (b)). Deduce from this that \(X\) is affine.

Solution

(a). The principal open sets \(D(f)\) form a base of the topology of \(\Spec A\), so \(X\) is the union of the principal open sets \(D(f)\) contained in \(X\). As \(X\) is quasi-compact, finitely many of them, \(D(f_1),\dots,D(f_n)\), suffice.

Preliminaries. \(X\) is separated (open subscheme of an affine scheme: Prop. 3.3.9(a),(b)) and quasi-compact, so Prop. 1.6 applies to \(X\). For \(g\in B\) write \(X_g=\{x\in X:g_x\in\mathcal O_{X,x}^*\}\). Let \(\bar f_i\in B\) be the restriction of \(f_i\) to \(X\). At \(x=\mathfrak p\in X\), \(\bar f_{i,x}\) is the image of \(f_i\) in \(A_{\mathfrak p}\), a unit iff \(f_i\notin\mathfrak p\), so \(X_{\bar f_i}=D(f_i)=U_i\). For an index sequence \(\alpha=(i_0,\dots,i_p)\) put \(\bar f_\alpha=\bar f_{i_0}\cdots\bar f_{i_p}\in B\); then \(X_{\bar f_\alpha}=U_{i_0}\cap\dots\cap U_{i_p}=U_\alpha\). By Prop. 1.6 (for \(\mathcal F\) quasi-coherent, in particular for \(\mathcal O_X\))

\[ \mathcal F(U_\alpha)=\mathcal F(X)_{\bar f_\alpha}=\mathcal F(X)\otimes_BB_{\bar f_\alpha},\qquad\mathcal O_X(U_\alpha)=B_{\bar f_\alpha},\qquad(2) \]

and these identifications are compatible with the restriction maps (they are induced by the restrictions \(\mathcal F(X)\to\mathcal F(U_\alpha)\) via the universal property of localization).

(b). Use the complex \(C''(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\) of strictly increasing index sequences (Cor. 2.4) as the complex (2.9); then \(C''^p=0\) for \(p\ge n\) (Cor. 2.5), so (2.9) is zero from rank \(n\) on. By (2), \(C''^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)=\bigoplus_{i_0<\dots<i_p}B_{\bar f_{i_0\dots i_p}}\) is a finite direct sum of localizations of \(B\), hence a flat \(B\)-module; \(B\) itself is flat. Exactness. \(B\to C''^0=\prod_i\mathcal O_X(U_i)\) is injective with image the kernel of \(d:C''^0\to C''^1\) (sheaf axiom for the covering \(\mathcal U\) of \(X\)). For \(p\ge1\), \(H^p(C''(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X))=H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\) (Cor. 2.4) \(=H^p(X,\mathcal O_X)\) (Thm. 2.19: \(X\) is separated and \(\mathcal U\) is an affine covering) \(=0\) by hypothesis. So (2.9) is exact.

Lemma. Let \(0\to E^{-1}\to E^0\to\dots\to E^{n-1}\to0\) be an exact complex of flat \(B\)-modules. Then it stays exact after \(\otimes_BM\), for every \(B\)-module \(M\). Proof. Let \(Z^q=\ker(d^q)\) (\(Z^n=0\), \(Z^{-1}=0\)), so we have short exact sequences \(0\to Z^q\to E^q\to Z^{q+1}\to0\) (\(-1\le q\le n-1\)). By descending induction \(Z^q\) is flat: \(Z^n=0\) is flat, and if \(Z^{q+1}\) and \(E^q\) are flat then the long exact \(\Tor\) sequence gives \(\Tor_1^B(Z^q,M)\) between \(\Tor_2^B(Z^{q+1},M)=0\) and \(\Tor_1^B(E^q,M)=0\), so \(Z^q\) is flat. Since \(Z^{q+1}\) is flat, \(0\to Z^q\otimes M\to E^q\otimes M\to Z^{q+1}\otimes M\to0\) is exact (the \(\Tor_1\) term vanishes; compare Prop. 1.2.6). The map \(d^q\otimes1\) factors as \(E^q\otimes M\twoheadrightarrow Z^{q+1}\otimes M\hookrightarrow E^{q+1}\otimes M\), so \(\ker(d^q\otimes1)=\operatorname{im}(Z^q\otimes M\to E^q\otimes M)=\operatorname{im}(d^{q-1}\otimes1)\), using that \(E^{q-1}\otimes M\to Z^q\otimes M\) is onto. For \(q=-1\): \(E^{-1}\otimes M\to E^0\otimes M\) factors through \(Z^0\otimes M\hookrightarrow E^0\otimes M\) and \(E^{-1}\otimes M\to Z^0\otimes M\) is an isomorphism (\(Z^{-1}=0\) and \(Z^{-1}\otimes M=0\)).

Applying the Lemma to \(E^{-1}=B\) and \(E^q=C''^q(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\), (2.9)\(\otimes_BM\) is exact for all \(M\).

(c). Let \(\mathcal F\) be quasi-coherent on \(X\) and \(M=\mathcal F(X)\). By (2), \(C''^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=\bigoplus_{i_0<\dots<i_p}M\otimes_BB_{\bar f_{i_0\dots i_p}}=C''^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\otimes_BM\), compatibly with \(d\) (the differential is an alternating sum of the restriction maps, which correspond to the localization maps tensored with \(M\)). By (b), \(C''(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\otimes_BM\) is exact in degrees \(\ge1\), so \(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=H^p(C''(\mathcal U,\mathcal F))=0\) for \(p\ge1\) (Cor. 2.4). By Thm. 2.19 (\(X\) separated, \(\mathcal U\) affine), \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)=H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=0\) for \(p\ge1\).

Thus (ii) of Serre's criterion (Thm. 2.23) holds for \(X\), which is separated and quasi-compact, so \(X\) is affine. ∎

Exercise 2.9

Problem

Show that in Serre's criterion (Theorem 2.23), we can replace condition (iii) by \(H^1(X,\mathcal J)=0\) for every quasi-coherent sheaf of ideals \(\mathcal J\).

Solution

Let \(X\) be Noetherian, or separated and quasi-compact. We show: \(X\) is affine if and only if (iii\('\)) \(H^1(X,\mathcal J)=0\) for every quasi-coherent sheaf of ideals \(\mathcal J\) of \(\mathcal O_X\). If \(X\) is affine, then (iii\('\)) holds by Thm. 2.18, since an ideal sheaf is a quasi-coherent sheaf. Conversely assume (iii\('\)). We follow the proof of Thm. 2.23; the only places where it used \(H^1\) of a sheaf that is not an ideal sheaf are the last step (\(\ker\psi\)), which we replace below, and we check that the first step uses an ideal sheaf. Recall \(X_f=\{y\in X:f_y\in\mathcal O_{X,y}^*\}\) for \(f\in A:=\mathcal O_X(X)\).

Step 1: every point lies in an affine open \(X_f\). Let \(\phi:X\to\Spec A\) be the canonical morphism. Let \(x\in X\). As in the book, \(\overline{\{x\}}\) with its reduced structure is a nonempty quasi-compact scheme, so it has a point closed in it, which is closed in \(X\) (Ex. 2.4.8). An open set containing this closed point \(y\in\overline{\{x\}}\) contains \(x\). So it is enough to find an affine open of the form \(X_f\) containing a given closed point \(y\); rename \(y\) as \(x\).

Let \(U\ni x\) be an affine open and let \(\mathcal J\) (resp. \(\mathcal M\)) be the quasi-coherent ideal sheaf of the reduced closed subscheme with underlying set \(X\setminus U\) (resp. \(\{x\}\)). The product \(\mathcal M\mathcal J\) is again a quasi-coherent sheaf of ideals (image of \(\mathcal M\otimes\mathcal J\to\mathcal O_X\); Prop. 1.12(b),(c)). In the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal M\mathcal J\to\mathcal J\to\mathcal J/\mathcal M\mathcal J\to0\) we have \((\mathcal J/\mathcal M\mathcal J)_y=0\) for \(y\ne x\) (because \(\mathcal M_y=\mathcal O_{X,y}\)), and \((\mathcal J/\mathcal M\mathcal J)_x=\mathcal O_{X,x}/\mathfrak m_x=k(x)\) since \(x\in U\) gives \(\mathcal J_x=\mathcal O_{X,x}\) and \(\mathcal M_x=\mathfrak m_x\). As \(x\) is closed, \((\mathcal J/\mathcal M\mathcal J)(X)=k(x)\) (a sheaf with zero stalks off the closed point \(x\) has its global sections equal to its stalk at \(x\): a germ at \(x\) extends by \(0\) on \(X\setminus\{x\}\)). By (iii\('\)), \(H^1(X,\mathcal M\mathcal J)=0\), so Prop. 2.15 shows \(\mathcal J(X)\to k(x)\) is surjective. Take \(f\in\mathcal J(X)\) with class \(1\) in \(k(x)\), i.e. \(f_x\notin\mathfrak m_x\). Then \(x\in X_f\). Also \(X_f\subseteq U\): for \(y\notin U\), \(f_y\in\mathcal J_y\subseteq\mathfrak m_y\). Since \(U\) is affine and \(X_f=D(f|_U)\) inside \(U\), \(X_f\) is affine.

Step 2: reduction to \(\sum f_iA=A\). Under our hypotheses, condition (3.2) of Subsection 2.3.1 holds, so \(\mathcal O_X(X_f)=A_f\) (Prop. 1.6 with \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X\)) and \(\phi^{-1}(D(f))=X_f\). If \(X_f\) is affine, then \(\phi|_{X_f}:X_f=\Spec A_f\to D(f)=\Spec A_f\) is an isomorphism. By Step 1 and quasi-compactness of \(X\) there are \(f_1,\dots,f_n\in A\) with \(X=\bigcup X_{f_i}\) and every \(X_{f_i}\) affine. If we show \(\sum_if_iA=A\), then \(\Spec A=\bigcup D(f_i)\) and \(\phi\) is an isomorphism over each \(D(f_i)\), so \(\phi\) is an isomorphism and \(X\) is affine.

Step 3: \(\sum f_iA=A\). Let \(\mathcal J_k\subseteq\mathcal O_X\) be the image of \(\mathcal O_X^k\to\mathcal O_X\), \((a_1,\dots,a_k)\mapsto\sum_{i\le k}f_ia_i\), i.e. the ideal sheaf generated by \(f_1,\dots,f_k\); it is quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.12(c)), and \(\mathcal J_0=0\). At each \(x\in X\) some \(f_i\) is a unit in \(\mathcal O_{X,x}\), so \(\mathcal J_n=\mathcal O_X\). We prove by induction on \(k\) that \(\mathcal J_k(X)=\sum_{i\le k}f_iA\). For \(k=0\) both sides are \(0\). Let \(k\ge1\). The quotient \(\mathcal J_k/\mathcal J_{k-1}\) is quasi-coherent, and the morphism \(\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal J_k/\mathcal J_{k-1}\), \(a\mapsto af_k\bmod\mathcal J_{k-1}\) is surjective. Its kernel \(\mathcal I'=(\mathcal J_{k-1}:f_k)\) is a quasi-coherent sheaf of ideals (Prop. 1.12(c)), so \(H^1(X,\mathcal I')=0\) by (iii\('\)). Prop. 2.15 for \(0\to\mathcal I'\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal J_k/\mathcal J_{k-1}\to0\) shows that \(A\to(\mathcal J_k/\mathcal J_{k-1})(X)\), \(a\mapsto af_k\bmod\mathcal J_{k-1}\), is surjective. Let \(s\in\mathcal J_k(X)\). Its image in \((\mathcal J_k/\mathcal J_{k-1})(X)\) equals that of \(af_k\) for some \(a\in A\). Hence \(s-af_k\) maps to \(0\), so lies in \(\mathcal J_{k-1}(X)\) (left exactness of sections, Ex. 2.2.4), and \(\mathcal J_{k-1}(X)=\sum_{i<k}f_iA\) by induction. So \(s\in\sum_{i\le k}f_iA\); the other inclusion is clear.

For \(k=n\) this gives \(A=\mathcal O_X(X)=\mathcal J_n(X)=\sum_if_iA\). Hence \(X\) is affine. So condition (iii) of Thm. 2.23 can be replaced by (iii\('\)). (If \(X\) is Noetherian, all the sheaves of ideals used above are coherent, so it is enough to ask \(H^1(X,\mathcal J)=0\) for coherent ideal sheaves \(\mathcal J\).) ∎

Exercise 2.10

Problem

Let us keep the notation and hypotheses of Exercise 1.16: \(f:X\to S\) is a morphism of schemes, \(\pi:T\to S\) is a flat morphism, \(X_T=X\times_ST\) with projections \(p:X_T\to X\) and \(f_T:X_T\to T\), \(\mathcal F\) is a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal O_X\)-module, and \(X\) is Noetherian or \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact. Show that we have a canonical isomorphism \(\pi^*(R^nf_*\mathcal F)\to R^nf_{T*}(p^*\mathcal F)\) for every \(n\ge0\).

Solution

Remark on the hypotheses. The sheaves \(R^nf_*\mathcal F\) are defined in Prop. 2.28 only when \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact. So we assume this. If \(X\) is merely Noetherian, \(f\) need not be separated and Prop. 2.28 does not define \(R^nf_*\) for \(n\ge1\); for \(n=0\) the statement is Ex. 1.16(b) and is covered by the case below only if \(f\) is separated. The base change \(f_T\) is again separated (Prop. 3.3.9(c)) and quasi-compact (stability of quasi-compactness under base change), and \(p^*\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.14(b)), so \(R^nf_{T*}(p^*\mathcal F)\) is defined.

Lemma 1. Let \(\rho:A\to B\) be a ring homomorphism and \(M\) a \(B\)-module such that, for every prime \(\mathfrak q\) of \(B\), the module \(M_{\mathfrak q}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\), where \(\mathfrak p=\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak q)\). Then \(M\) is flat over \(A\).

Proof. Let \(N'\to N\) be an injective homomorphism of \(A\)-modules, and let \(\kappa:M\otimes_AN'\to M\otimes_AN\) be the induced map; it is \(B\)-linear. For a prime \(\mathfrak q\) of \(B\) we have \((M\otimes_AN)_{\mathfrak q}=M_{\mathfrak q}\otimes_AN\). The elements of \(A\setminus\mathfrak p\) act invertibly on \(M_{\mathfrak q}\), so \(M_{\mathfrak q}\otimes_AN=M_{\mathfrak q}\otimes_{A_{\mathfrak p}}N_{\mathfrak p}\). Hence \(\kappa_{\mathfrak q}\) is \(M_{\mathfrak q}\otimes_{A_{\mathfrak p}}(N'_{\mathfrak p}\to N_{\mathfrak p})\), which is injective because \(M_{\mathfrak q}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\). So \((\Ker\kappa)_{\mathfrak q}=0\) for all \(\mathfrak q\), hence \(\Ker\kappa=0\). ∎

If \(V=\Spec A\) and \(W=\Spec B\) are affine and \(\varphi:W\to V\) is a flat morphism, then \(B\) is flat over \(A\): for a prime \(\mathfrak q\) of \(B\), \(B_{\mathfrak q}=\mathcal O_{W,w}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}=\mathcal O_{V,\varphi(w)}\) by definition of flatness, and Lemma 1 applies with \(M=B\).

Construction of the map. Let \(V=\Spec A\subseteq S\) and \(W=\Spec B\subseteq T\) be affine open subsets with \(\pi(W)\subseteq V\). Then \(B\) is flat over \(A\) (just shown). Put \(X_V=f^{-1}(V)\) and \(Z_W=f_T^{-1}(W)=X_V\times_VW\), so that \(p\) restricts to \(p_W:Z_W\to X_V\). The scheme \(X_V\) is separated and quasi-compact. Cor. 2.27 (applied to \(X_V\to\Spec A\), the flat \(A\)-algebra \(B\) and the sheaf \(\mathcal F|_{X_V}\)) gives an isomorphism

\[ \varphi_{V,W}:\ H^n(X_V,\mathcal F)\otimes_AB\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ H^n(Z_W,p^*\mathcal F). \]

Recall how it arises. Take a finite affine covering \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}\) of \(X_V\). Then \(\{p^{-1}(U_i)\}\) is an affine covering of \(Z_W\), all intersections \(U_{i_0\dots i_k}\) are affine (\(X_V\) is separated), and \((p^*\mathcal F)(p^{-1}U_\alpha)=\mathcal F(U_\alpha)\otimes_AB\) by Prop. 1.14(b). So the Cech complex of the new covering is \(C(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\otimes_AB\). Since \(B\) is flat, its cohomology is \(H^n(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\otimes_AB\). One concludes with Thm. 2.19 on \(X_V\) and on \(Z_W\). This map is built from restrictions of sections and tensor products with \(B\), so it commutes with refinement of coverings, and it is compatible with shrinking: if \(V''\subseteq V\) and \(W''\subseteq W\) are affine opens with \(\pi(W'')\subseteq V''\), the square formed by \(\varphi_{V,W}\), \(\varphi_{V'',W''}\) and the restriction maps \(H^n(X_V,\mathcal F)\otimes_AB\to H^n(X_{V''},\mathcal F)\otimes_{A''}B''\) and \(H^n(Z_W,p^*\mathcal F)\to H^n(Z_{W''},p^*\mathcal F)\) commutes (check it on Cech cochains for coverings of \(X_V\) and the restricted, then refined, coverings of \(X_{V''}\)).

Now \(R^nf_*\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent with \((R^nf_*\mathcal F)(V)=H^n(X_V,\mathcal F)\) (Prop. 2.28), so by Prop. 1.14(b)

\[ (\pi^*R^nf_*\mathcal F)|_W=\bigl(H^n(X_V,\mathcal F)\otimes_AB\bigr)^\sim . \]

On the other side, \(R^nf_{T*}(p^*\mathcal F)|_W\) is quasi-coherent on the affine scheme \(W\) with module of sections \(H^n(Z_W,p^*\mathcal F)\) (Prop. 2.28 and Thm. 1.7), so it equals \(H^n(Z_W,p^*\mathcal F)^\sim\). Let

\[ \Phi_W:(\pi^*R^nf_*\mathcal F)|_W\to R^nf_{T*}(p^*\mathcal F)|_W \]

be \(\varphi_{V,W}^\sim\); it is an isomorphism.

Gluing. Let \((V,W)\) and \((V',W')\) be two admissible pairs as above and \(t\in W\cap W'\). Choose an affine open \(V''\ni\pi(t)\) contained in \(V\cap V'\) and then an affine open \(W''\ni t\) contained in \(W\cap W'\cap\pi^{-1}(V'')\). By compatibility with shrinking, both \(\Phi_W\) and \(\Phi_{W'}\) restrict on \(W''\) to \(\Phi_{W''}\) (computed with \(V''\)). Hence \(\Phi_W\) and \(\Phi_{W'}\) agree near every point of \(W\cap W'\), so they agree on \(W\cap W'\). Since the admissible \(W\) cover \(T\), the \(\Phi_W\) glue to a homomorphism \(\Phi:\pi^*(R^nf_*\mathcal F)\to R^nf_{T*}(p^*\mathcal F)\). It is canonical (no choices were made) and it is an isomorphism because it is one on each \(W\). For \(n=0\) it is the homomorphism of Ex. 1.16(a), which is thereby shown to be an isomorphism under the stated hypotheses. ∎

Exercise 2.11

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism. Let \(\mathcal F\) be an \(\mathcal O_X\)-module, flat over \(Y\). (a) Show that for any affine open subset \(V\) of \(Y\) and any affine open subset \(U\) of \(f^{-1}(V)\), \(\mathcal F(U)\) is a flat \(\mathcal O_Y(V)\)-module (draw inspiration from the proof of Corollary 1.2.15). (b) Let us suppose that \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact, and that \(R^pf_*\mathcal F=0\) for every \(p\ge1\). Show that \(f_*\mathcal F\) is a flat \(\mathcal O_Y\)-module (use Exercise 1.2.15).

Solution

Hypothesis. In (a) the sheaf \(\mathcal F\) must be quasi-coherent: for a general \(\mathcal O_X\)-module the module \(\mathcal F(U)\) is not controlled by the stalks, and the argument below (as the argument of Cor. 1.2.15) uses \(\mathcal F|_U=\mathcal F(U)^\sim\). In (b) quasi-coherence is needed anyway for \(R^pf_*\mathcal F\) to be defined (Prop. 2.28). So \(\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent throughout.

(a) Let \(V=\Spec A\), \(U=\Spec B\subseteq f^{-1}(V)\), and let \(\rho:A\to B\) correspond to \(f|_U:U\to V\). Put \(M=\mathcal F(U)\), so \(\mathcal F|_U=M^\sim\). For a point \(x\in U\) corresponding to a prime \(\mathfrak q\) of \(B\), we have \(f(x)\leftrightarrow\mathfrak p=\rho^{-1}(\mathfrak q)\), \(\mathcal F_x=M_{\mathfrak q}\), \(\mathcal O_{Y,f(x)}=A_{\mathfrak p}\). By hypothesis \(M_{\mathfrak q}\) is flat over \(A_{\mathfrak p}\). Lemma 1 of Ex. 2.10 gives that \(M\) is flat over \(A\).

(b) Let \(V=\Spec A\) be an affine open subset of \(Y\). By Lemma 2.31(a) and the quasi-coherence of \(f_*\mathcal F\) (Prop. 1.14(c)), it suffices to show that \((f_*\mathcal F)(V)=\mathcal F(X_V)\) is flat over \(A\), where \(X_V=f^{-1}(V)\). If \(X_V=\emptyset\) there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, \(X_V\) is quasi-compact and separated; choose a finite affine covering \(\mathcal U=\{U_1,\dots,U_m\}\) of \(X_V\), totally ordered by the index. All \(U_{i_0\dots i_k}\) are affine (Prop. 3.3.6). Consider the complex of ordered cochains \(C^k=\bigoplus_{i_0<\dots<i_k}\mathcal F(U_{i_0\dots i_k})\), \(0\le k\le m-1\). By (a), each \(C^k\) is a finite direct sum of flat \(A\)-modules, hence flat. By Cor. 2.4, Prop. 2.6 and Thm. 2.19, \(H^k(C^\bullet)=H^k(X_V,\mathcal F)\), which is \(\mathcal F(X_V)\) for \(k=0\) and is \((R^kf_*\mathcal F)(V)=0\) for \(k\ge1\) (Prop. 2.28). So

\[ 0\to\mathcal F(X_V)\to C^0\to C^1\to\dots\to C^{m-1}\to0 \]

is exact.

We use Ex. 1.2.15 in the following form: if \(0\to M'\to M\to M''\to0\) is exact with \(M,M''\) flat, then \(M'\) is flat. (Indeed, for every module \(N\) the Tor sequence contains \(\Tor_2^A(M'',N)\to\Tor_1^A(M',N)\to\Tor_1^A(M,N)\) with both ends zero.) Let \(Z^k=\Ker(C^k\to C^{k+1})\). Then \(Z^{m-1}=C^{m-1}\) is flat, and for \(k<m-1\) exactness gives \(0\to Z^k\to C^k\to Z^{k+1}\to0\). By descending induction \(Z^k\) is flat for all \(k\), in particular \(\mathcal F(X_V)=Z^0\) is flat over \(A\). ∎

Exercise 2.12

Problem

Let \(S\) be a scheme and \(f:X\to S\) a morphism with \(X\) Noetherian or \(f\) separated and quasi-compact. We suppose that there exists a faithfully flat \(S\)-scheme \(T\) such that \(X_T\to T\) is affine. We want to show that \(f\) is an affine morphism. (a) Let us suppose \(S\) is affine. Let us fix a quasi-coherent sheaf \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\). Show that for any \(s\in S\), we have \(H^1(X,\mathcal F)\otimes_{\mathcal O_S(S)}\mathcal O_{S,s}=0\) (use Corollary 2.27). (b) Deduce from this that \(X\) is affine if \(S\) is affine (use Lemma 1.2.12). Conclude.

Solution

Here “faithfully flat” means that the structure morphism \(\pi:T\to S\) is flat and surjective; \(X_T=X\times_ST\) and \(f_T:X_T\to T\) is the projection.

Two lemmas. We prove two lemmas, also used in Ex. 2.13 and 2.15.

Lemma A (descent of separatedness). Let \(g:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes with \(X\) Noetherian, let \(q:Y'\to Y\) be flat and surjective, and let \(g':X'=X\times_YY'\to Y'\) be the base change. If \(g'\) is separated, then \(g\) is separated (and quasi-compact, as \(X\) is Noetherian).

Proof. Separatedness is local on \(Y\) (Def. 3.2), and the hypotheses pass to \(g^{-1}(V)\to V\) and \(q^{-1}(V)\to V\) for \(V\subseteq Y\) an affine open. So we may assume \(Y\) affine. Put \(W=X\times_YX\), with diagonal \(\Delta:X\to W\), and \(W'=W\times_YY'=X'\times_{Y'}X'\) with the projection \(q_W:W'\to W\), which is flat and surjective (base change of \(q\)). The diagonal \(\Delta'\) of \(g'\) is the base change of \(\Delta\) along \(q_W\), so \(\Delta'(X')=q_W^{-1}(\Delta(X))\) (points of fibre products, Ex. 3.1.7). By assumption \(\Delta'\) is a closed immersion, so \(q_W^{-1}(\Delta(X))\) is closed. By Cor. 3.3.5 (a morphism whose diagonal has closed image is separated) we must show that \(\Delta(X)\) is closed in \(W\).

Cover \(X\) by finitely many affine opens \(X_1,\dots,X_m\). As \(Y\) is affine, the opens \(W_{ab}=X_a\times_YX_b\) are affine and cover \(W\), and \(\Delta(X)\cap W_{ab}=\Delta(X_a\cap X_b)=:E\). The scheme \(X_a\cap X_b\) is Noetherian, hence a finite union of affine opens \(N_c=\Spec C_c\). Write \(W_{ab}=\Spec R\). It suffices to show that \(E\) is closed in \(W_{ab}\).

Claim: every point \(z\) of the closure \(\overline E\) of \(E\) in \(W_{ab}\) is a specialization of a point of \(E\). Indeed, \(\overline E=\bigcup_c\overline{\Delta(N_c)}\) (finite union). The restriction \(N_c\to W_{ab}\) corresponds to a ring homomorphism \(\theta:R\to C_c\); the closure of its image is \(V(\Ker\theta)\). Let \(P\in V(\Ker\theta)\), put \(R'=R/\Ker\theta\subseteq C_c\), and let \(P'\) be a minimal prime of \(R'\) contained in the image of \(P\). Then \(R'_{P'}\) is local with the unique prime \(P'R'_{P'}\), and \(R'_{P'}\to(C_c)_{P'}\) is injective with \((C_c)_{P'}\ne0\). Any prime of \((C_c)_{P'}\) contracts to the unique prime of \(R'_{P'}\), so \(P'\) lies in the image of \(\Spec C_c\). Thus \(P\) is a specialization of a point of \(\Delta(N_c)\). This proves the claim.

Now let \(z\in\overline E\) and \(e\in E\) a generization of \(z\). Choose \(z'\in W'\) over \(z\). The local homomorphism \(\mathcal O_{W,z}\to\mathcal O_{W',z'}\) is flat, hence faithfully flat (Prop. 1.2.17: it is flat and \(\mathfrak m_z\mathcal O_{W',z'}\ne\mathcal O_{W',z'}\)), so \(\Spec\mathcal O_{W',z'}\to\Spec\mathcal O_{W,z}\) is surjective. The points of these spectra are the generizations of \(z'\), \(z\). So there is a generization \(e'\) of \(z'\) with \(q_W(e')=e\). Then \(e'\in q_W^{-1}(\Delta(X))=\Delta'(X')\), which is closed, so \(z'\in\overline{\{e'\}}\subseteq q_W^{-1}(\Delta(X))\). Hence \(z=q_W(z')\in\Delta(X)\cap W_{ab}=E\). So \(E\) is closed. ∎

Lemma B. Let \(\Spec C=Y\) be affine, \(q:Y'\to Y\) flat, \(y\in Y\), \(t\in Y'\) a point over \(y\), and \(W=\Spec B\ni t\) an affine open subset of \(Y'\) (so \(W\to Y\) gives \(C\to B\), which is flat). If \(N\) is a \(C\)-module with \(N\otimes_CB=0\), then \(N\otimes_C\mathcal O_{Y,y}=0\).

Proof. \(\mathcal O_{Y',t}=B_{\mathfrak q}\) is a localization of \(B\), so \(N\otimes_C\mathcal O_{Y',t}=(N\otimes_CB)\otimes_BB_{\mathfrak q}=0\). Now \(\mathcal O_{Y,y}\to\mathcal O_{Y',t}\) is a flat local homomorphism, hence faithfully flat (Prop. 1.2.17), and \(N\otimes_C\mathcal O_{Y',t}=(N\otimes_C\mathcal O_{Y,y})\otimes_{\mathcal O_{Y,y}}\mathcal O_{Y',t}\). Faithful flatness gives \(N\otimes_C\mathcal O_{Y,y}=0\). ∎

Reduction to \(f\) separated and quasi-compact. The morphism \(f_T\) is affine, hence separated (Prop. 3.3.4), and it is the base change of \(f\) along the flat surjective \(\pi\). If \(X\) is Noetherian, Lemma A shows that \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact. So in all cases \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact.

(a) Let \(S=\Spec A\), \(s\in S\), \(M=H^1(X,\mathcal F)\). Since \(\pi\) is surjective, choose \(t\in T\) with \(\pi(t)=s\) and an affine open \(W=\Spec B\ni t\) of \(T\). Then \(W\to S\) is flat, so \(B\) is flat over \(A\) (Ex. 2.10). The scheme \(X_W=f_T^{-1}(W)=X\times_SW\) is affine, as \(f_T\) is affine. Cor. 2.27 (for \(f:X\to\Spec A\) and the flat \(A\)-algebra \(B\)) and Thm. 2.18 give

\[ M\otimes_AB\simeq H^1(X_W,p^*\mathcal F)=0. \]

Lemma B gives \(M\otimes_A\mathcal O_{S,s}=0\).

(b) Let \(S=\Spec A\). By (a) all localizations of the \(A\)-module \(H^1(X,\mathcal F)\) at primes of \(A\) vanish, so \(H^1(X,\mathcal F)=0\) (Lemma 1.2.12). This holds for every quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\), and \(X\) is Noetherian or separated and quasi-compact (see above), so \(X\) is affine by Serre's criterion (Thm. 2.23).

Conclusion. Let \(S\) be arbitrary and \(V\subseteq S\) an affine open subset. Then \(\pi^{-1}(V)\to V\) is flat and surjective, \((f^{-1}(V))_{\pi^{-1}(V)}\to\pi^{-1}(V)\) is the restriction of \(f_T\) over \(\pi^{-1}(V)\), hence affine, and \(f^{-1}(V)\) is Noetherian (open in \(X\)) or \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\) is separated and quasi-compact. By (b), \(f^{-1}(V)\) is affine. Since \(V\) was an arbitrary affine open, \(f\) is affine. ∎

Exercise 2.13

Problem

Let \(S\) be a scheme and \(g:X\to Y\) a morphism of \(S\)-schemes such that \(X\) is Noetherian or \(g\) is separated and quasi-compact. We suppose that there exists a faithfully flat \(S\)-scheme \(T\) such that \(g_T\) is an isomorphism. We want to show that \(g\) is an isomorphism. (a) Show that we can reduce to \(S\) and \(Y\) affine. (b) Show that under the hypotheses of (a), we have \(X\) affine and \(g\) is an isomorphism (use Lemma 1.2.12).

Solution

We use Lemma A and Lemma B from Ex. 2.12 and Ex. 2.10 (Lemma 1 there for flatness of \(B\) over \(A\)).

(a) Being an isomorphism is local on \(Y\). Let \(V\subseteq Y\) be an affine open subset mapping into an affine open subset \(S_0\) of \(S\). Then \(g^{-1}(V)\to V\) is a morphism of \(S_0\)-schemes, \(T_0=T\times_SS_0\to S_0\) is faithfully flat, and the base change of \(g^{-1}(V)\to V\) to \(T_0\) is the restriction of the isomorphism \(g_T\) to \(g_T^{-1}\) of \(V\times_ST_0=V_T\), hence an isomorphism. The hypothesis on \(X\) or \(g\) passes to \(g^{-1}(V)\to V\) (\(g^{-1}(V)\) is open in \(X\); separatedness and quasi-compactness restrict). So it suffices to prove the claim for \(g^{-1}(V)\to V\), i.e. we may assume \(S\) and \(Y\) affine.

(b) Let \(Y=\Spec C\). Put \(Y'=Y_T=Y\times_ST\), with \(q:Y'\to Y\) flat and surjective (base change of \(T\to S\)), and note \(X\times_YY'=X_T\), so that \(g_T\) is the base change of \(g\) along \(q\). By assumption \(g_T\) is an isomorphism, hence separated. By Lemma A (if \(X\) is Noetherian) \(g\) is separated and quasi-compact; in the other case this is the hypothesis.

\(X\) is affine. Let \(y\in Y\). Since \(q\) is surjective choose \(t\in Y'\) over \(y\) and an affine open \(W=\Spec B\ni t\) of \(Y'\); \(C\to B\) is flat. Then \(X\times_YW=g_T^{-1}(W)\to W\) is an isomorphism, so \(X_W:=X\times_YW\) is affine. For a quasi-coherent \(\mathcal F\) on \(X\), Cor. 2.27 and Thm. 2.18 give \(H^1(X,\mathcal F)\otimes_CB\simeq H^1(X_W,\mathcal F_W)=0\), hence \(H^1(X,\mathcal F)\otimes_C\mathcal O_{Y,y}=0\) by Lemma B. As \(y\) was arbitrary, \(H^1(X,\mathcal F)=0\) (Lemma 1.2.12). By Serre's criterion (Thm. 2.23; \(X\) is Noetherian, or separated and quasi-compact), \(X\) is affine, say \(X=\Spec D\), and \(g\) corresponds to a ring homomorphism \(\varphi:C\to D\).

\(\varphi\) is an isomorphism. With \(y,t,W\) as above, \(X_W=\Spec(D\otimes_CB)\) and \(g_W\) is the isomorphism \(\Spec(D\otimes_CB)\to\Spec B\), i.e. \(B\to D\otimes_CB\), \(b\mapsto1\otimes b\), is an isomorphism. Let \(K=\Ker\varphi\), \(Q=\Coker\varphi\). Tensoring \(0\to K\to C\to D\to Q\to0\) with the flat \(C\)-module \(B\) gives the exact sequence \(0\to K\otimes_CB\to B\to D\otimes_CB\to Q\otimes_CB\to0\) whose middle map is an isomorphism. So \(K\otimes_CB=0=Q\otimes_CB\), and Lemma B gives \(K_y=0=Q_y\) for every \(y\in Y\). Hence \(K=Q=0\) (Lemma 1.2.12), so \(\varphi\) is an isomorphism and \(g\) is an isomorphism. ∎

Exercise 2.14

Problem

Let \(f:X\dashrightarrow Y\) be a birational map as in Exercise 4.3.15 but without the \(Y\) affine hypothesis. Using Exercises 2.13 and 3.3.14, show that \(f\) is defined everywhere.

(Exercise 4.3.15: \(X,Y\) are integral schemes, separated and of finite type over a locally Noetherian scheme \(S\); \(f:X\dashrightarrow Y\) is a birational map; \(T\to S\) is a faithfully flat morphism such that \(X_T\) and \(Y_T\) are integral; \(f_T:X_T\dashrightarrow Y_T\) is defined everywhere.)

Solution

Set-up. The map \(f\) is given by an isomorphism of \(S\)-schemes \(u:U\to V\) between non-empty (dense) open subschemes of \(X\) and \(Y\). Then \(f_T\) is given by the isomorphism \(u_T:U_T\to V_T\). Here \(U_T\) is non-empty (\(U_T\to U\) is surjective) and open in the integral scheme \(X_T\), hence integral and dense. That \(f_T\) is defined everywhere means: there is a \(T\)-morphism \(F:X_T\to Y_T\) agreeing with \(u_T\) on a dense open subset of \(U_T\). As \(U_T\) is reduced and \(Y_T\) is separated over \(T\), \(F|_{U_T}=u_T\) (Prop. 3.3.11).

Let \(W=X\times_SY\), \(p:W\to X\), \(q_Y:W\to Y\) the projections. As \(Y/S\) is separated, \(i=(\operatorname{Id}_U,u):U\to W\) is a closed immersion into the open subscheme \(p^{-1}(U)=U\times_SY\). Its image is the graph \(\Gamma_U\). By definition (Ex. 3.3.13(c)), \(\Gamma_f\) is the closure of \(\Gamma_U\) in \(W\) with the reduced structure, and \(g=p|_{\Gamma_f}:\Gamma_f\to X\). By Ex. 3.3.14 (the proof of which uses only that \(Y/S\) is separated), \(f\) is defined everywhere iff \(g\) is an isomorphism. For completeness we give the direction we need: if \(g\) is an isomorphism then \(\hat f=q_Y\circ g^{-1}:X\to Y\) is an \(S\)-morphism; and \(\Gamma_f\cap p^{-1}(U)=\Gamma_U\) (it is closed in \(p^{-1}(U)\)), so \(g^{-1}|_U=i\) and \(\hat f|_U=q_Y\circ i=u\). Thus \(\hat f\) extends \(u\) and \(f\) is defined everywhere.

So it remains to show that \(g\) is an isomorphism. We use Ex. 2.13 for the \(S\)-morphism \(g:\Gamma_f\to X\) and the faithfully flat \(S\)-scheme \(T\).

Hypotheses of Ex. 2.13. \(g\) is the composite of the closed immersion \(\Gamma_f\to W\) and \(p\). The projection \(p\) is separated (base change of \(Y/S\)) and of finite type (base change of \(Y/S\) of finite type). Hence \(g\) is separated and of finite type, so quasi-compact.

The base change of \(g\). The schemes \(X,Y,W\) are of finite type over the locally Noetherian \(S\), hence locally Noetherian, so every open immersion into them is quasi-compact. Therefore \(i\) is a quasi-compact and separated morphism. Put \(\mathcal I=\Ker(\mathcal O_W\to i_*\mathcal O_U)\). A local section \(a\) of \(\mathcal O_W\) lies in \(\mathcal I\) iff \(i^\#(a)=0\) in \(\mathcal O_U\). Since \(U\) is reduced, this holds iff \(a\) vanishes (in the residue fields) at all points of \(i(U)\), i.e. iff \(a\) vanishes at all points of the closure of \(i(U)\), since the vanishing locus of a section is closed. So \(\mathcal I\) is the ideal sheaf of the reduced closed subscheme \(\Gamma_f\).

Let \(q_W:W_T=X_T\times_TY_T\to W\) be the projection; it is flat. Then \((\Gamma_f)_T=\Gamma_f\times_WW_T\) is the closed subscheme of \(W_T\) with ideal sheaf \(q_W^*\mathcal I\subseteq\mathcal O_{W_T}\) (flatness). Moreover \(q_W^*(i_*\mathcal O_U)=(i_T)_*\mathcal O_{U_T}\), where \(i_T=(\operatorname{Id},u_T):U_T\to W_T\) is the base change of \(i\): this is Ex. 1.16(b) for the quasi-compact separated morphism \(i\) and the quasi-coherent sheaf \(\mathcal O_U\), which on an affine open \(\Spec R\subseteq W\) and an affine open \(\Spec R'\) over it amounts to \(\mathcal O(i^{-1}\Spec R)\otimes_RR'=\mathcal O(i_T^{-1}\Spec R')\) (Cor. 2.27, case \(p=0\)). By flatness of \(q_W\) the kernel is preserved, so

\[ q_W^*\mathcal I=\Ker\bigl(\mathcal O_{W_T}\to(i_T)_*\mathcal O_{U_T}\bigr). \]

Since \(U_T\) is reduced, the same argument as above shows that this is the ideal of the reduced closed subscheme of \(W_T\) supported on the closure of \(i_T(U_T)\). Now \((\operatorname{Id},F):X_T\to W_T\) is a closed immersion (\(Y_T/T\) separated) with image \(\Gamma_F\), the first projection restricts to an inverse isomorphism \(\Gamma_F\to X_T\), and \(\Gamma_F\) is reduced (\(\simeq X_T\)). It contains \(i_T(U_T)\) (as \(F|_{U_T}=u_T\)) as a dense subset (\(U_T\) is dense in \(X_T\)). Hence \(\Gamma_F\) is the reduced subscheme on the closure of \(i_T(U_T)\), and so \((\Gamma_f)_T=\Gamma_F\). Under this identification \(g_T:(\Gamma_f)_T\to X_T\) is the first projection \(\Gamma_F\to X_T\), an isomorphism.

Conclusion. By Ex. 2.13, \(g\) is an isomorphism, hence \(f\) is defined everywhere by the above. ∎

Exercise 2.15

Problem

Let \(S\) be a locally Noetherian scheme, \(f:X\to S\) a proper morphism. (a) Let us suppose that \(S=\Spec A\), with \(A\) a complete local ring. Let \(s\) be the closed point of \(S\). Show that if \(X_s\) is finite, then \(X\) is finite over \(S\) (use Lemma 4.4.1). (b) Show that (a) is true without the \(A\) complete hypothesis (use Exercise 2.13 and Lemma 3.3.17). (c) Let us suppose that \(X_s\) is finite for every \(s\in S\). Show that \(f\) is a finite morphism (compare with Exercise 4.4.2).

Solution

“\(X_s\) is finite” means that \(X_s\to\Spec k(s)\) is a finite morphism, i.e. \(X_s=\Spec\) of a finite \(k(s)\)-algebra; its underlying space is finite and discrete. (Since \(X_s\) is of finite type over \(k(s)\), this is equivalent to \(X_s\) having finitely many points.) Recall that a proper morphism is closed and universally so, and that a morphism which is finite over an affine open covering of the base is finite (Ex. 3.3.15).

(a) Here \(A\) is a Noetherian complete local ring. Let \(X_s=\{x_1,\dots,x_n\}\) (possibly \(n=0\)). Because \(X_s\) is discrete, there is an open subset \(X_i\subseteq X\) with \(X_i\cap X_s=\{x_i\}\), and \(X_i\) is of finite type over \(S\). By Lemma 4.4.1 there is an affine open neighbourhood \(U_i\subseteq X_i\) of \(x_i\) finite over \(S\). Then \(U_i\to S\) is proper (Ex. 3.3.17(d)), so \(U_i\to X\) is proper (Prop. 3.3.16(e); \(X\to S\) is separated), in particular closed. As \(U_i\) is also open, \(U_i\) is open and closed in \(X\). Put \(U=\bigcup_iU_i\), open and closed. The set \(f(X\setminus U)\) is closed (\(f\) is proper). If \(X\setminus U\ne\emptyset\), this closed non-empty subset of the spectrum of a local ring contains the closed point \(s\), so \(X\setminus U\) meets \(X_s\subseteq U\), absurd. So \(X=U\).

Put \(V_1=U_1\) and \(V_k=U_k\setminus(U_1\cup\dots\cup U_{k-1})\). These are open and closed in \(X\), pairwise disjoint, and cover \(X\). \(V_k\) is a clopen subscheme of the affine scheme \(U_k=\Spec C_k\), so \(V_k=\Spec C'_k\) with \(C_k=C'_k\times C''_k\) (idempotent); \(C'_k\) is a quotient of \(C_k\), hence finite over \(A\). Therefore \(X=\bigsqcup_kV_k=\Spec(\prod_kC'_k)\) is affine and finite over \(A\). By Ex. 3.3.15, \(f\) is finite.

(b) Now \(A\) is a Noetherian local ring with closed point \(s\). Let \(\widehat A\) be its completion, \(T=\Spec\widehat A\), \(\pi:T\to S\). By Thm. 1.3.16, \(\widehat A\) is flat over \(A\), and \(\widehat A/\mathfrak m\widehat A=A/\mathfrak m\ne0\), so \(A\to\widehat A\) is faithfully flat (Prop. 1.2.17) and \(\pi\) is flat and surjective. The morphism \(f_T:X_T\to T\) is proper, \(T\) is the spectrum of a complete local ring, and its closed fibre is \(X_s\otimes_{k(s)}k(s')=X_s\) (the residue field of \(\widehat A\) is \(k(s)\)), which is finite. By (a), \(f_T\) is finite; in particular affine. By Ex. 2.12 (here \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact) \(f\) is affine, so \(X\) is affine, and it is proper over \(\Spec A\). Lemma 3.3.17 shows that \(\mathcal O_X(X)\) is finite over \(A\). So \(f\) is finite. (One may use Ex. 2.13 instead: it applies to \(g:X\to\Spec\mathcal O_X(X)\), using that \(X_T\) is affine and \(\mathcal O(X_T)=\mathcal O_X(X)\otimes_A\widehat A\) by Cor. 2.27 with \(p=0\); the route via Ex. 2.12 is shorter.)

(c) Let \(T=\bigsqcup_{s\in S}\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\), with \(\pi:T\to S\) the canonical morphism. Each \(\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\to S\) is flat, and \(\pi\) is surjective, so \(T\) is a faithfully flat \(S\)-scheme. For \(s\in S\), the component of \(f_T\) over \(\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\) is the base change \(X\times_S\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\to\Spec\mathcal O_{S,s}\). It is proper, \(\mathcal O_{S,s}\) is a Noetherian local ring, and its closed fibre is \(X_s\), finite. By (b) it is finite, in particular affine. A morphism whose restriction over each member of a disjoint open covering of the base is affine is affine, so \(f_T\) is affine. By Ex. 2.12 \(f\) is affine. For an affine open \(V=\Spec A\subseteq S\), \(f^{-1}(V)=\Spec B\) is proper over \(\Spec A\), so \(B\) is finite over \(A\) (Lemma 3.3.17). Hence \(f\) is finite. ∎

Exercise 2.16

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be an affine morphism. (a) Let \(\mathcal F\) be a quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). Show that the canonical homomorphism \(f^*f_*\mathcal F\to\mathcal F\) (Exercise 1.1) is surjective. (b) Let us suppose \(Y\) is quasi-compact and \(f\) finite. Let \(\mathcal L\) be an ample sheaf on \(Y\). Show that \(f^*\mathcal L\) is ample on \(X\) (use Propositions 1.14(d) and 2.32).

Solution

(a) The sheaf \(f_*\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent (Prop. 1.14(c); \(f\) is affine, hence separated and quasi-compact). The canonical homomorphism \(f^*f_*\mathcal F=f^{-1}f_*\mathcal F\otimes_{f^{-1}\mathcal O_Y}\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal F\) is induced by \(s\otimes a\mapsto as\). The question is local on \(Y\), so let \(V=\Spec A\) be an affine open subset of \(Y\). Then \(f^{-1}(V)=\Spec B\) is affine. Put \(M=\mathcal F(f^{-1}V)\), a \(B\)-module with \(\mathcal F|_{f^{-1}V}=M^\sim\); as an \(A\)-module it gives \((f_*\mathcal F)|_V=M^\sim\) (Thm. 1.7). By Prop. 1.14(b), \((f^*f_*\mathcal F)|_{f^{-1}V}=(M\otimes_AB)^\sim\), and the canonical homomorphism is \((M\otimes_AB\to M,\ m\otimes b\mapsto bm)^\sim\). This map of modules is surjective, since \(m\otimes1\mapsto m\). Hence the sheaf homomorphism is surjective over each \(f^{-1}(V)\), so everywhere.

(b) As \(Y\) is quasi-compact, it is a finite union of affine opens \(Y_a\), and \(f^{-1}(Y_a)\) is affine; so \(X\) is quasi-compact. The sheaf \(f^*\mathcal L\) is invertible. Let \(\mathcal F\) be a finitely generated quasi-coherent sheaf on \(X\). By Prop. 1.14(d), \(f_*\mathcal F\) is quasi-coherent and finitely generated. Since \(\mathcal L\) is ample, there is \(n_0\) with \(f_*\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^{\otimes n}\) generated by global sections for all \(n\ge n_0\). Fix such an \(n\) and put \(\mathcal G=\mathcal F\otimes(f^*\mathcal L)^{\otimes n}=\mathcal F\otimes f^*(\mathcal L^{\otimes n})\), a quasi-coherent sheaf. The projection formula (Prop. 2.32 with \(p=0\); \(\mathcal L^{\otimes n}\) is locally free, hence flat, and \(f\) is separated and quasi-compact) gives

\[ f_*\mathcal G\simeq f_*\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal L^{\otimes n}. \]

So \(f_*\mathcal G\) is generated by global sections: there are \(s_i\in(f_*\mathcal G)(Y)=\mathcal G(X)\), \(i\in I\), such that \(\mathcal O_Y^{(I)}\to f_*\mathcal G\), \(e_i\mapsto s_i\), is surjective. Applying \(f^*\), which is right exact, \(\mathcal O_X^{(I)}=f^*\mathcal O_Y^{(I)}\to f^*f_*\mathcal G\) is surjective.
