Composing with the surjection \(f^*f_*\mathcal G\to\mathcal G\) of (a), we get a surjection \(\mathcal O_X^{(I)}\to\mathcal G\) that sends the \(i\)th basis vector to \(s_i\in\mathcal G(X)\). So \(\mathcal G=\mathcal F\otimes(f^*\mathcal L)^{\otimes n}\) is generated by global sections for all \(n\ge n_0\). Hence \(f^*\mathcal L\) is ample (Def. 1.33). ∎

Exercise 2.17

Problem

Let \((X,\mathcal O_X)\) be a ringed topological space, and let \(\mathcal F\), \(\mathcal G\) be two \(\mathcal O_X\)-modules. We are going to define an operation called the cup product \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)\times H^q(X,\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(X,\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal G)\) for any \(p,q\ge0\). Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_i\), \(\mathcal V=\{V_j\}_j\) be two coverings of \(X\). Let \(\mathcal W=\{U_i\cap V_j\}_{(i,j)}\) be the covering \(\mathcal U\cap\mathcal V\) of \(X\). We will denote the index \((i,j)\) by \(\alpha\). (a) Let \(f\in C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\), \(g\in C^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\), and set \(f\smile g\in C^{p+q}(\mathcal W,\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal G)\) equal to the cochain defined by \((f\smile g)_{\alpha_0\dots\alpha_{p+q}}=f_{i_0\dots i_p}|_{W_\alpha}\otimes g_{j_p\dots j_{p+q}}|_{W_\alpha}\), \(W_\alpha=U_i\cap V_j\). Show that \(d(f\smile g)=df\smile g+(-1)^p(f\smile dg)\). (b) Show that the operation \(\smile\) in (a) induces a homomorphism \(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\times H^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(\mathcal W,\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal G)\). By taking the direct limit, we thus obtain a homomorphism \(\smile:H^p(X,\mathcal F)\times H^q(X,\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(X,\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal G)\) called the cup product. (c) Show that the cup product is associative in an evident sense. We can show that it is anti-commutative, that is to say that \(\xi\smile\eta=(-1)^{pq}\eta\smile\xi\) if \(\xi\in H^p(X,\mathcal F)\), \(\eta\in H^q(X,\mathcal G)\), and if we identify \(\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal G\) with \(\mathcal G\otimes_{\mathcal O_X}\mathcal F\) (see [40], Section II.6.6).

Solution

Conventions. Cochains are the (not necessarily alternating) cochains \(C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)=\prod_{(i_0,\dots,i_p)}\mathcal F(U_{i_0\dots i_p})\) with the differential of the text. For \(\alpha_k=(i_k,j_k)\) and \(n=p+q\) one has \(W_{\alpha_0\dots\alpha_n}=W_{\alpha_0}\cap\dots\cap W_{\alpha_n}=U_{i_0\dots i_n}\cap V_{j_0\dots j_n}\), and both \(f_{i_0\dots i_p}\) and \(g_{j_p\dots j_n}\) are restricted to this open set. Here \(a\otimes b\in(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)(W)\) for \(a\in\mathcal F(W)\), \(b\in\mathcal G(W)\) denotes the image of \(a\otimes b\) under the canonical map \(\mathcal F(W)\otimes_{\mathcal O_X(W)}\mathcal G(W)\to(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)(W)\). This construction is bilinear and compatible with restriction to smaller open sets. Observe also that \(\smile\) is biadditive.

(a) Write \(n=p+q\) and \(h=f\smile g\). Fix \(\alpha_0,\dots,\alpha_{n+1}\) with \(\alpha_k=(i_k,j_k)\); all sections below are restricted to \(W_{\alpha_0\dots\alpha_{n+1}}\). By definition \((dh)_{\alpha_0\dots\alpha_{n+1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}(-1)^kh_{\alpha_0\dots\hat\alpha_k\dots\alpha_{n+1}}\). Consider the omitted index \(k\).

If \(k\le p\), the first \(p+1\) entries of the sequence \((\alpha_0,\dots,\hat\alpha_k,\dots,\alpha_{n+1})\) are \(\alpha_0,\dots,\hat\alpha_k,\dots,\alpha_{p+1}\) and the entries from the \((p+1)\)-th on are \(\alpha_{p+1},\dots,\alpha_{n+1}\). So the term is \(A_k=f_{i_0\dots\hat\imath_k\dots i_{p+1}}\otimes g_{j_{p+1}\dots j_{n+1}}\).

If \(k\ge p+1\), the first \(p+1\) entries are \(\alpha_0,\dots,\alpha_p\) and the entries from the \((p+1)\)-th on are \(\alpha_p,\dots,\hat\alpha_k,\dots,\alpha_{n+1}\). So the term is \(B_k=f_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes g_{j_p\dots\hat\jmath_k\dots j_{n+1}}\).

Thus \(dh=\sum_{k=0}^{p}(-1)^kA_k+\sum_{k=p+1}^{n+1}(-1)^kB_k\).

On the other hand,

\[ (df\smile g)_{\alpha_0\dots\alpha_{n+1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{p+1}(-1)^kf_{i_0\dots\hat\imath_k\dots i_{p+1}}\otimes g_{j_{p+1}\dots j_{n+1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{p}(-1)^kA_k+(-1)^{p+1}f_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes g_{j_{p+1}\dots j_{n+1}}, \]

and, with \(k=p+m\),

\[ (-1)^p(f\smile dg)_{\alpha_0\dots\alpha_{n+1}}=(-1)^p\sum_{m=0}^{q+1}(-1)^mf_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes g_{j_p\dots\hat\jmath_{p+m}\dots j_{n+1}}=(-1)^pf_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes g_{j_{p+1}\dots j_{n+1}}+\sum_{k=p+1}^{n+1}(-1)^kB_k. \]

Adding these two expressions, the two terms \(\pm f_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes g_{j_{p+1}\dots j_{n+1}}\) cancel and we obtain \(dh\). So \(d(f\smile g)=df\smile g+(-1)^p(f\smile dg)\).

(b) Let \(f\in C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\) and \(g\in C^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\) be cocycles, \(df=0=dg\). By (a), \(d(f\smile g)=0\). If \(f=d\varphi\) with \(\varphi\in C^{p-1}(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\) and \(dg=0\), then (a) gives \(d(\varphi\smile g)=d\varphi\smile g+(-1)^{p-1}\varphi\smile dg=f\smile g\). If \(g=d\psi\) with \(\psi\in C^{q-1}(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\) and \(df=0\), then \(d(f\smile\psi)=df\smile\psi+(-1)^pf\smile d\psi=(-1)^pf\smile g\). So the cup product of a cocycle with a coboundary is a coboundary. Since \(\smile\) is biadditive, it induces a biadditive (indeed \(\mathcal O_X(X)\)-bilinear) map \(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\times H^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(\mathcal W,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)\).

Passage to the limit. Let \(\mathcal U'=\{U'_a\}_{a\in I'}\) refine \(\mathcal U\) via \(\sigma:I'\to I\) and \(\mathcal V'=\{V'_b\}_{b\in J'}\) refine \(\mathcal V\) via \(\tau:J'\to J\). Then \(\mathcal W'=\mathcal U'\cap\mathcal V'\) refines \(\mathcal W\) via \((\sigma,\tau):(a,b)\mapsto(\sigma(a),\tau(b))\), because \(U'_a\cap V'_b\subseteq U_{\sigma(a)}\cap V_{\tau(b)}\). For cochains one checks directly from the definitions that

\[ \sigma(f)\smile\tau(g)=(\sigma,\tau)(f\smile g): \]

the component at \(((a_0,b_0),\dots,(a_n,b_n))\) of the left side is \(f_{\sigma(a_0)\dots\sigma(a_p)}|\otimes g_{\tau(b_p)\dots\tau(b_n)}|\), and that of the right side is \((f\smile g)_{(\sigma a_0,\tau b_0)\dots(\sigma a_n,\tau b_n)}|\), which is the same element. Hence the cup product of classes commutes with the refinement maps (which on cohomology do not depend on the choice of the refinement map, Lemma 2.8). Two coverings always have a common refinement, so the pairs \((\mathcal U,\mathcal V)\) form a directed set, and

\[ \varinjlim_{(\mathcal U,\mathcal V)}\bigl(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\times H^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\bigr)=H^p(X,\mathcal F)\times H^q(X,\mathcal G). \]

(Indeed, the directed set of coverings is cofinal in the product directed set via the diagonal \(\mathcal U\mapsto(\mathcal U,\mathcal U)\) up to refinement, and a limit of products over the product directed set is the product of the limits.) The maps \(H^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\times H^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(\mathcal U\cap\mathcal V,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)\) are compatible with the transition maps by what we have just shown. So they define in the limit the cup product \(H^p(X,\mathcal F)\times H^q(X,\mathcal G)\to H^{p+q}(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)\): for \(\xi=[f]\), \(\eta=[g]\), we have \(\xi\smile\eta=[f\smile g]\).

(c) Associativity. Let \(\mathcal H\) be a third \(\mathcal O_X\)-module, \(\mathcal U,\mathcal V,\mathcal W\) coverings indexed by \(I,J,K\), and \(f\in C^p(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\), \(g\in C^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\), \(h\in C^r(\mathcal W,\mathcal H)\) cochains; put \(n=p+q+r\). The coverings \((\mathcal U\cap\mathcal V)\cap\mathcal W\) and \(\mathcal U\cap(\mathcal V\cap\mathcal W)\) are both the family \(\{U_i\cap V_j\cap W_k\}\) indexed, respectively, by \((I\times J)\times K\) and \(I\times(J\times K)\); we identify both index sets with \(I\times J\times K\) (a relabelling of the index set, under which the Cech complexes correspond to each other). For indices \(\gamma_m=(i_m,j_m,k_m)\), \(0\le m\le n\), and with all sections restricted to the open set \(U_{i_0\dots i_n}\cap V_{j_0\dots j_n}\cap W_{k_0\dots k_n}\),

\[ \bigl((f\smile g)\smile h\bigr)_{\gamma_0\dots\gamma_n}=(f_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes g_{j_p\dots j_{p+q}})\otimes h_{k_{p+q}\dots k_n}, \]
\[ \bigl(f\smile(g\smile h)\bigr)_{\gamma_0\dots\gamma_n}=f_{i_0\dots i_p}\otimes(g_{j_p\dots j_{p+q}}\otimes h_{k_{p+q}\dots k_n}). \]

Let \(\operatorname{as}:(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G)\otimes\mathcal H\to\mathcal F\otimes(\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal H)\) be the canonical associativity isomorphism of sheaves, \((a\otimes b)\otimes c\mapsto a\otimes(b\otimes c)\). It commutes with restrictions, hence acts on cochains, and the displayed formulas say \(\operatorname{as}\bigl((f\smile g)\smile h\bigr)=f\smile(g\smile h)\). Since \(\operatorname{as}\) commutes with the differentials, on cohomology of \((\mathcal U\cap\mathcal V)\cap\mathcal W\) we get \(\operatorname{as}_*\bigl(([f]\smile[g])\smile[h]\bigr)=[f]\smile([g]\smile[h])\). The two sides are compatible with the refinement maps of the three coverings (the same computation as in (b)). Passing to the limit, for \(\xi\in H^p(X,\mathcal F)\), \(\eta\in H^q(X,\mathcal G)\), \(\zeta\in H^r(X,\mathcal H)\),

\[ \operatorname{as}_*\bigl((\xi\smile\eta)\smile\zeta\bigr)=\xi\smile(\eta\smile\zeta)\quad\text{in }H^{p+q+r}\bigl(X,\mathcal F\otimes(\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal H)\bigr). \]

This is associativity of the cup product.

Anti-commutativity. The exercise says “we can show” this, and asks to prove only associativity. I prove only the easy cases \(p=0\) or \(q=0\) and do not prove the general sign rule here. (A complete proof needs an explicit homotopy between \(f\smile g\) and \(\pm\,t^*(g\smile f)\), where \(t\) is the swap of the index pairs, and a sign calculation that I have not carried out.)

Case \(p=0\): here \(\xi=s\in H^0(X,\mathcal F)=\mathcal F(X)\) (Prop. 2.6) is represented by the one-element covering \(\mathcal U=\{X\}\) and \(f=s\in C^0(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\). Let \(\eta=[g]\) with \(g\in C^q(\mathcal V,\mathcal G)\). Then \(\mathcal U\cap\mathcal V\) is \(\mathcal V\) with index set \(\{*\}\times J\), and \((s\smile g)_{j_0\dots j_q}=s|_{V_{j_0\dots j_q}}\otimes g_{j_0\dots j_q}\), while \((g\smile s)_{j_0\dots j_q}=g_{j_0\dots j_q}\otimes s|_{V_{j_0\dots j_q}}\). Under the identification \(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G=\mathcal G\otimes\mathcal F\) they coincide. So \(\xi\smile\eta=\eta\smile\xi\), which is the claim since \((-1)^{pq}=1\). The case \(q=0\) is the same with the roles exchanged. ∎

5.3 Cohomology of projective schemes

Cohomology is Cech cohomology as in Def. 2.10, computed on a finite affine covering of a separated scheme (Thm. 2.19) with ordered cochains (Cor. 2.4). Results of the text are cited by number (“Thm. 3.2”, “Cor. 3.22”, …) and results of other chapters as “Ex. 2.2.8”, “Prop. 3.3.6”, …. Exercises of Chapter 5 are cited as “Ex. 2.3” and so on. For a ring \(A\) we write \(\mathbf P^d_A=\operatorname{Proj}A[T_0,\dots,T_d]\), and \(\mathcal O(q)\) for \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^d}(q)\). An algebraic variety is a separated scheme of finite type over a field (not necessarily reduced), so a closed subscheme \(V_+(F_1,\dots,F_c)\) of \(\mathbf P^d_k\) is a variety.

Honest notes. Several statements of this section are not correct as printed or need a hypothesis; the solutions say so where it happens. Ex. 3.2: the formula of Lemma 3.1(c) needs \(d\ge1\). Ex. 3.3: “defined as a variety by \(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r}\)” is read as “equal to the closed subscheme \(V_+(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r})\)” (varieties need not be reduced), and standard dimension theory (Krull's theorem, Cohen–Macaulayness of regular local rings) is used without proof. Ex. 3.5(a),(b) are false when the special fibre has dimension \(0\) (a counterexample with three points in \(\mathbf P^2\) is given), and in (a) the ideal \(I\) has to be the saturated ideal of \(Z\); both are proved for \(r\ge1\) (using Rem. 3.21 of the text and the upper semicontinuity of the dimension of the fibres of a proper morphism, Chevalley, EGA IV 13.1.5, which is not proved in the text). Part (c) is proved as printed. Ex. 3.6: the relative Frobenius is the morphism \(X\to X^{(p)}\) and not the other way round; both \(F_{X/S}\) and the projection \(X^{(p)}\to X\) are treated. Ex. 3.9: \(f\) has to be dominant. Ex. 3.10 is false if \(f\) is not surjective (counterexample: a closed immersion of two points into the affine line), so it is proved for surjective \(f\); this affects Ex. 3.9(b) only through the (automatic) surjectivity of \(f\). Ex. 3.12: the geometrically connected case is proved as printed. The geometrically integral case is false for non-reduced \(Y\) (counterexample given); it is proved here only for \(Y\) integral and \(f\) flat. The case of a reduced \(Y\) with \(f\) not flat is not proved. Ex. 3.13 and 3.14 rest on Thm. 3.20 and Lemma 3.14 of the text; in Ex. 3.14(e) the phrase “\(X\) is a complete intersection in some \(\mathbf P^d_Y\)” is read as “\(X=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_c)\) and all fibres have dimension \(d-c\)”. Ex. 3.4 and 3.15 are proved completely.

Exercise 3.1

Problem

Let \(k\) be a field, and let \(X\) be the open subvariety \(D(x)\cup D(y)\) of \(\operatorname{Spec}k[x,y]\). We want to show that \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\) is of infinite dimension over \(k\).

  1. (a)

    Show that the \(x^iy^j\), \(i,j\in\Z\), form a basis of \(k[x,1/x,y,1/y]\) as a \(k\)-vector space.

  2. (b)

    Show that \(k[x,1/x,y]+k[x,y,1/y]\) is generated by the vectors \(x^iy^j\) with \(\max\{i,j\}\ge0\), and that

    \[ H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\simeq k[x,1/x,y,1/y]/\bigl(k[x,1/x,y]+k[x,y,1/y]\bigr) \]

    has infinite dimension over \(k\).

Solution

(a) The ring \(R=k[x,1/x,y,1/y]=k[x,y]_{xy}\) is spanned by the elements \(f/(xy)^n\) with \(f\in k[x,y]\), that is by the monomials \(x^{i}y^{j}=x^{i+n}y^{j+n}/(xy)^n\) with \(i,j\in\Z\). Suppose a finite combination \(\sum c_{ij}x^iy^j\) vanishes in \(R\). Choose \(n\) with \(i,j\ge-n\) for all \((i,j)\) occurring. Multiplying by \((xy)^n\) we get \(\sum c_{ij}x^{i+n}y^{j+n}=0\) in \(R\), hence in \(k[x,y]\), because \(k[x,y]\to R\) is injective (\(k[x,y]\) is a domain). Distinct monomials of \(k[x,y]\) are linearly independent, so all \(c_{ij}=0\).

(b) We have \(\mathcal O(D(x))=k[x,1/x,y]\), which by (a) is the span of the \(x^iy^j\) with \(i\in\Z\), \(j\ge0\) (it is the localization of \(k[x,y]\) at \(x\)), and \(\mathcal O(D(y))=k[x,y,1/y]\) is the span of the \(x^iy^j\) with \(i\ge0\), \(j\in\Z\). The sum of two subspaces spanned by subsets of a basis is spanned by the union of these subsets: the monomials with \(j\ge0\) or \(i\ge0\), that is with \(\max\{i,j\}\ge0\).

The scheme \(X\) is separated (open in an affine scheme) and \(\mathcal U=\{D(x),D(y)\}\) is an affine covering with \(D(x)\cap D(y)=D(xy)=\operatorname{Spec}R\). By Thm. 2.19 and Cor. 2.4 (order \(D(x)<D(y)\)), \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=H^1(\mathcal U,\mathcal O_X)\) is the cohomology in degree \(1\) of the complex \(C^0\to C^1\to C^2=0\) with \(C^0=k[x,1/x,y]\oplus k[x,y,1/y]\), \(C^1=R\), \(d(a,b)=b-a\). Hence

\[ H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=R/\bigl(k[x,1/x,y]+k[x,y,1/y]\bigr). \]

By (a) and the description of the sum, the classes of the monomials \(x^iy^j\) with \(i\le-1\) and \(j\le-1\) form a basis of this quotient (the sum is spanned by a subset of a basis, the quotient has as basis the remaining basis vectors). There are infinitely many such monomials, so \(\dim_kH^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=\infty\). ∎

Exercise 3.2

Problem

We are going to show Lemma 3.1(c). Let \(A\) be a ring, and let \(X=\operatorname{Proj}A[T_0,\dots,T_d]\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\}_{0\le i\le d}\) denote the covering of \(X\) with \(U_i=D_+(T_i)\). Let \(q\in\Z\). Show that \(\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{01\dots d})\) admits as a basis over \(A\) the elements of the form \(D/N\), where \(D,N\) are monomials without common variable and such that \(\deg D=\deg N+q\). Show that if a \(T_i\) does not divide \(N\), then \(D/N\) belongs to \(\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{01\dots\hat i\dots d})|_{U_{01\dots d}}\).

  2. (b)

    Show that the images under \(\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{01\dots d})\to H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) of the elements of the form \(T_0^{r_0}\cdots T_d^{r_d}\) with \(r_i\le-1\) and \(\sum_ir_i=q\) form a basis of \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) over \(A\). In particular, the module \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1))\) is free of rank \(1\) over \(A\), with \(e:=[(T_0\cdots T_d)^{-1}]\) as a basis.

  3. (c)

    Let \(n\ge0\). Show that the cup product (Ex. 2.17)

    \[ \smile\;:\;H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(n))\times H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1-n))\to H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1)) \]

    induces a non-degenerate bilinear map (let \(M,N\) be monomials of degree \(n\), show that \([M]\smile[(T_0\cdots T_dN)^{-1}]=e\) if \(M=N\) and \(0\) otherwise).

Solution

Hypothesis \(d\ge1\). The proof (and Lemma 3.1(c)) needs \(d\ge1\): for \(d=0\) we have \(X=\operatorname{Spec}A\), and \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X)=A\) while \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(-1))^\vee=0\), so the formula of Lemma 3.1(c) fails. From now on \(d\ge1\).

Set-up. Put \(B=A[T_0,\dots,T_d]\) and \(B^{\pm}=B[T_0^{-1},\dots,T_d^{-1}]\). This is a free \(A\)-module with basis the Laurent monomials \(T^r=T_0^{r_0}\cdots T_d^{r_d}\), \(r\in\Z^{d+1}\) (the argument of Ex. 3.1(a) applies), graded by \(|r|=\sum r_i\). For \(I\subseteq\{0,\dots,d\}\) let \(U_I=\bigcap_{i\in I}U_i=D_+(T_I)\), \(T_I=\prod_{i\in I}T_i\). Then \(\mathcal O_X(q)(U_I)=(B_{T_I})_q\), the degree \(q\) part of \(B_{T_I}\), and all these modules and the restriction maps are inclusions of \(A\)-submodules of \(B^{\pm}\) (as in the proof of Lemma 1.22). For \(I=\{0,\dots,d\}\setminus\{i\}\) write \(\hat i\) for the index set.

(a) The module \(\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{01\dots d})=(B^{\pm})_q\) is free with basis the \(T^r\) with \(|r|=q\). Write \(r=\rho-\nu\) with \(\rho_i=\max(r_i,0)\) and \(\nu_i=\max(-r_i,0)\). Then \(T^r=D/N\) with \(D=T^\rho\), \(N=T^\nu\) monomials without common variable and \(\deg D-\deg N=|r|=q\). Conversely a pair \((D,N)\) of monomials without common variable determines \(r=\rho-\nu\) uniquely. So the \(D/N\) of the statement are exactly the \(T^r\), \(|r|=q\), and form a basis. A variable \(T_i\) does not divide \(N\) iff \(\nu_i=0\) iff \(r_i\ge0\). The module \((B_{T_{\hat i}})_q\) is spanned by the \(T^r\) with \(|r|=q\) and \(r_i\ge0\) (the other exponents are arbitrary). So if \(T_i\nmid N\), then \(D/N=T^r\in\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{\hat i})\) restricted to \(U_{01\dots d}\). Conversely every element of \(\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{\hat i})\) is a combination of such \(T^r\) with \(r_i\ge0\).

(b) By Thm. 2.19 and Cor. 2.4, \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) is the cokernel of \(d:C^{d-1}\to C^d\) in the complex of ordered cochains for \(0<1<\dots<d\) (here \(d\ge1\) and \(C^{d+1}=0\)). We have \(C^d=\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{01\dots d})\), \(C^{d-1}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^d\mathcal O_X(q)(U_{\hat i})\) and \(d(g)=\sum_i(-1)^ig_{\hat i}|_{U_{01\dots d}}\). By (a) the image of \(d\) is the span of the \(T^r\) (\(|r|=q\)) having some \(r_i\ge0\) (each \(T^r\) with \(r_i\ge0\) is the image of \(\pm T^r\) placed in the component \(\hat i\)). Hence the cokernel is the quotient of the free module with basis \(\{T^r\}_{|r|=q}\) by the span of a subset of this basis, so it is free with basis the classes of the \(T^r\) with all \(r_i\le-1\) and \(|r|=q\). For \(q=-d-1\) the only such \(r\) is \((-1,\dots,-1)\), so \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1))\) is free of rank \(1\) with basis \(e\). (If \(q>-d-1\) there is no such \(r\), so \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(q))=0\), which is the last assertion of Lemma 3.1(c).)

(c) Cup product with a global section. Let \(s\in H^0(X,\mathcal F)\) and \(\xi=[g]\in H^d(\mathcal U,\mathcal G)\) with \(g\in C^d(\mathcal U,\mathcal G)\) a cocycle. In the definition of Ex. 2.17 take both coverings equal to \(\mathcal U\) and \(f=(s|_{U_i})_i\in C^0(\mathcal U,\mathcal F)\). The covering \(\mathcal W=\{U_i\cap U_j\}_{(i,j)}\) refines \(\mathcal U\) through \(\rho:(i,j)\mapsto j\), and \((f\smile g)_{\alpha}=s|_{W_\alpha}\otimes g_{j_0\dots j_d}|_{W_\alpha}\) for \(\alpha=((i_0,j_0),\dots,(i_d,j_d))\). This is \(\rho^*(sg)\) where \((sg)_{j_0\dots j_d}=s|\otimes g_{j_0\dots j_d}\) is the image of \(g\) under the sheaf homomorphism \(\mathcal G\to\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G\), \(u\mapsto s\otimes u\). Since refinement maps induce the canonical maps into \(H^d(X,-)\), we get

\[ s\smile[g]=[s\,g]\in H^d(X,\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G). \]

Take \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X(n)\), \(\mathcal G=\mathcal O_X(-d-1-n)\) and identify \(\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal G=\mathcal O_X(-d-1)\) canonically. Then \(s\,g\) is the product of Laurent polynomials.

Let \(M,N\) be monomials of degree \(n\) with exponents \(\mu,\nu\in\mathbf N^{d+1}\), \(|\mu|=|\nu|=n\). By Lemma 1.22, the monomials \(M\) form a basis of \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(n))=B_n\). By (b) with \(q=-d-1-n\), the classes \([(T_0\cdots T_dN)^{-1}]=[T^{-\nu-\mathbf 1}]\) form a basis of \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1-n))\) (the exponents \(r=-\nu-\mathbf 1\) are exactly the \(r\) with \(r_i\le-1\) and \(|r|=-d-1-n\)). Now

\[ M\cdot(T_0\cdots T_dN)^{-1}=T^{\mu-\nu-\mathbf 1}. \]

If \(M=N\) this is \((T_0\cdots T_d)^{-1}\) and the class is \(e\). If \(M\ne N\), then \(\mu\ne\nu\) and \(|\mu|=|\nu|\), so there is \(i\) with \(\mu_i>\nu_i\); then the exponent \(\mu_i-\nu_i-1\ge0\), and by (b) the class of \(T^{\mu-\nu-\mathbf 1}\) is \(0\). Thus, after identifying \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1))\) with \(A\) through the basis \(e\), the pairing has the monomials \(M\) and the classes \([(T_0\cdots T_dN)^{-1}]\) as dual bases: the matrix of the pairing is the identity. Both modules are free of the same finite rank, and the induced maps \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1-n))\to\operatorname{Hom}_A(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(n)),A)\) and \(H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(n))\to\operatorname{Hom}_A(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(-d-1-n)),A)\) are isomorphisms: the pairing is non-degenerate (perfect). With \(m=-d-1-n\) this gives Lemma 3.1(c): \(H^d(X,\mathcal O_X(m))\simeq H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(-m-d-1))^\vee\) for \(m\le-d-1\), while for \(m>-d-1\) both sides are \(0\) by (b) and Lemma 1.22. ∎

Exercise 3.3

Problem

Let \(P=\mathbf P^d_k\) be a projective space over a field \(k\). Let \(X\) be a closed subvariety of \(P\) of dimension \(r\). We say that \(X\) is a complete intersection if it is defined (as a variety) by \(d-r\) homogeneous polynomials \(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r}\). This notion depends on the embedding \(X\to P\).

  1. (a)

    Let \(Z=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r-1})\) (\(Z=P\) if \(d-r=1\)). Show that \(Z\) is a complete intersection. Let \(\mathcal J\) be the sheaf of ideals of \(\mathcal O_Z\) that defines the subscheme \(X\) of \(Z\). Show that \(\mathcal J\simeq\mathcal O_Z(-\deg F_{d-r})\).

  2. (b)

    Let \(q\in\Z\). Show the following properties by induction on \(d-r\): (1) \(H^p(X,\mathcal O_X(q))=0\) if \(1\le p\le r-1\). (2) \(H^0(P,\mathcal O_P(q))\to H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) is surjective if \(r\ge1\). (3) \(\dim_kH^p(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) is an integer that only depends on \(p,q,d\), and the \(\deg F_i\).

  3. (c)

    Show that \(X\) is geometrically connected if \(r\ge1\).

  4. (d)

    Let \(d=2\), and let \(X=V_+(F)\) with \(\deg F=n\). Show that \(\dim_kH^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=(n-1)(n-2)/2\).

Solution

Interpretation. “Defined as a variety by \(F_1,\dots,F_c\)” (\(c=d-r\)) means that \(X\) is the closed subscheme \(V_+(F_1,\dots,F_c)\) of \(P\) (the scheme whose homogeneous ideal is generated by the \(F_i\)), and \(\dim X=r\). We put \(e_i=\deg F_i\), \(Z_i=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_i)\) (\(Z_0=P\), \(Z_c=X\)) and \(S=k[T_0,\dots,T_d]\). For a point \(x\in P\) and \(x\in D_+(T_l)\) let \(f_i=F_i/T_l^{e_i}\in\mathcal O_{P,x}\); then \(\mathcal O_{Z_i,x}=\mathcal O_{P,x}/(f_1,\dots,f_i)\).

We use the following standard facts of dimension theory. (K1) (Krull) If \(m\le d\), every irreducible component of \(V_+(F_1,\dots,F_m)\subseteq\mathbf P^d_k\) is non-empty of dimension \(\ge d-m\). (K2) If \(C\subseteq\mathbf P^d_k\) is an integral closed subscheme of dimension \(n\ge1\) and \(F\) is a form of positive degree not vanishing identically on \(C\), then \(C\cap V_+(F)\) is non-empty and all its irreducible components have dimension \(n-1\). (K3) For an integral scheme \(C\) of finite type over a field and \(x\in C\), \(\dim\mathcal O_{C,x}=\dim C-\dim\overline{\{x\}}\). (K4) A regular local ring is Cohen–Macaulay; hence in it, if an ideal of height \(i\) is generated by \(i\) elements, these elements form a regular sequence (Matsumura, Thm. 17.4), and so does every initial segment of them.

Lemma A. Let \(F_1,\dots,F_c\in S\) be homogeneous, \(c\le d\), with \(\dim V_+(F_1,\dots,F_c)=d-c\). Then for every \(0\le i\le c\), every irreducible component of \(Z_i\) has dimension exactly \(d-i\), and for every \(x\in Z_i\) the elements \(f_1,\dots,f_i\) form a regular sequence in \(\mathcal O_{P,x}\). Consequently, for \(1\le i\le c\) the multiplication by \(F_i\),

\[ 0\to\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1}}(-e_i)\xrightarrow{\,F_i\,}\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1}}\to\mathcal O_{Z_i}\to0, \]

is an exact sequence of sheaves on \(Z_{i-1}\).

Proof. (The forms \(F_i\) have positive degree: a non-zero constant would give \(X=\emptyset\), which is excluded by \(\dim X=r\ge0\), and the zero form is excluded by the argument below, since it vanishes on every \(C\).) We show by descending induction on \(i\) that \(\dim Z_i=d-i\) and that \(Z_i\) is pure of dimension \(d-i\). For \(i=c\) the dimension is the hypothesis. Suppose \(\dim Z_i=d-i\) for some \(i\ge1\). Let \(C\) be an irreducible component of \(Z_{i-1}\) (with reduced structure). By (K1) \(\dim C\ge d-i+1\ge1\). If \(F_i\) vanished identically on \(C\), then \(C\subseteq Z_i\), contradicting \(\dim Z_i=d-i<\dim C\). So by (K2) the components of \(C\cap V_+(F_i)\) have dimension \(\dim C-1\). As \(Z_i=\bigcup_C(C\cap V_+(F_i))\), we get \(d-i=\dim Z_i=\max_C\dim C-1\). Hence \(\dim C=d-i+1\) for every component \(C\) of \(Z_{i-1}\) (all are \(\ge d-i+1\) by (K1)), so \(Z_{i-1}\) is pure of dimension \(d-(i-1)\), and the same argument shows that \(Z_i\) (whose components are among those of the \(C\cap V_+(F_i)\)) is pure of dimension \(d-i\). This proves the first claim.

Now let \(x\in Z_i\) and \(R=\mathcal O_{P,x}\), a regular local ring of dimension \(d-\dim\overline{\{x\}}\). The minimal primes of \(R/(f_1,\dots,f_i)=\mathcal O_{Z_i,x}\) correspond to the components \(C\) of \(Z_i\) through \(x\), and by (K3) \(\dim\mathcal O_{C,x}=d-i-\dim\overline{\{x\}}=\dim R-i\). So \(\dim R/(f_1,\dots,f_i)=\dim R-i\), i.e. \((f_1,\dots,f_i)\) has height \(i\) (\(R\) is Cohen–Macaulay). By (K4), \(f_1,\dots,f_i\) is a regular sequence.

For the exact sequence, check stalks at \(x\in Z_{i-1}\). If \(x\in Z_i\), \(f_i\) is a non-zero-divisor on \(\mathcal O_{P,x}/(f_1,\dots,f_{i-1})=\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1},x}\) by what we have shown, so multiplication by \(F_i\) is injective. If \(x\notin Z_i\), then \(f_i(x)\ne0\), \(f_i\) is a unit and the multiplication is bijective and \(\mathcal O_{Z_i,x}=0\). The cokernel is \(\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1}}/F_i\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1}}(-e_i)=\mathcal O_{Z_i}\). ∎

(a) Let \(c=d-r\). The hypotheses of Lemma A hold for \(F_1,\dots,F_c\) (\(c\le d\), \(\dim X=r=d-c\)). Put \(Z=Z_{c-1}\). By Lemma A, \(\dim Z=d-(c-1)=r+1\), and \(Z=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_{c-1})\) is defined by \(d-(r+1)\) forms, so \(Z\) is a complete intersection of dimension \(r+1\) (for \(c=1\), \(Z=P\) of dimension \(d=r+1\)). The ideal sheaf of \(X\) in \(Z\) is the image \(\mathcal J\) of \(F_c:\mathcal O_Z(-e_c)\to\mathcal O_Z\), which is injective by Lemma A. So \(\mathcal J\simeq\mathcal O_Z(-e_c)\), \(e_c=\deg F_{d-r}\), and

\[ 0\to\mathcal O_Z(-e_c)\to\mathcal O_Z\to\mathcal O_X\to0 \tag{1}\]

is exact (as a sequence of sheaves on \(Z\), \(\mathcal O_X\) meaning \(i_*\mathcal O_X\) for \(i:X\to Z\)).

(b) We prove (1), (2), (3) by induction on \(c=d-r\ge0\), for all \(d,k,q\) and all complete intersections. If \(c=0\), then \(X=P\) and (1) holds by Lemma 3.1(b) (there \(r=d\), so \(p\) ranges over \(1\le p\le d-1\)), (2) is the identity, (3) holds by Lemma 3.1 because \(\dim_kH^p(P,\mathcal O(q))\) is \(0\) for \(p\ne0,d\) and equals \(\dim B_q\), resp. \(\dim B_{-q-d-1}\), for \(p=0\), resp. \(p=d\).

Let \(c\ge1\), and let \(Z\), \(e=e_c\) be as in (a); \(Z\) is a complete intersection of dimension \(r+1\) defined by \(c-1\) forms, so the properties hold for \(Z\) (all \(q\)): (1\(_Z\)) \(H^p(Z,\mathcal O_Z(m))=0\) for \(1\le p\le r\); (2\(_Z\)) \(H^0(P,\mathcal O_P(m))\to H^0(Z,\mathcal O_Z(m))\) is surjective (\(r+1\ge1\)); (3\(_Z\)) \(h^p(Z,\mathcal O_Z(m)):=\dim_kH^p(Z,\mathcal O_Z(m))\) depends only on \(p,m,d,e_1,\dots,e_{c-1}\). Twisting (1) by the invertible, hence flat, sheaf \(\mathcal O(q)\) gives the exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal O_Z(q-e)\to\mathcal O_Z(q)\to\mathcal O_X(q)\to0\) and, by Cor. 2.22 and Ex. 2.3(b), the long exact sequence

\[ \cdots\to H^p(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))\to H^p(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))\to H^p(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\to H^{p+1}(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))\to H^{p+1}(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))\to\cdots\tag{2}\]

(Z is projective, hence separated). Moreover \(H^p(X,-)=0\) for \(p>r\) by Prop. 2.24.

(1) Let \(1\le p\le r-1\). In (2), \(H^p(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))=0\) and \(H^{p+1}(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))=0\) by (1\(_Z\)) because \(1\le p<p+1\le r\). So \(H^p(X,\mathcal O_X(q))=0\).

(2) Let \(r\ge1\). In (2) with \(p=0\), \(H^1(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))=0\) by (1\(_Z\)) (\(1\le r\)), so \(H^0(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))\to H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) is surjective. The map \(H^0(P,\mathcal O_P(q))\to H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) is the composition of this map with \(H^0(P,\mathcal O_P(q))\to H^0(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))\), which is surjective by (2\(_Z\)). So (2) holds.

(3) By (1) and the vanishing for \(p>r\) it remains to treat \(p=0\) and \(p=r\). If \(r\ge1\): for \(p=0\) (2) gives \(0\to H^0(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))\to H^0(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))\to H^0(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\to H^1(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))=0\) (the first zero because multiplication by \(F_c\) is injective on sections), so \(h^0(X,\mathcal O_X(q))=h^0(Z,q)-h^0(Z,q-e)\). For \(p=r\ge1\): since \(H^r(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))=0\) (by (1\(_Z\))) and \(H^{r+1}(X,\mathcal O_X(q))=0\), (2) gives \(0\to H^r(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\to H^{r+1}(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q-e))\to H^{r+1}(Z,\mathcal O_Z(q))\to0\), so \(h^r(X,q)=h^{r+1}(Z,q-e)-h^{r+1}(Z,q)\). If \(r=0\): \(p=0=r\) and (2) gives the exact sequence \(0\to H^0(Z,q-e)\to H^0(Z,q)\to H^0(X,q)\to H^1(Z,q-e)\to H^1(Z,q)\to H^1(X,q)=0\), so \(h^0(X,q)=h^0(Z,q)-h^0(Z,q-e)+h^1(Z,q-e)-h^1(Z,q)\). In all cases \(\dim_kH^p(X,\mathcal O_X(q))\) is an integer combination of the numbers \(h^*(Z,q)\), \(h^*(Z,q-e)\), which by (3\(_Z\)) depend only on the indicated data. This proves (3).

(c) Let \(r\ge1\) and let \(k'/k\) be any field extension. The scheme \(X_{k'}=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_c)\subseteq\mathbf P^d_{k'}\) is non-empty, has dimension \(r\) (dimension does not change under extension of the base field) and is defined by \(d-r\) forms, so it is a complete intersection of dimension \(r\) over \(k'\), and the whole of (b) applies to it. By (b)(2), \(k'=H^0(\mathbf P^d_{k'},\mathcal O)\to H^0(X_{k'},\mathcal O_{X_{k'}})\) is surjective; it is also injective (it is the structure map, \(X_{k'}\ne\emptyset\)), so \(H^0(X_{k'},\mathcal O_{X_{k'}})=k'\). If \(X_{k'}=U_1\sqcup U_2\) with \(U_i\) open and closed and non-empty, the characteristic functions give a \(k'\)-subalgebra \(k'\times k'\subseteq H^0(X_{k'},\mathcal O)\) of dimension \(2\), a contradiction. So \(X_{k'}\) is connected for every \(k'\), in particular for \(k'=\bar k\): \(X\) is geometrically connected.

(d) Here \(d=2\), \(c=1\), \(r=1\) and \(X=V_+(F)\), \(\deg F=n\ge1\). We apply sequence (1) with \(Z=P=\mathbf P^2_k\) and cohomology: \(H^1(P,\mathcal O_P)\to H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\to H^2(P,\mathcal O_P(-n))\to H^2(P,\mathcal O_P)\). By Lemma 3.1 (with \(d=2\)), \(H^1(P,\mathcal O_P)=0\) and \(H^2(P,\mathcal O_P)=0\) (as \(0\ge-d\)). Therefore \(H^1(X,\mathcal O_X)\simeq H^2(P,\mathcal O_P(-n))\), and by Lemma 3.1(c) (applied with Ex. 3.2),

\[ \dim_kH^1(X,\mathcal O_X)=\dim_kH^0(P,\mathcal O_P(n-3))=\dim_kB_{n-3}=\binom{n-1}{2}=\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}2, \]

where \(B=k[T_0,T_1,T_2]\); for \(n=1,2\) the module \(B_{n-3}\) is \(0\) and the binomial coefficient \(\binom{n-1}{2}\) is \(0\) as well. ∎

Exercise 3.4

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a Noetherian local scheme with closed point \(y_0\), and \(X=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r})\) be a closed subscheme of \(\mathbf P^d_Y\). Let us suppose that \(X_{y_0}\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y_0)}\), of dimension \(r\). Show that for any \(y\in Y\), \(X_y\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\), of dimension \(r\) (use induction on \(d-r\)). Likewise, show that \(X\) is flat over \(Y\) (use Lemma 4.3.16).

Solution

Let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\) with \(A\) Noetherian local, \(\mathfrak m\) its maximal ideal, \(k_0=k(y_0)=A/\mathfrak m\). Here \(F_j\in A[T_0,\dots,T_d]\) are homogeneous of degree \(e_j\), \(c=d-r\), and \(Z_i=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_i)\subseteq\mathbf P^d_Y\) (\(Z_0=\mathbf P^d_Y\), \(Z_c=X\)). For \(y\in Y\) the fibre \((Z_i)_y\) is \(V_+(F_1(y),\dots,F_i(y))\subseteq\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\), where \(F_j(y)\) is the image of \(F_j\) in \(k(y)[T]\). In particular \(X_y=V_+(F_1(y),\dots,F_c(y))\), so to be a complete intersection of dimension \(r\) it only remains to see that \(\dim X_y=r\). By (K1) of Ex. 3.3 every non-empty \(V_+\) of \(m\le d\) forms in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\) has all components of dimension \(\ge d-m\); so \((Z_i)_y\ne\emptyset\) and \(\dim(Z_i)_y\ge d-i\), and what must be shown is \(\dim(Z_i)_y\le d-i\).

We prove by induction on \(i=0,\dots,c\) the statement

\(S(i)\): \(Z_i\) is flat over \(Y\), and \(\dim(Z_i)_y\le d-i\) for every \(y\in Y\).

\(S(0)\) is clear. Assume \(S(i-1)\), \(i\ge1\). The hypothesis says that \(\dim V_+(F_1(y_0),\dots,F_c(y_0))=d-c\); so Lemma A of Ex. 3.3 applies over the field \(k_0\) to the forms \(F_j(y_0)\) and shows: for every \(x\in(Z_i)_{y_0}\) the element \(f_i\) (local equation of \(F_i\)) is a non-zero-divisor on \(\mathcal O_{(Z_{i-1})_{y_0},x}\).

Flatness at the points over \(y_0\). Let \(x\in Z_i\) lie over \(y_0\). Put \(B=\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1},x}\). It is a Noetherian local ring, flat over \(A\) by \(S(i-1)\), and \(A\to B\) is a local homomorphism. We have \(B/\mathfrak mB=\mathcal O_{(Z_{i-1})_{y_0},x}\), and the image of \(f_i\) in it is a non-zero-divisor. By Lemma 4.3.16, \(B/f_iB=\mathcal O_{Z_i,x}\) is flat over \(A\). Moreover \(f_i\) is a non-zero-divisor on \(B\) (this is Ex. 4.3.14; here is the argument). Put \(b=f_i\) and \(J=\operatorname{Ann}_B(b)\). The module \(bB=\operatorname{Ker}(B\to B/bB)\) is flat, since \(B\) and \(B/bB\) are (\(\operatorname{Tor}_j^A(bB,N)=\operatorname{Tor}_{j+1}^A(B/bB,N)=0\) for \(j\ge1\)). So both sequences \(0\to J\to B\to bB\to0\) and \(0\to bB\to B\to B/bB\to0\) remain exact after \(\otimes_AA/\mathfrak m\). The composite \(B/\mathfrak mB\to bB\otimes A/\mathfrak m\hookrightarrow B/\mathfrak mB\) is multiplication by \(b\), which is injective; hence the first map is injective, so \(J\otimes A/\mathfrak m=J/\mathfrak mJ=0\), and \(J=0\) by Nakayama's lemma.

Flatness everywhere. Let \(x'\in Z_i\) be arbitrary and \(y'\) its image. The closure \(T\) of \(\{x'\}\) in \(Z_i\) is closed, and \(Z_i\to Y\) is projective, hence closed (proper). So \(f(T)\) is a closed set containing \(y'\), hence contains the closure of \(y'\), which contains the closed point \(y_0\) (\(Y\) is local). Thus there is \(x\in T\) over \(y_0\), and \(x'\) is a generization of \(x\). So \(\mathcal O_{Z_i,x'}\) is a localization of \(\mathcal O_{Z_i,x}\), which is flat over \(A\), hence \(\mathcal O_{Z_i,x'}\) is flat over \(A\). As \(\mathcal O_{Z_i,x'}\) is a module over the localization \(A_{\mathfrak p'}=\mathcal O_{Y,y'}\) of \(A\), it is flat over \(\mathcal O_{Y,y'}\) (for an \(A_{\mathfrak p'}\)-module \(N\), \(M\otimes_{A_{\mathfrak p'}}N=M\otimes_AN\)). So \(Z_i\) is flat over \(Y\).

Dimension of the fibres. Let \(y\in Y\) and let \(x'\) be a closed point of \((Z_i)_y\). Put \(B'=\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1},x'}\), flat over \(A\) and \(B'/f_iB'=\mathcal O_{Z_i,x'}\) flat over \(A\) by what we just proved. Tensoring \(0\to B'\xrightarrow{f_i}B'\to B'/f_iB'\to0\) (exact since \(f_i\) is a non-zero-divisor on \(B'\): with \(x\in Z_i\) over \(y_0\) such that \(x'\) is a generization of \(x\), as in the previous step, \(B'\) is a localization of \(\mathcal O_{Z_{i-1},x}\) on which \(f_i\) is regular) with \(k(y)\) over \(A\), we obtain an exact sequence, because \(\operatorname{Tor}_1^A(B'/f_iB',k(y))=0\):

\[ 0\to B'\otimes_Ak(y)\xrightarrow{\,f_i\,}B'\otimes_Ak(y)\to(B'/f_iB')\otimes_Ak(y)\to0. \]

Here \(B'\otimes_Ak(y)=\mathcal O_{(Z_{i-1})_y,x'}\). So \(f_i\) is a non-zero-divisor in the Noetherian local ring \(\mathcal O_{(Z_{i-1})_y,x'}\), and therefore \(\dim\mathcal O_{(Z_i)_y,x'}=\dim\mathcal O_{(Z_{i-1})_y,x'}-1\le\dim(Z_{i-1})_y-1\le d-i\) by \(S(i-1)\). A scheme of finite type over a field has dimension equal to the supremum of the dimensions of its local rings at closed points, so \(\dim(Z_i)_y\le d-i\). This proves \(S(i)\).

For \(i=c\), \(S(c)\) says that \(X\) is flat over \(Y\) and \(\dim X_y\le r\); with \(\dim X_y\ge r\) we get \(\dim X_y=r\) for all \(y\in Y\). Hence every \(X_y\) is a complete intersection of dimension \(r\) in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\). ∎

Exercise 3.5

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a locally Noetherian scheme. Let \(Z\) be a closed subscheme of \(\mathbf P^d_Y\), flat over \(Y\). Let \(y_0\in Y\) be a point such that \(Z_{y_0}\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y_0)}\). Let us set \(r=\dim Z_{y_0}\).

  1. (a)

    Let us suppose \(Y\) is affine. Let us write \(Z=V_+(I)\). Show that there exist \(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r}\in I\) such that if we set \(X=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_{d-r})\), then \(X_{y_0}=Z_{y_0}\) as a subscheme of \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y_0)}\).

  2. (b)

    Show that there exists an open neighborhood \(V\) of \(y_0\) such that \(Z\times_YV=X\times_YV\) (Ex. 4.3.8). Show that for \(V\) small enough, \(Z_y\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\) of dimension \(r\) for every \(y\in V\).

  3. (c)

    Let us suppose that \(Y\) is connected and that \(Z_y\) is a complete intersection in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\) for all \(y\in Y\). Let \(p\ge0\), \(q\in\Z\). Show that the function \(y\mapsto\dim_{k(y)}H^p(Z_y,\mathcal O_{Z_y}(q))\) is constant on \(Y\).

Solution

Summary of what is true. (a) and the second assertion of (b) are false as printed when \(r=0\), and in (a) the ideal \(I\) must be the saturated ideal of \(Z\). We prove (a), (b) for \(r\ge1\) (with \(I=\bigoplus_nH^0(\mathbf P^d_A,\mathcal I_Z(n))\)), and (c) as printed.

Counterexample for \(r=0\). Let \(A=k[t]_{(t)}\) (a discrete valuation ring), \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\), \(d=2\), and let \(Z\subset\mathbf P^2_Y\) be the union of the three sections \(P_1=(1:0:0)\), \(P_2=(0:1:0)\), \(P_3=(1:1:t)\); \(Z\simeq\bigsqcup_3\operatorname{Spec}A\) is flat over \(Y\). Their reductions modulo \(t\) are three distinct points of the line \(z=0\), so \(Z_{y_0}=V_+(z,xy(x-y))\) is a complete intersection with \(r=0\), \(d-r=2\). If \(F_1,F_2\in I\) (any homogeneous ideal \(I\) with \(Z=V_+(I)\) is contained in the saturated ideal) satisfied \(V_+(F_1,F_2)_{y_0}=Z_{y_0}\), then \(V_+(F_1(y_0),F_2(y_0))\) is a zero-dimensional complete intersection of length \(3\) in \(\mathbf P^2_k\), so by Bezout \(\{\deg F_1,\deg F_2\}=\{1,3\}\). A linear form \(ax+by+cz\) in the saturated ideal vanishes on the three sections: \(a=0\) (at \(P_1\)), \(b=0\) (at \(P_2\)), \(ct=0\) (at \(P_3\)), so \(c=0\) as \(A\) is a domain, and the form is \(0\); but \(V_+(0,F_2)\) is a curve. So (a) fails. For (b): the generic fibre \(Z_\eta\) consists of the three points \((1:0:0),(0:1:0),(1:1:t)\) of \(\mathbf P^2_K\), \(K=k(t)\); these are not collinear, while a zero-dimensional complete intersection of length \(3\) in \(\mathbf P^2_K\) has degrees \((1,3)\) and lies on a line. So \(Z_\eta\) is not a complete intersection and the second assertion of (b) fails for every neighborhood \(V\) of \(y_0\).

Preliminaries. (i) The ideal sheaf commutes with base change. Let \(\mathcal I\) be the ideal sheaf of \(Z\) in \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^d_Y}\). As \(\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^d_Y}\) and \(\mathcal O_Z\) are flat over \(Y\), so is \(\mathcal I\) (the kernel of a surjection of flat modules with flat target is flat), and for every \(y\in Y\) the sequence \(0\to\mathcal I\otimes k(y)\to\mathcal O_{\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}}\to\mathcal O_{Z_y}\to0\) is exact because \(\operatorname{Tor}_1(\mathcal O_Z,k(y))=0\). So \(\mathcal I|_{\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}}\) is the ideal sheaf \(\mathcal I_y\) of \(Z_y\). (ii) By definition, \(Z_{y_0}=V_+(G_1,\dots,G_c)\) with \(c=d-r\) and \(G_j\in k[T_0,\dots,T_d]\) forms of degree \(e_j\), \(k=k(y_0)\). The \(G_j\) are sections of \(\mathcal I_{y_0}(e_j)\) on \(\mathbf P^d_k\). (iii) Fibre cohomology of \(\mathcal I_{y_0}\) if \(r\ge1\) and \(c\ge1\). Suppose \(r\ge1\) and \(c=d-r\ge1\) (so \(d\ge2\)) and let \(X_0=Z_{y_0}\). By Ex. 3.3(b)(2), \(H^0(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal O(q))\to H^0(X_0,\mathcal O_{X_0}(q))\) is surjective for all \(q\). The exact sequence \(0\to\mathcal I_{y_0}(q)\to\mathcal O(q)\to\mathcal O_{X_0}(q)\to0\) and Lemma 3.1(b) (\(H^1(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal O(q))=0\), since \(d\ge2\)) give \(H^1(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal I_{y_0}(q))=0\) for every \(q\). (iv) Dimension of fibres. We use the upper semicontinuity of the dimension of the fibres of a proper morphism (Chevalley; EGA IV 13.1.5), which is not proved in the text: for \(g:W\to Y\) proper, \(\{y\mid\dim W_y\ge n\}\) is closed.

(a) Let \(r\ge1\). If \(c=0\) then \(Z_{y_0}=\mathbf P^d_k\), the list \(F_1,\dots,F_c\) is empty, \(X=\mathbf P^d_Y\), and (a) is vacuous; so let \(c\ge1\) in the rest of (a). Let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}A\), \(y_0\) corresponds to the prime \(\mathfrak p\), \(R=A_{\mathfrak p}\) (local with residue field \(k\)), and \(I=\bigoplus_{n\ge0}I_n\), \(I_n=H^0(\mathbf P^d_A,\mathcal I(n))\subseteq H^0(\mathbf P^d_A,\mathcal O(n))=A[T]_n\) (Lemma 1.22).

Claim. For \(e\in\Z\) the map \(H^0(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal I_R(e))\to H^0(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal I_{y_0}(e))\) is surjective. Let \(\mathcal F=\mathcal I_R(e)\); it is coherent, flat over \(R\), and \(\mathcal F\otimes_Rk=\mathcal I_{y_0}(e)\) by (i). Let \(f:\mathbf P^d_R\to\operatorname{Spec}R\). By (iii) \(H^1(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal F_{y_0})=0\). By Rem. 3.21(a) the function \(y\mapsto\dim H^1(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)},\mathcal F_y)\) is upper semicontinuous on \(\operatorname{Spec}R\); the set where it is \(\ge1\) is closed and does not contain the closed point \(y_0\), but a closed subset containing some point contains its closure, hence \(y_0\); so this set is empty: \(H^1(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)},\mathcal F_y)=0\) for all \(y\in\operatorname{Spec}R\). Therefore the map (3.15) for \(p=1\) is surjective at every point, and by Rem. 3.21(c) (first part: bijective at \(y_0\), so \(H^1(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal F)\otimes k\simeq H^1(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal F_{y_0})=0\); this module is finitely generated by Thm. 3.2(a), hence \(H^1(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal F)=0\) by Nakayama; second part with \(\mathcal G=\widetilde{\mathfrak m}\)) we get \(H^1(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal F\otimes_R\mathfrak m)\simeq H^1(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal F)\otimes_R\mathfrak m=0\). Now \(\mathcal F\) is flat, so \(0\to\mathcal F\otimes_R\mathfrak m\to\mathcal F\to\mathcal F\otimes_Rk\to0\) is exact, and Cor. 2.22 and Ex. 2.3(b) (closed immersion \(\mathbf P^d_k\to\mathbf P^d_R\)) give an exact sequence \(H^0(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal F)\to H^0(\mathbf P^d_k,\mathcal I_{y_0}(e))\to H^1(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal F\otimes\mathfrak m)=0\). This proves the claim.

By the claim there are \(F'_j\in H^0(\mathbf P^d_R,\mathcal I_R(e_j))=I_{e_j}\otimes_AR\) (flat base change, Cor. 2.27) whose image in \(k[T]\) is \(G_j\). Write \(F'_j=F_j/s_j\) with \(F_j\in I_{e_j}\), \(s_j\in A\setminus\mathfrak p\). The reduction of \(F_j\) in \(k[T]\) is \(\bar s_jG_j\) with \(\bar s_j\in k^\times\). So \(F_1,\dots,F_c\in I\) are forms of degrees \(e_j\) with \(V_+(F_1(y_0),\dots,F_c(y_0))=V_+(G_1,\dots,G_c)\), i.e. \(X=V_+(F_1,\dots,F_c)\) satisfies \(X_{y_0}=Z_{y_0}\) as subschemes of \(\mathbf P^d_{k}\). Since \(F_j\in I\), \(Z\subseteq X\) is a closed subscheme.

(b) Take \(Y\) affine and \(F_j\), \(X\) as in (a) (if \(c=0\) take \(X=\mathbf P^d_Y\)); \(Z\) is a closed subscheme of \(X\) with \(Z_{y_0}=X_{y_0}\). Let \(\mathcal J\subseteq\mathcal O_X\) be the (coherent) ideal sheaf of \(Z\) in \(X\). Since \(\mathcal O_Z\) is flat over \(Y\), tensoring \(0\to\mathcal J\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathcal O_Z\to0\) with \(k(y_0)\) stays exact, and \(\mathcal O_{X_{y_0}}\to\mathcal O_{Z_{y_0}}\) is an isomorphism, so \(\mathcal J\otimes k(y_0)=0\). For \(x\in X_{y_0}\) this says \(\mathcal J_x=\mathfrak m_{y_0}\mathcal J_x\subseteq\mathfrak m_x\mathcal J_x\), hence \(\mathcal J_x=0\) by Nakayama. So \(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal J\) is a closed subset of \(X\) disjoint from \(X_{y_0}\). The morphism \(X\to Y\) is projective, hence closed, so \(V=Y\setminus f(\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal J)\) is an open neighborhood of \(y_0\), and \(\mathcal J=0\) over \(V\), i.e. \(Z\times_YV=X\times_YV\). (This is Ex. 4.3.8(b).)

Now let \(y\in V\). Then \(Z_y=X_y=V_+(F_1(y),\dots,F_c(y))\) is non-empty of dimension \(\ge d-c=r\) by (K1) of Ex. 3.3. By (iv) applied to \(X\to Y\) the set \(\{y\mid\dim X_y\ge r+1\}\) is closed and does not contain \(y_0\); replacing \(V\) by the complement in \(V\) of this closed set, we get \(\dim X_y=r\) for all \(y\in V\) (also \(Z\times_YV=X\times_YV\) still holds on the smaller \(V\)). So \(Z_y\) is a complete intersection of dimension \(r\) in \(\mathbf P^d_{k(y)}\), defined by forms of the same degrees \(e_1,\dots,e_c\) for all \(y\in V\).

(c) Let \(\varphi_{p,q}(y)=\dim_{k(y)}H^p(Z_y,\mathcal O_{Z_y}(q))\). As \(Y\) is connected, it suffices to show that \(\varphi_{p,q}\) is locally constant. Let \(y_0\in Y\) and \(r=\dim Z_{y_0}\); we may replace \(Y\) by an affine neighborhood of \(y_0\).

Case \(r\ge1\). By (b) there is a neighborhood \(V\) of \(y_0\) and forms \(F_1,\dots,F_c\) of degrees \(e_1,\dots,e_c\) such that \(Z_y=V_+(F_1(y),\dots,F_c(y))\) is a complete intersection of dimension \(r\) for every \(y\in V\). By Ex. 3.3(b)(3), \(\varphi_{p,q}(y)\) depends only on \(p,q,d,e_1,\dots,e_c\), hence is constant on \(V\).

Case \(r=0\). By (iv) there is a neighborhood \(V\) of \(y_0\) on which \(\dim Z_y\le0\); the fibres \(Z_y\) are non-empty complete intersections, hence \(0\)-dimensional. Then \(Z_V\to V\) is proper and quasi-finite, hence finite, and flat, so \(\mathcal E=f_*\mathcal O_{Z_V}\) is a coherent flat, hence locally free, \(\mathcal O_V\)-module of finite rank, and the rank at \(y\) is \(\dim_{k(y)}H^0(Z_y,\mathcal O_{Z_y})\) (formation of \(f_*\) commutes with base change for an affine morphism); the rank is locally constant. For \(p\ge1\) we have \(H^p(Z_y,-)=0\) since \(Z_y\) is affine. For \(p=0\), \(\mathcal O_{Z_y}(q)\simeq\mathcal O_{Z_y}\) because \(Z_y\) is the spectrum of an Artinian ring, so every invertible sheaf on it is trivial; so \(\varphi_{0,q}(y)=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal E_y\), locally constant.

Thus \(\varphi_{p,q}\) is locally constant, hence constant on the connected scheme \(Y\). ∎

Exercise 3.6

Problem

Let \(S\) be a scheme over a finite field \(\mathbb F_p\), and let \(X\to S\) be a scheme of finite type. Show that the relative Frobenius \(X^{(p)}\to X\) (Definition 3.2.23) is a purely inseparable morphism.

Solution

Which morphism. Recall (Def. 3.2.23): \(F_S\) is the absolute Frobenius of \(S\), \(X^{(p)}=X\times_{S,F_S}S\) with projections \(\phi:X^{(p)}\to X\), \(\pi:X^{(p)}\to S\), \(q:X\to S\) the structure morphism, and the relative Frobenius is the morphism \(F_{X/S}:X\to X^{(p)}\) determined by \(\phi\circ F_{X/S}=F_X\) (absolute Frobenius of \(X\)) and \(\pi\circ F_{X/S}=q\) (it exists since \(q\circ F_X=F_S\circ q\)). So the relative Frobenius goes \(X\to X^{(p)}\) and not \(X^{(p)}\to X\) as printed. We prove that \(F_{X/S}\) is purely inseparable, and also (Remark at the end) the projection \(\phi:X^{(p)}\to X\). Finite type is not needed.

Recall that \(F_X\) is the identity on the underlying space and induces \(a\mapsto a^p\) on \(\mathcal O_X\); hence on residue fields it induces the map \(k(x)\to k(x)\), \(a\mapsto a^p\).

Injectivity. If \(F_{X/S}(x_1)=F_{X/S}(x_2)\), then applying \(\phi\) gives \(F_X(x_1)=F_X(x_2)\), i.e. \(x_1=x_2\).

Residue fields. Let \(x\in X\) and \(x'=F_{X/S}(x)\); then \(\phi(x')=F_X(x)=x\). The identity \(\phi\circ F_{X/S}=F_X\) gives on residue fields that the composite

\[ k(x)\xrightarrow{\ \phi^\#\ }k(x')\xrightarrow{\ F_{X/S}^\#\ }k(x) \]

is \(a\mapsto a^p\). So for \(\beta\in k(x)\) we have \(\beta^p=F^\#_{X/S}(\phi^\#(\beta))\in F^\#_{X/S}(k(x'))\). Thus every element of \(k(x)\) has its \(p\)-th power in the subfield \(F^\#_{X/S}(k(x'))\), i.e. \(k(x')\to k(x)\) is an algebraic purely inseparable extension. Hence \(F_{X/S}\) is purely inseparable.

Remark. The absolute Frobenius \(F_S\) is purely inseparable by the same reasoning (identity on points, \(a\mapsto a^p\) on residue fields), hence universally injective by Ex. 3.8; this property is stable under base change, so \(\phi\), the base change of \(F_S\) along \(q\), is universally injective, hence purely inseparable by Ex. 3.8 again. ∎

Exercise 3.7

Problem

Let \(k\) be an algebraically closed field. Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a finite surjective morphism of integral algebraic varieties over \(k\).

  1. (a)

    Let us suppose that \(K(X)/K(Y)\) is separable of degree \(n\). Show that there exists a non-empty open subset \(V\) of \(Y\) such that \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\) decomposes into a finite birational morphism \(f^{-1}(V)\to W\) followed by a finite etale morphism \(W\to V\). Show that for any \(y\in V(k)\), \(f^{-1}(y)\) consists of at least \(n\) points.

  2. (b)

    Use (a) to show that if \(f\) induces an injective map \(X(k)\to Y(k)\), then it is purely inseparable.

Solution

Write \(K=K(Y)\), \(L=K(X)\). We first prove a lemma which contains (a) and which also serves for (b).

Lemma. Let \(K\subseteq M\subseteq L\) be an intermediate field with \(M/K\) separable of degree \(m\). Then there are a non-empty affine open \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A'\subseteq Y\) with \(f^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}B'\), a domain \(C\) which is a free \(A'\)-module of rank \(m\) with \(\operatorname{Frac}C=M\) and \(\operatorname{Spec}C\to V\) finite etale, and an injective \(A'\)-algebra map \(C\to B'\) making \(B'\) a finite \(C\)-module, such that for every \(y\in V(k)\) the fibre \(f^{-1}(y)\) contains at least \(m\) points.

Proof. Take a non-empty affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\), with \(f^{-1}(\operatorname{Spec}A)=\operatorname{Spec}B\) (\(f\) is finite, hence affine); \(B\) is a domain, finite over the domain \(A\), and \(\operatorname{Frac}A=K\), \(\operatorname{Frac}B=L\). Then \(L=B\otimes_AK\), so every element of \(L\) has the form \(b/a\) with \(b\in B\), \(0\ne a\in A\). Let \(\theta_0\) be a primitive element of the finite separable extension \(M/K\), and choose \(0\ne a\in A\) with \(\theta:=a\theta_0\in B\); still \(M=K(\theta)\). Let \(P\in K[T]\) be the minimal polynomial of \(\theta\): monic of degree \(m\), separable, so its discriminant \(\operatorname{disc}(P)\in K^\times\). Choose \(0\ne a_1\in A\) such that the coefficients of \(P\) and \(\operatorname{disc}(P)^{-1}\) lie in \(A':=A_{a_1}\). Then \(P\in A'[T]\) is monic with \(\operatorname{disc}(P)\in A'^\times\), so \(C:=A'[T]/(P)\) is a free \(A'\)-module of rank \(m\) and etale over \(A'\). Put \(V=D(a_1)\) and \(B'=B_{a_1}\).

Since \(P(\theta)=0\) in \(L\), the map \(T\mapsto\theta\) defines an \(A'\)-algebra map \(C\to B'\). Since \(C\) is free over \(A'\), \(C\hookrightarrow C\otimes_{A'}K=K[T]/(P)\simeq M\) (as \(P\) is irreducible), so \(C\) is a domain with fraction field \(M\), and the composite \(C\to B'\subseteq L\) is this inclusion, so \(C\to B'\) is injective. \(B'\) is finite over \(A'\), hence over \(C\), and \(C\subseteq B'\) are domains, so \(\operatorname{Spec}B'\to\operatorname{Spec}C\) is surjective (lying over). Let \(y\in V(k)\), corresponding to a maximal ideal \(\mathfrak n\) of \(A'\) with \(A'/\mathfrak n=k\). The fibre of \(\operatorname{Spec}C\) over \(y\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}k[T]/(\bar P)\) with \(\bar P\) monic of degree \(m\) and non-zero discriminant, so it has \(m\) distinct points; each is the image of a point of \(\operatorname{Spec}B'\) lying over \(y\), and distinct points of \(\operatorname{Spec}C\) have distinct preimages. So \(f^{-1}(y)\) has at least \(m\) points. ∎

(a) Apply the lemma with \(M=L\), \(m=n\). Put \(W=\operatorname{Spec}C\). Then \(f^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}B'\to W\) is finite, surjective, and birational since \(\operatorname{Frac}B'=L=M=\operatorname{Frac}C\); and \(W\to V\) is finite etale. So \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\) is the composition of these two, and each \(f^{-1}(y)\), \(y\in V(k)\), has at least \(n\) points.

(b) Suppose \(X(k)\to Y(k)\) is injective.

Step 1: if \(f\) is as in the statement, then \(L/K\) is purely inseparable. The extension \(L/K\) is finite. Let \(K_s\) be the separable closure of \(K\) in \(L\) and \(n_s=[K_s:K]\); then \(L/K_s\) is purely inseparable. Apply the lemma with \(M=K_s\): for \(y\in V(k)\) (a non-empty open subset of a variety over an algebraically closed field has a \(k\)-point) the fibre \(f^{-1}(y)\) has at least \(n_s\) points, and these points are closed points over a closed point, i.e. elements of \(X(k)\). By injectivity, \(n_s\le1\). So \(K_s=K\) and \(L/K\) is purely inseparable.

Step 2: residue fields. Let \(x\in X\), \(T=\overline{\{x\}}\) and \(T'=f(T)=\overline{\{f(x)\}}\) (closed as \(f\) is finite), with reduced structures. Then \(T\to T'\) is a finite surjective morphism of integral varieties, \(T(k)\subseteq X(k)\), \(T'(k)\subseteq Y(k)\), and \(T(k)\to T'(k)\) is injective. By Step 1, \(K(T)/K(T')\), that is \(k(x)/k(f(x))\), is purely inseparable.

Step 3: injectivity on all points. Let \(x_1,x_2\in X\) with \(f(x_1)=f(x_2)=y\), \(T_i=\overline{\{x_i\}}\), \(T'=\overline{\{y\}}=f(T_1)=f(T_2)\). Let \(y_0\) be any closed point of \(T'\). Since \(T_i\to T'\) is surjective, \(T_i\) contains a closed point over \(y_0\); since \(f\) is injective on \(k\)-points, the closed points of \(T_1\) over \(y_0\) and the closed points of \(T_2\) over \(y_0\) are the same unique point. Every closed point of \(T_i\) lies over a closed point of \(T'\), so \(T_1\) and \(T_2\) have the same closed points. In a scheme of finite type over a field a closed subset is the closure of the set of its closed points, so \(T_1=T_2\), whence \(x_1=x_2\) (generic points).

By Steps 2 and 3, \(f\) is injective with purely inseparable residue extensions, i.e. purely inseparable. ∎

Exercise 3.8

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a morphism of schemes. Show that the following properties are equivalent:

  1. (i)

    \(f\) is purely inseparable.

  2. (ii)

    For every \(Y\)-scheme \(Z\), the projection morphism \(X\times_YZ\to Z\) is injective (in other words, \(f\) is universally injective).

  3. (iii)

    For every field \(K\), the canonical map \(X(K)\to Y(K)\) is injective.

Solution

Field lemma. For a field extension \(E/F\) the following are equivalent: (1) \(E/F\) is algebraic and purely inseparable (if \(\operatorname{char}F=p>0\): every \(e\in E\) has \(e^{p^n}\in F\) for some \(n\); if \(\operatorname{char}F=0\): \(E=F\)); (2) for every algebraically closed field \(\Omega\supseteq F\), there is at most one \(F\)-embedding \(E\to\Omega\) (we then say “at most one \(F\)-point of \(E\) in \(\Omega\)”). Proof. (1)\(\Rightarrow\)(2): in characteristic \(0\), \(E=F\). In characteristic \(p\), if \(\sigma\) is an \(F\)-embedding and \(e\in E\) with \(e^{p^n}=a\in F\), then \(\sigma(e)^{p^n}=a\), and \(T^{p^n}-a=(T-\alpha)^{p^n}\) has a unique root in \(\Omega\); so \(\sigma(e)\) is determined. (2)\(\Rightarrow\)(1): if some \(e\in E\) is transcendental over \(F\), choose a transcendence basis \(B\ni e\) of \(E/F\); the \(F\)-automorphism of \(F(B)\) with \(e\mapsto e+1\) fixing the other elements of \(B\) extends to an embedding \(E\to\overline E\) (as \(E/F(B)\) is algebraic and \(\overline E\) is algebraically closed), distinct from the inclusion. If all elements are algebraic but some \(e\) has an irreducible polynomial with at least two distinct roots in \(\Omega=\overline E\) (this happens exactly when \(e\) is not purely inseparable over \(F\)), we get two embeddings of \(F(e)\), which extend to \(E\) (\(E/F(e)\) algebraic). So (2) fails in both cases. ∎

(ii)\(\Rightarrow\)(iii). Let \(a,b:\operatorname{Spec}K\to X\) with \(f\circ a=f\circ b=:c\). Regard \(Z=\operatorname{Spec}K\) as a \(Y\)-scheme through \(c\). The pairs \((a,\operatorname{id})\) and \((b,\operatorname{id})\) define two sections \(\sigma_a,\sigma_b:\operatorname{Spec}K\to X_Z=X\times_YZ\) of the projection \(\pi:X_Z\to Z=\operatorname{Spec}K\). The one-point space \(Z\) has one point, so \(\pi\) injective implies that \(\sigma_a\) and \(\sigma_b\) have the same image point \(w\). A section with image \(w\) corresponds to a ring homomorphism \(\sigma^\#:k(w)\to K\) with \(\sigma^\#\circ\pi^\#=\operatorname{id}_K\), where \(\pi^\#:K\to k(w)\). Then \(\sigma^\#\) is injective (field homomorphism) and surjective (\(\sigma^\#\pi^\#(K)=K\)), so \(\sigma^\#=(\pi^\#)^{-1}\) does not depend on the section. A morphism from \(\operatorname{Spec}K\) is determined by its image point and the map on residue fields, so \(\sigma_a=\sigma_b\) and \(a=\operatorname{pr}_1\sigma_a=\operatorname{pr}_1\sigma_b=b\).

(iii)\(\Rightarrow\)(i). Injectivity. Let \(x_1,x_2\in X\) with \(f(x_1)=f(x_2)=y\). The ring \(k(x_1)\otimes_{k(y)}k(x_2)\) is non-zero; let \(K\) be its quotient by a maximal ideal. We get \(a_i:\operatorname{Spec}K\to\operatorname{Spec}k(x_i)\to X\) with \(f\circ a_1=f\circ a_2\) (both factor through \(\operatorname{Spec}k(y)\to Y\) and the two maps \(k(y)\to K\) agree by construction). By (iii), \(a_1=a_2\), so \(x_1=x_2\). Residue fields. Let \(x\in X\), \(y=f(x)\), \(F=k(y)\), \(E=k(x)\), and let \(\Omega\) be an algebraically closed field containing \(F\) and let \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2:E\to\Omega\) be two \(F\)-embeddings. They define two morphisms \(\operatorname{Spec}\Omega\to\operatorname{Spec}k(x)\to X\) with the same composition with \(f\) (both factor through \(\operatorname{Spec}F\to Y\) with the inclusion \(F\subseteq\Omega\)). By (iii) they are equal, so \(\sigma_1=\sigma_2\). By the field lemma \(E/F\) is purely inseparable.

(i)\(\Rightarrow\)(ii). Let \(Z\to Y\) be a morphism and \(z\in Z\) a point with image \(y\in Y\). The fibre of \(X_Z\to Z\) over \(z\) is \(X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}k(z)=X_y\times_{k(y)}\operatorname{Spec}k(z)\), whose points are the points of \(X_Z\) above \(z\). By (i), \(f^{-1}(y)\) is empty or a single point \(x\). If it is empty there is nothing to prove. Otherwise the underlying space of \(X_y\) is the single point \(x\), so \(X_y=\operatorname{Spec}D\) with \(D\) a local ring whose maximal ideal \(\mathfrak m_D\) is the nilradical (the only prime) and \(D/\mathfrak m_D=k(x)\). Then \(X_y\times_{k(y)}\operatorname{Spec}k(z)=\operatorname{Spec}(D\otimes_{k(y)}k(z))\), in which the ideal generated by \(\mathfrak m_D\) is nil; so its spectrum is that of \(G:=k(x)\otimes_{k(y)}k(z)\). We claim \(G\) has exactly one prime ideal. It is non-zero. If \(\operatorname{char}k(y)=0\), then \(k(x)=k(y)\) and \(G=k(z)\). If \(\operatorname{char}k(y)=p>0\): every element \(u=\sum e_i\otimes g_i\) of \(G\) satisfies \(u^{p^n}=\sum e_i^{p^n}\otimes g_i^{p^n}\in1\otimes k(z)\) for \(n\) large (the \(e_i^{p^n}\) lie in \(k(y)\)). If \(\mathfrak P\) is a prime of \(G\) and \(u\in\mathfrak P\), then \(u^{p^n}\in\mathfrak P\cap(1\otimes k(z))=0\) (a proper ideal of the field \(1\otimes k(z)\)), so \(u\) is nilpotent. Hence every prime consists of nilpotents, i.e. equals the nilradical: \(G\) has exactly one prime. So each fibre of \(X_Z\to Z\) has at most one point: the map is injective. ∎

Exercise 3.9

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a normal, locally Noetherian, integral scheme, and let \(f:X\to Y\) be a projective morphism with \(X\) integral.

  1. (a)

    Let us suppose \(f\) is finite. Show that \(f\) is purely inseparable if and only if the extension \(K(Y)\to K(X)\) is purely inseparable. Moreover, if \(f\) is purely inseparable, it will be a homeomorphism.

  2. (b)

    Let us suppose that the algebraic closure of \(K(Y)\) in \(K(X)\) is purely inseparable over \(K(Y)\). Show that the fibers of \(X\to Y\) are geometrically connected.

Solution

In both parts \(f\) is assumed dominant, as is implicit in the statement (the map \(K(Y)\to K(X)\) must exist). A dominant projective (hence closed) morphism is surjective. We write \(K=K(Y)\), \(L=K(X)\).

Lemma. Let \(A\) be a normal domain with fraction field \(K\), and \(B\) a domain containing \(A\) and integral over \(A\), with \(L'=\operatorname{Frac}B\) purely inseparable over \(K\) (so \(L'=K\) if \(\operatorname{char}K=0\)). Then \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) is purely inseparable; if \(B\) is finite over \(A\), it is a homeomorphism. Proof. Let \(b\in B\). There is \(n\ge0\) with \(b^{p^n}\in K\) (take \(p^n=1\) in characteristic \(0\)). But \(b^{p^n}\) is integral over \(A\) and \(A\) is integrally closed, so \(b^{p^n}\in A\). Let \(\mathfrak q_1,\mathfrak q_2\) be primes of \(B\) with \(\mathfrak q_1\cap A=\mathfrak q_2\cap A=\mathfrak p\). For \(b\in\mathfrak q_1\), \(b^{p^n}\in\mathfrak q_1\cap A=\mathfrak p\subseteq\mathfrak q_2\), so \(b\in\mathfrak q_2\). Thus \(\mathfrak q_1\subseteq\mathfrak q_2\) and by symmetry they are equal: \(\operatorname{Spec}B\to\operatorname{Spec}A\) is injective. For the residue fields, \(k(\mathfrak q)=\operatorname{Frac}(B/\mathfrak q)\), and an element \(\bar u/\bar v\) (\(u,v\in B\)) satisfies \((\bar u/\bar v)^{p^n}=\overline{u^{p^n}}/\overline{v^{p^n}}\in k(\mathfrak p)\) for \(n\) large, since \(u^{p^n},v^{p^n}\in A\). So \(k(\mathfrak q)/k(\mathfrak p)\) is purely inseparable. If \(B\) is finite over \(A\), the map is closed (integral extension), surjective (lying over) and bijective; a continuous closed bijection is a homeomorphism. ∎

(a) If \(f\) is purely inseparable, the generic point \(\xi_X\) of \(X\) maps to the generic point \(\xi_Y\) (\(f\) is dominant), and the residue field extension there is \(K\to L\), which is purely inseparable. Conversely suppose \(L/K\) is purely inseparable. Being purely inseparable is local on \(Y\): for an affine open \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subseteq Y\) we have \(f^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}B\), with \(A\) a normal domain, \(B\) a domain finite over \(A\) and \(\operatorname{Frac}B=L\) (\(f^{-1}(V)\ne\emptyset\) as \(f\) is dominant). The Lemma shows \(f^{-1}(V)\to V\) is purely inseparable and a homeomorphism; these properties glue, so \(f\) is purely inseparable. If \(f\) is purely inseparable, it is injective, and surjective (dominant and closed), and closed (finite), hence a homeomorphism.

(b) By Cor. 3.5, \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is a coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-algebra, and \(g:Y'=\operatorname{Spec}f_*\mathcal O_X\to Y\) (Ex. 1.17) is finite. For an affine open \(V=\operatorname{Spec}A\) of \(Y\) we have \(g^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}B\) with \(B=\mathcal O_X(f^{-1}V)\), a domain (\(X\) integral) containing \(A\), finite over \(A\) and contained in \(L\). Every element of \(B\) is algebraic over \(K\), so it lies in the algebraic closure \(K'\) of \(K\) in \(L\), which is purely inseparable over \(K\) by hypothesis; so \(\operatorname{Frac}B\subseteq K'\) is purely inseparable over \(K\). By the Lemma, \(g\) is purely inseparable; this says that \(\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) is purely inseparable (Def. 3.13). As \(f\) is surjective, Ex. 3.10 (proved below) applies: for any connected \(Z\to Y\), the scheme \(X\times_YZ\) is connected. Let \(y\in Y\) and let \(\Omega\) be an algebraic closure of \(k(y)\), \(Z=\operatorname{Spec}\Omega\to Y\). Then \(Z\) is connected and \(X\times_YZ=X_y\times_{k(y)}\Omega\), so \(X_y\) is geometrically connected. ∎

Exercise 3.10

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a locally Noetherian scheme, and \(f:X\to Y\) be a projective morphism such that \(\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) is purely inseparable. Show that for any morphism \(Z\to Y\) with \(Z\) connected, the fibered product \(X\times_YZ\) is connected. Also show that \(f^{-1}(F)\) is connected for any connected subset \(F\) of \(Y\).

Solution

A needed hypothesis. The statement is false if \(f\) is not surjective: let \(Y=\mathbf A^1_k\) and \(X=\{0,1\}\hookrightarrow Y\) the closed immersion of two rational points (finite, hence projective). Then \(\operatorname{Spec}f_*\mathcal O_X\to Y\) is this immersion, which is purely inseparable (injective, trivial residue extensions), but \(X\times_YY=X\) is not connected. So we assume \(f\) surjective. (Here a space is connected if it is not the union of two disjoint non-empty open subsets; surjectivity makes everything non-empty when \(Z\ne\emptyset\).)

Step 0 (Stein factorization; used also in Ex. 3.11 and 3.12). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be projective, \(Y\) locally Noetherian. By Cor. 3.5, \(\mathcal A=f_*\mathcal O_X\) is a coherent \(\mathcal O_Y\)-algebra; let \(h:Y'=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal A\to Y\) (Ex. 1.17), a finite morphism with \(h_*\mathcal O_{Y'}=\mathcal A\). Over an affine open \(V\subseteq Y\), a morphism \(f^{-1}(V)\to h^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal A(V)\) over \(V\) is the same as an algebra homomorphism \(\mathcal A(V)\to\mathcal O_X(f^{-1}V)\); taking the identity we obtain (compatibly) a morphism \(g:X\to Y'\) with \(f=h\circ g\). Then: (S1) \(\mathcal O_{Y'}\to g_*\mathcal O_X\) is an isomorphism: for \(V\) affine, \(W=h^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_X(f^{-1}V)\) is affine, \(g^{-1}(W)=f^{-1}(V)\), and \(\mathcal O_{Y'}(W)=\mathcal O_X(f^{-1}V)=(g_*\mathcal O_X)(W)\). Both sheaves are quasi-coherent on \(W\), so they agree. (S2) \(g\) is projective: choose a closed immersion \(i:X\to\mathbf P^n_Y\) over \(Y\). The graph \((\operatorname{id},g):X\to X\times_YY'\) is a closed immersion, being a base change of the diagonal of the separated morphism \(h\); and \(i\times\operatorname{id}:X\times_YY'\to\mathbf P^n_Y\times_YY'=\mathbf P^n_{Y'}\) is a closed immersion. The composition is a closed immersion \(X\to\mathbf P^n_{Y'}\) over \(Y'\). (S3) \(g\) is surjective: \(g\) is proper, so \(g(X)\) is closed, and \(\operatorname{Supp}g_*\mathcal O_X\subseteq g(X)\) (if \(y'\notin g(X)\) then \(g_*\mathcal O_X\) vanishes near \(y'\)); by (S1) \(\operatorname{Supp}g_*\mathcal O_X=\operatorname{Supp}\mathcal O_{Y'}=Y'\). (S4) For every \(y'\in Y'\) and every field extension \(E\supseteq k(y')\), the scheme \(X_{y'}\otimes_{k(y')}E\) is non-empty and connected. Non-emptiness is (S3). By (S1), (S2) and Thm. 3.15 with Cor. 3.17 applied to \(g\) (\(Y'\) is locally Noetherian since \(h\) is finite), \(X_\kappa:=X_{y'}\otimes_{k(y')}\kappa\) is connected, \(\kappa=\overline{k(y')}\). It is projective over \(\kappa\), so \(\Gamma=H^0(X_\kappa,\mathcal O)\) is a finite-dimensional \(\kappa\)-algebra (Thm. 3.2(a)) without idempotents other than \(0,1\) (Ex. 2.4.6), hence a local Artinian ring with residue field \(\kappa\). For an algebraically closed \(\Omega\supseteq\kappa\), \(H^0(X_\Omega,\mathcal O)=\Gamma\otimes_\kappa\Omega\) (Cor. 2.27) is local (its nilpotent maximal ideal is \(\mathfrak m_\Gamma\otimes\Omega\), with quotient \(\Omega\)), so again has no non-trivial idempotents, and \(X_\Omega\) is connected (Ex. 2.4.6). Given \(E\), take \(\Omega\) an algebraic closure of \(E\); it contains a copy of \(\kappa\), and \(X_\Omega\to X_{y'}\otimes E\) is a surjective continuous map, so \(X_{y'}\otimes E\) is connected.

A topological lemma (TL). Let \(q:T\to W\) be a closed, surjective, continuous map with connected non-empty fibres, \(W\) connected. Then \(T\) is connected. Indeed, if \(T=T_1\sqcup T_2\) with \(T_i\) open and closed, each fibre lies in \(T_1\) or \(T_2\); so \(W=q(T_1)\sqcup q(T_2)\) with both closed and disjoint; \(W\) connected forces one of them to be empty, so one \(T_i\) is empty.

Solution. Let \(f\) be surjective with \(h:Y'\to Y\) purely inseparable, in the notation of Step 0; \(h\) is finite and surjective (\(f=h\circ g\)), and universally injective by Ex. 3.8(ii). Let \(Z\to Y\) be a morphism, \(Z\) connected, and \(Y'_Z=Y'\times_YZ\). Then \(h_Z:Y'_Z\to Z\) is closed (finite), injective (universal injectivity), and surjective (base change of a surjection), hence a homeomorphism, so \(Y'_Z\) is connected. Now \(X_Z=X\times_YZ=X\times_{Y'}Y'_Z\) and the projection \(g_Z:X_Z\to Y'_Z\) is the base change of \(g\) along \(Y'_Z\to Y'\). It is proper (hence closed) and surjective, and its fibre over \(w\in Y'_Z\) with image \(y'\in Y'\) is \(X_{y'}\otimes_{k(y')}k(w)\), which is non-empty and connected by (S4). By TL, \(X_Z\) is connected.

For the second assertion, let \(F\subseteq Y\) be a connected subset. The map \(f:X\to Y\) is closed and surjective; its fibres are connected: \(h\) is bijective (injective since purely inseparable, surjective), so \(f^{-1}(y)=g^{-1}(y')\) where \(y'\) is the unique point over \(y\), i.e. the topological space of \(X_{y'}\), which is connected by (S4) with \(E=k(y')\). The restriction \(f^{-1}(F)\to F\) is closed (for \(C\) closed in \(f^{-1}(F)\), \(C=C'\cap f^{-1}(F)\) with \(C'\) closed in \(X\), and \(f(C)=f(C')\cap F\)), surjective, with connected fibres. By TL, \(f^{-1}(F)\) is connected. ∎

Exercise 3.11

Problem

(Stein factorization). Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a projective morphism to a locally Noetherian scheme \(Y\).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(f\) factors into \(g:X\to Z\) and \(h:Z\to Y\) such that \(g\) has only geometrically connected fibres, and \(h\) is finite.

  2. (b)

    Show that we can factor \(f\) as in (a) with moreover \(\mathcal O_Z\simeq g_*\mathcal O_X\). Show that this condition implies that \(Z\) is unique up to isomorphism (Ex. 1.17).

Solution

(b) (and thereby (a)). Take \(Z=Y'=\operatorname{Spec}f_*\mathcal O_X\), \(h:Z\to Y\) and \(g:X\to Z\) as in Step 0 of Ex. 3.10. Then \(h\) is finite, \(f=h\circ g\) and \(\mathcal O_Z\simeq g_*\mathcal O_X\) (S1). Moreover \(g\) is projective (S2) and \(Z\) is locally Noetherian, so Thm. 3.15 and Cor. 3.17 applied to \(g\) show that every fibre \(X_z\), \(z\in Z\), is (non-empty and) geometrically connected. This gives (b) existence, and (a), because the factorization of (b) satisfies the requirements of (a).

Uniqueness. Let \(f=h_1\circ g_1\) with \(g_1:X\to Z_1\), \(h_1:Z_1\to Y\) finite and \(\mathcal O_{Z_1}\simeq g_{1*}\mathcal O_X\). Then \(h_{1*}\mathcal O_{Z_1}\simeq h_{1*}g_{1*}\mathcal O_X=f_*\mathcal O_X\) as \(\mathcal O_Y\)-algebras (the isomorphism being induced by \(g_1^\#\)). As \(h_1\) is affine, \(Z_1\simeq\operatorname{Spec}h_{1*}\mathcal O_{Z_1}\) over \(Y\) (Ex. 1.17(c)), so we get an isomorphism of \(Y\)-schemes \(\varphi:Z_1\to\operatorname{Spec}f_*\mathcal O_X=Z\). By the description of morphisms \(X\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal A\) as algebra homomorphisms \(\mathcal A\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) (Step 0), \(g_1\) corresponds to the isomorphism above, composed with which the identity of \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is the morphism \(g\); hence \(\varphi\circ g_1=g\). So \(Z\) is unique up to a unique isomorphism compatible with \(g\) and \(h\). ∎

Exercise 3.12

Problem

Let \(f:X\to Y\) be a projective morphism. Let us suppose that for every \(y\in Y\), \(X_y\) is geometrically connected (resp. geometrically integral). Show that \(\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) is purely inseparable (resp. an isomorphism).

Solution

Throughout, \(Y\) is locally Noetherian (as in the whole section) and “geometrically connected/integral” includes non-empty, so \(f\) is surjective. Let \(f=h\circ g\), \(Z=\operatorname{Spec}f_*\mathcal O_X\), be the factorization of Step 0 (Ex. 3.10). Recall \(h\) is finite, \(g\) is projective and surjective, \(\mathcal O_Z\simeq g_*\mathcal O_X\).

Geometrically connected fibres. Let \(y\in Y\), \(k=k(y)\) and \(\kappa=\bar k\). The fibre \(Z_y=h^{-1}(y)\) is \(\operatorname{Spec}C_y\) with \(C_y\) a finite \(k\)-algebra (as \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is coherent), so \(Z_y\) is a finite discrete set \(\{z_1,\dots,z_n\}\). We have \(X_y=X\times_ZZ_y\), so \(X_y\to Z_y\) is surjective (\(g\) is) and \(X_y=\bigsqcup_ig^{-1}(z_i)\) is a decomposition into \(n\) disjoint non-empty open and closed subsets. As \(X_y\) is connected (\(X_\kappa\to X_y\) is surjective and \(X_\kappa\) is connected), \(n=1\). So \(h\) is injective. Let \(z\) be the point of \(Z_y\). The map \(X_\kappa\to Z_y\otimes_k\kappa=\operatorname{Spec}(C_y\otimes_k\kappa)\) is surjective and \(X_\kappa\) is connected, so \(\operatorname{Spec}(C_y\otimes_k\kappa)\) is connected; it is a finite discrete set, hence a point. Its underlying space is that of \(\operatorname{Spec}(k(z)\otimes_k\kappa)\) (\(C_y\) is local Artinian with residue field \(k(z)\), finite over \(k\), and the kernel of \(C_y\otimes\kappa\to k(z)\otimes\kappa\) is nilpotent). The prime ideals of the Artinian ring \(k(z)\otimes_k\kappa\) correspond to the \(\kappa\)-points, i.e. to the \(k\)-embeddings \(k(z)\to\kappa\) (the residue field at each prime is finite over \(\kappa\), so equals \(\kappa\)). So there is exactly one \(k\)-embedding of \(k(z)\) in \(\kappa\), and by the field lemma of Ex. 3.8 \(k(z)/k\) is purely inseparable. Hence \(h\) is injective with purely inseparable residue extensions, i.e. purely inseparable: \(\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) is purely inseparable (Def. 3.13). This proves the first assertion, as printed.

Geometrically integral fibres. As printed, the second assertion is false for non-reduced \(Y\): let \(Y=\operatorname{Spec}k[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^2)\) and \(X=\operatorname{Spec}k\to Y\) the closed immersion (projective). The only fibre is \(\operatorname{Spec}k\), geometrically integral, but \(\mathcal O_Y=k[\varepsilon]\to f_*\mathcal O_X=k\) is not an isomorphism. We prove the assertion when \(Y\) is integral and \(f\) is flat; the case of a reduced \(Y\) with a non-flat \(f\) is not proved here.

For every \(y\in Y\) the scheme \(X_y\) is projective over \(k=k(y)\) and geometrically integral, so \(X_\kappa\) is integral and projective over \(\kappa=\bar k\), and \(H^0(X_\kappa,\mathcal O)\) is a finite-dimensional \(\kappa\)-algebra (Thm. 3.2(a)) which is a domain (it embeds in the function field), hence a field finite over \(\kappa\), i.e. \(=\kappa\). By flat base change (Cor. 2.27), \(H^0(X_y,\mathcal O_{X_y})\otimes_k\kappa=\kappa\), so \(\dim_{k(y)}H^0(X_y,\mathcal O_{X_y})=1\) for every \(y\in Y\). Now \(\mathcal F=\mathcal O_X\) is coherent and flat over \(Y\), and \(Y\) is integral; the function \(y\mapsto\dim H^0(X_y,\mathcal F_y)\) is constant, so by Rem. 3.21(b) (\(p=0\)) the sheaf \(f_*\mathcal O_X=R^0f_*\mathcal O_X\) is locally free and \(f_*\mathcal O_X\otimes k(y)\to H^0(X_y,\mathcal O_{X_y})\) is an isomorphism for every \(y\); so \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) is locally free of rank \(1\). The unit section \(\mathcal O_Y\to f_*\mathcal O_X\) induces on \(k(y)\)-fibres a map \(k(y)\to H^0(X_y,\mathcal O_{X_y})=k(y)\) sending \(1\) to \(1\ne0\) (\(X_y\ne\emptyset\)), which is an isomorphism. By Nakayama's lemma \(\mathcal O_{Y,y}\to(f_*\mathcal O_X)_y\) is surjective, hence an isomorphism (a surjection between free modules of rank \(1\) over a local ring is an isomorphism). Thus \(\mathcal O_Y\simeq f_*\mathcal O_X\). ∎

Exercise 3.13

Problem

Let \(X\to S\) be a projective morphism to a locally Noetherian scheme. Let us suppose that the fibers of \(X\to S\) are of dimension \(\le d\).

  1. (a)

    Show that if \(\mathcal F\to\mathcal G\) is a surjective homomorphism of quasi-coherent sheaves on \(X\), then \(H^d(X,\mathcal F)\to H^d(X,\mathcal G)\) is surjective (use Prop. 2.34). Show that the canonical homomorphism

    \[ R^df_*\mathcal F\otimes_{\mathcal O_S}k(s)\to H^d(X_s,\mathcal F_s)\tag{3.16} \]

    is surjective for all \(s\in S\).

  2. (b)

    Let us suppose that \(S\) is a Dedekind scheme, and that \(\mathcal F\) is coherent and flat over \(S\). Show that (3.16) is bijective.

Solution

Let \(f:X\to S\) be the morphism. In the first assertion of (a), \(S\) is affine (for general \(S\) the statement is that \(R^df_*\mathcal F\to R^df_*\mathcal G\) is surjective, which is local on \(S\) by Prop. 2.28); then \(H^p(X,-)=R^pf_*(-)(S)\).

(a) Let \(\mathcal K=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal F\to\mathcal G)\); it is quasi-coherent. \(X\) is separated, so Cor. 2.22 gives an exact sequence \(H^d(X,\mathcal F)\to H^d(X,\mathcal G)\to H^{d+1}(X,\mathcal K)\), and \(H^{d+1}(X,\mathcal K)=0\) by Prop. 2.34 (the fibres have dimension \(\le d\), and \(f\) is quasi-projective). So \(H^d(X,\mathcal F)\to H^d(X,\mathcal G)\) is surjective.

Now let \(s\in S\); choose an affine open \(\operatorname{Spec}A\ni s\) and replace \(S\) by it; \(s\) corresponds to a prime \(\mathfrak p\) and \(k(s)=\kappa(\mathfrak p)\). The source of (3.16) is \(H^d(X,\mathcal F)\otimes_Ak(s)\), and the target is \(H^d(X_s,\mathcal F_s)=H^d(X,\mathcal F\otimes_Ak(s))\) (Ex. 2.3(b)(1): \(X_s\to X\) is affine and \(X\) is separated; \(\mathcal F\otimes_Ak(s)\) is the direct image of \(\mathcal F_s\)). The map (3.16) is the map \(\rho_M\) of Lemma 2.26 for \(M=k(s)\). This map is natural in \(M\), and it is bijective if \(M\) is free (if \(M=A^{(I)}\), both sides are \(H^d(X,\mathcal F)^{(I)}\): cohomology of the quasi-compact scheme \(X\) commutes with direct sums, as one sees on a finite affine covering). Choose a surjection \(A^{(I)}\to k(s)\); it induces a surjection of quasi-coherent sheaves \(\mathcal F\otimes A^{(I)}\to\mathcal F\otimes k(s)\), hence a surjection on \(H^d\) by the first part. By naturality of \(\rho\) in \(M\) we have a commutative square, in which the left vertical arrow is \(\operatorname{id}\otimes(A^{(I)}\to k(s))\), the right one is induced by the surjection of sheaves, and the horizontal arrows are \(\rho_{A^{(I)}}\) and \(\rho_{k(s)}\). Here the top arrow is an isomorphism, the right arrow is surjective, so \(\rho_{k(s)}\) is surjective. This is (3.16).

(b) The formation of \(R^df_*\) commutes with the flat base change \(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{S,s}\to S\) (Cor. 2.27) and \(X_s\), \(\mathcal F_s\) do not change; so we may assume \(S=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{S,s}\). The ring \(\mathcal O_{S,s}\) of a Dedekind scheme is a field (then \(s\) is the only point, \(X_s=X\), and (3.16) is the identity) or a discrete valuation ring with closed point \(s\) or generic point \(s\). If \(s\) is the generic point, \(k(s)=K\) is flat over \(\mathcal O_K\), so (3.16) is bijective by Lemma 2.26 and Ex. 2.3. Let \(s\) be the closed point. Then (3.16) is the homomorphism (3.14) of Thm. 3.20 for \(p=d\); by Thm. 3.20(d) it is an isomorphism if and only if \(H^{d+1}(X,\mathcal F)\) is free over \(\mathcal O_K\). But \(H^{d+1}(X,\mathcal F)=0\) by Prop. 2.34. So (3.16) is bijective. ∎

Exercise 3.14

Problem

Let \(Y\) be a locally Noetherian scheme, and let \(f:X\to Y\) be a flat projective morphism. We say that \(f\) is cohomologically flat (in dimension \(0\)) if the construction of \(f_*\mathcal O_X\) commutes with base change (Cor. 3.22(i)).

  1. (a)

    Show that \(f\) is cohomologically flat if and only if for every \(y\in Y\), the morphism \(X\times_Y\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{Y,y}\to\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{Y,y}\) is cohomologically flat.

  2. (b)
